# Arjun's projects

Personal site hosted with GitHub Pages at **https://ascoding11.github.io**, the main address: every page's canonical and share links, the sitemap and robots.txt use it. Cloudflare Pages also serves the same files from this repo's `main` branch at https://ascoding11.com (every merge goes live on both in a minute or two). `ads.txt` sits at the root of the repo, so both addresses serve it at `/ads.txt`. Google sign-in only works on addresses listed in Firebase (project `rumblekarts`) → Authentication → Settings → Authorized domains, so ascoding11.com needs to be listed there too. Browsers keep saved data (settings, guest accounts, drafts) per address, so the two addresses don't share it.

- `showcase/`: the landing page for ascoding11.com (a bare visit to that address, with nothing after it, is sent here by a small script at the top of `index.html`; ascoding11.github.io keeps the home page). One self-contained page: what the site is, a short tour (Docket and Prompted open with `#sample`), the Traffic panel, and the hard problems solved. Its "View the site" button goes to `/#new` (the home page with the Games card and feedback bar, see `index.html`). The Traffic numbers are pasted into the `trafficData` JSON tag in the page, in the format of the analytics dashboard's "Copy showcase numbers" button (anonymous totals only: no names, emails or visitor IDs); update them by pasting new numbers there. Counted in analytics as `showcase`.
- `index.html`: the home page, with links to the Web Development Projects, Productivity and Engineering Projects pages. Above the footer, an "Open the Back Room →" button (`#brOpen`, a `<button>` rather than a link, so it isn't a crawlable link to the hidden folder) opens the Back Room on the page without the code, the same way the code does (`reveal()`; on a phone, the phone version); the row (`.br-door`) hides once the Back Room is showing. The code (triple-click the © in the footer) still works too. A share link, the home page address followed by `#` and the Back Room's folder name (`ascoding11.com/#e63334287a3deaa3`), opens the Back Room there without the code. It's meant for the showcase page; the folder name still isn't written in the home page's source, and without the link the code is still needed. The showcase uses a second link, `ascoding11.com/#new`: it leaves the home page as it is and adds a fourth card, Games (the cards go two by two on wide screens), whose links open the whole Back Room there without the code, with its Party, Online (chat) and Leaderboard panels; this happens on phones too (`reveal(html, dir, true)`), because the phone version has no Online panel. It also loads the Back Room's "Tell us" feedback bar (`feedback.js`) at the top of the page straight away; its "Which game?" list starts with "The website, not a game" (`game: "site"`) for notes about the rest of the site. This link does write the Back Room's folder name into the home page
- `projects/`: the Web Development Projects page, with a full card for each game
- `productivity/`: the Productivity page, with a full card for each tool
- `engineering/`: the Engineering Projects page, with a full card for each engineering project (four workbenches and one build plan, Project Daedalus)
- `account/account.js`: optional Google sign-in, added to every page with the shared top bar. Uses the `rumblekarts` Firebase project (same as the analytics and the Back Room games). Signed-in visitors get a row in Firestore `members/{uid}` (name, email, photo, first/last visit, page count and the analytics visitor IDs of their browsers), which the analytics dashboard shows under Signed-in visitors. The Back Room games reuse a signed-in account instead of switching to an anonymous one.
- `site.css`: styles shared by the home, Web Development Projects and Productivity pages
- `theme.js`: the site's light/dark switch, with three choices: Auto (follow the device's light or dark mode, the default), Light and Dark. Loaded with a plain `<script src="/theme.js"></script>` in the `<head>`, just before the stylesheet (not deferred, so it runs before the page is drawn and there's no flash), by every page that uses `site.css` and nothing else of its own for colours (home, Web Development Projects, Productivity, Engineering Projects, Marketplace, About, Project Daedalus, the Docket, Prompted, Boxed and Hype Cycle Exchange guides), by Docket and Prompted, the Back Room page, the phone page and the showcase landing page (which has no top bar, so no switch; it follows the choice made on any other page). **The switch** is one small button showing the current choice (half circle, sun or moon) that opens a menu with the three; theme.js puts it at the end of the page's `.topbar` by itself (its menu opens leftwards, or rightwards when the top bar has wrapped it to the left edge, as on a phone held sideways), right after the Sign in button (so `account/` is unchanged), which makes it the same place on every page with the top bar, the Back Room included; any other empty element with `data-theme-switch` is filled in too (the phone page has one in its header, since it has no top bar). **Storage:** localStorage `theme` (`light` or `dark`; removed for Auto), so the choice carries to every page; pages already open in other tabs, and pages inside frames (the home page's phone-version frame), follow a change straight away through the `storage` event. Nothing is stored online. **How the CSS follows it:** an explicit choice puts `data-theme="light"` or `data-theme="dark"` on `<html>`, and Auto leaves it off. Each dark-mode block is written twice: inside `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` as `:root:not([data-theme="light"])` (Auto, or Dark on a dark device), and again as `:root[data-theme="dark"]` (Dark on a light device). That's how `site.css`, `games.css`, Docket and Prompted do it; the phone page is dark by itself, so it does the opposite with its light look. A new page that uses `site.css` only needs the script line; a page with its own dark colours needs both blocks too. The shared Back Room scripts that add their own styles (party.js, avatar.js, miles.js, leaderboard.js, news.js, social.js, account-nudge.js, events.js, feedback.js and the rest) take their colours from the page's variables, so they follow without changes. `SiteTheme.get()` / `.set('auto'|'light'|'dark')` / `.wire(el)`, and a `site-theme` event on `window` after a change (seasons.js redoes its seasonal tint on it). **Pages that don't follow it** keep their own look: every game (each has its own fixed look, light or dark), the Daily Puzzles page, the admin page, and the pages that follow the device with their own CSS only: Boxed, Hype Cycle Exchange, the analytics dashboard and the privacy policy.
- `offline.js`, `sw.js`, `offline.html`: offline mode. `offline.js` (a deferred `<script>` on the home page, the Back Room page and the phone page) registers `sw.js`, a service worker for the whole site. Every page, game and file of the site that a browser opens is kept in its Cache Storage (`site-v1`), so it opens again on that device with no internet; nothing is downloaded ahead of time except `/` and `offline.html` (shown for a page that isn't saved yet). The site's own files are fetched network-first, exactly as before, and the cached copy is used only when the network fails, so online players always get the newest version and nothing needs bumping when a game changes. Versioned libraries (cdnjs, jsDelivr, Firebase's SDK on gstatic) and Google Fonts files are served from the cache first. Never cached, always straight to the network: `/api/` (Marketplace, wallet, checkout), Firebase/Firestore traffic, Google sign-in, analytics and ads, so online play, leaderboards and purchases are never stale. On the first visit `offline.js` sends the worker the files that page already loaded, so the page works offline right away. While the device is offline, it shows a small "Offline" note at the bottom (top-level pages only) and fades the Back Room's game cards (`a.btn.primary` Play links) and the phone page's tiles (`.tile[data-path]`) for games not saved on that device, with a "Not saved offline" tag. A game's practice/bot modes then work as normal; its online modes just can't connect until the internet is back. Bump `VERSION` in `sw.js` only to wipe every device's cache. With the worker in place, the phone page (which has `m/manifest.webmanifest`) is also installable as an app.
- `.claude/skills/`: two skills that Claude Code picks up by itself when working in this repo (nothing on the website loads them). `game-graphics` is a method for making a game look better (look at screenshots, choose a direction, fix colour and light before adding effects, keep a cheaper version for slow machines), with `scripts/shots.js` for before-and-after screenshots at laptop and phone sizes. `game-performance` is a method for making a game faster without changing how it looks or plays (measure, fix the biggest cost, measure again), with `scripts/perf_probe.js`, which reports frame times, script time, draw calls, triangles, canvas calls, memory churn and page weight for any game here and compares two runs. Both scripts need Node and Playwright and are only for development. The games' own rules in `CLAUDE.md` and this file come first.
- `hype-cycle-exchange/`: Hype Cycle Exchange, a live classroom stock-market game (Firebase)
- `boxed/`: Boxed, a competition math game with real AMC 10 and AIME problems: a Daily five, Practice, and live multiplayer games (Firebase, same project and Firestore rules as Hype Cycle Exchange; games are stored in `games` with `kind: "math"`). The page holds only contest names, problem numbers, official answers and a topic tag per problem (Algebra, Geometry, Number Theory, Counting & Probability); the problem text is loaded from the AoPS Wiki API while playing (it asks `artofproblemsolving.com/wiki/api.php` and `wiki.artofproblemsolving.com/wiki/api.php` at once (6-second limit), stops asking for the rest of the visit if both are blocked, and if a contest page can't be read it loads that problem's own page; it reads both the older page layout and newer MediaWiki layouts that wrap sections in `<section>`; failures are logged to the console as `Boxed: AoPS Wiki load failed`). If the wiki doesn't answer, AIME problems (not AMC 10) load from public Hugging Face datasets through `datasets-server.huggingface.co` (`gneubig/aime-1983-2024` and `di-zhang-fdu/AIME_1983_2024` up to 2024, `AI-MO/aimo-validation-aime` for 2022–2024, `MathArena/aime_<year>_<I|II>`, `MathArena/aime_<year>` and `math-ai/aime<yy>` for 2025 on); a row is only used when its answer matches Boxed's key, the LaTeX is drawn with KaTeX (loaded from cdnjs only when needed), `[asy]` diagrams are replaced with a link to AoPS, and full results are cached in localStorage as `boxed.hf1.<contest>`. When a problem can't be shown, the sheet links to it on LIVE by Po-Shen Loh first (`liveURL`: `past-contests/amc10/<year><A|B>` or `aime/<year><I|II>`, with the 2021 Fall contests as `2021C`/`2021D`), then the AoPS Wiki; Boxed only links to LIVE and never loads its pages
- `e63334287a3deaa3/rumble-karts/`: Rumble Karts (Back Room), a 3D kart-battle game for up to 12 players (Three.js, Firebase Realtime Database rooms under `rooms/<code>` with no prefix, so any invite code without a two-letter game prefix opens it; same project and rules as the other Back Room games). There is no Quick match: the menu's room code (a random word-number by default, or an invite's) is the room. Rooms hold up to 12 karts (`MAX_ROOM`): a full code says "Room is full"; the plain code `lobby` spreads to `lobby-2` … `lobby-6`. Modes: Classic (most wrecks in 3 minutes), Gun Game (level up through all 14 weapons; the ladder is `GG_LADDER`) and Zone. Each player writes only their own node `rooms/<code>/players/<uid>` (`n, c, x, z, h, s, st, hp, sh, tb, sp, gv, fz, gh, tn, d, sc, sm, sr, ts`), 20 times a second while anything changes; other karts are drawn from a short history of those updates (`applySnapshot`/`updateRemote`, the same idea as `netsmooth.js`). There is no host: the round record `rooms/<code>/round` (`{id, ends, st, m, w}`) is written with transactions by whoever notices it has run out, and the round number picks the map, the special drops and the zone spots. Everything else goes through `rooms/<code>/events/<push>` (removed after 5 s): shots, throws and hits. The shooter's computer decides what a rocket, blaster, boomerang, buffalo or tornado hit and sends a `hit` event, but only the victim's own computer applies damage to its kart and reports the wreck with a `kill` event (which is what credits the attacker), so no computer can hurt a kart it doesn't own. Practice vs bots runs the same code offline with `Net.send` looping events straight back. Items come out of ? boxes by the weights in `ITEMS` (`w`, relative odds; `tier` sets the frame colour and tier 2–3 pickups are announced to the room); epic items drop on the map every 30 s and Arjun's Gandiva with 30 s left. Two named weapons: Nico's Cream Cannon (common, two shots per pickup: a blob of whipped cream lobbed in a short arc, projectile kind `cream`; a hit event carries `cr`, the seconds of slow-down; every computer paints goo on the kart named and the victim's own computer takes 15 damage, drops to 55% top speed for 3 s and splatters its screen for 2.5 s; the goo washes off over about four seconds) and The Angry Whip (legendary, weight 1, half as common as the Gandiva; one use). The whip is the only weapon with a two-step hit: `wh` is the wind-up everyone sees (0.4 s telegraph, the target glows red and gets a note; the target's computer counts the 0.4 s from when the wind-up reaches it, so lag can't shorten the warning), then the target's own computer decides whether the lasso caught it (alive, no spawn shield or Spikes shield, not a ghost, not already caught, still within reach of a living attacker) and answers `whh` (caught: every computer lifts that kart from the event's time stamp for 1.2 s with three cracks, then slams it down, and the target's computer wrecks its own kart with weapon `whip`, so the attacker gets the wreck like any other; if another computer hears of the wreck before its own lift has finished, it slams the kart down there and then) or `whm` (missed: the whip cracks at empty air). Bots use both with the same rules and will Warp or Ghost out of a wind-up if they happen to hold one. Kill-feed text for the two reads "got creamed by" and "got whipped by". Nothing is stored between sessions except the driver name, paint, last room, mode and sound setting in the browser; the Leaderboards get play time, wrecks and wins through `stats.js`. On touch screens it shows a stick and a Fire button; `window.__rumble` exposes the game's objects for debugging and tests. **How it is drawn** (looks only; none of it is sent to the room): each map's `look` has a `dome` entry that `buildSky` turns into two unlit meshes, a sky dome (a painted fade from deep sky to haze with clouds and the sun, which follows the camera) and a ring of far-off hills or mountains (`ridge`: colour, tallest, lowest, and 1 for jagged peaks); the fog colour is the dome's haze colour (`fogColor`) so the ground fades into the sky. The arena floor is one tiling texture of four panels, and the markings texture that covers the whole arena also carries a soft dark edge under every block, pillar, tower and barrier (tint `ao`), painted once when the map is built, so it is still there when real shadows are switched off on a slow computer. Karts and ? boxes have a flat soft shadow on the floor as well (`blobTex`: one mesh per kart, one instanced mesh for all boxes, `updateBoxBlobs`). The usual kart body is two meshes built once and shared by every kart (`kartParts`: fixed colours as vertex colours, and the driver's paint), and each wheel with its rim and hub is one shared mesh, so a kart is six meshes rather than about twenty; the paint is the only glossy material (`MeshPhongMaterial`), and the hit flash and whip glow still go through its `emissive`. An ordinary explosion (`explodeFX` with the default colours) also sends a ring along the floor and leaves a scorch mark for 5 s. On an upright phone the item box and armour bar sit at the bottom right so they clear the stick. **Turkey Week** (`../events.js`, November 15 to 21, 2026): **Turkey Week** (`../events.js`, November 15 to 26, 2026): every kart is drawn as a turkey on wheels (`turkeyParts`, `dressKart`): the usual body (`parts.shellG`) is hidden and a bird of two shared meshes is shown in its place, with every other tail feather in the driver's paint so karts are still told apart by colour. There is no button for it in the game: the one Turkey Week switch on the Back Room page (see `events.js` below) turns it off or on for that browser, and the game reads it once, when it loads. It is only a look: kart sizes, hits and everything sent to the room are unchanged. **Garage** (hidden; captains only): drivers, kart bodies, wheels, horns and wreck effects, all drawn in code in `garage.js` beside the page. There are 30 drivers (toy figures that sit in the seat: Sir Honksalot, Captain Crumb, Granny Vroom, Bolt, Wasabi, Professor Pickle, DJ Raccoon, Chef Meatball, Yeti Betty, Count Dracoola, Tiny Rex, Sergeant Sprinkles, Moolissa, Astro Hamster, Cactus Jack, Waddles, Turkey Tom, and from the second wave Striker, Stretch, Linebacker, Slugger, Ref Reggie, Champ, Rally Bear, Captain Patch, The Boss, Pemberton, Clockwork, Dusty, Agent Shades), 25 karts (bathtub, shopping cart, lawnmower, hot dog cart, bumper car, giant shoe, banana, rubber duck, couch, bed, toilet, pumpkin carriage, ice cream truck, rocket sled, cardboard box, rowboat, long sedan, soccer ball, basketball, grandstand, ice groomer, robo racer, tea trolley, football helmet, boxing ring), 8 wheels, 9 horns (goose honk, clown horn, air horn, kazoo, bike bell, stadium horn, ship's bell, ref whistle, rally organ; synthesised with Web Audio) and 8 wreck effects. Every driver and kart has parts in the driver's paint, so players are still told apart by colour. It is shown only while `garageOn()` is true: `RUMBLE_PUBLIC` (false; flip it to open the Garage to everyone), a captain signed in (`../captains.js`), or `?garage=1` on localhost or in an automated browser. For everyone else the page is as before: `garage.js` is not fetched, the menu, saved data and room node are unchanged. A captain gets a Garage button under the paint swatches (everything free to wear; nothing is bought), saved in this browser as `rk_cos`. What a kart wears is five numbers, driver, kart, wheels, wreck effect and horn (0 for the usual one), sent as one extra field `ck` in the player's own node (`'3.12.0.2.1'`, only when something is worn; `cosOf` checks it, and a number this page doesn't know draws the usual one; older pages ignore the field). Any page that sees a `ck` in the room fetches `garage.js`, so everyone sees a captain's kart. The numbers are the items' `no` in `../cosmetics.js` (game `rumble-karts`): add new ones at the end and never renumber. **Looks only, and no extra cost:** a dressed kart is still the usual six meshes; its body and driver are merged into the same two geometries (vertex colours and the paint), made once per kart-and-driver pair, and a wheel style is one shared geometry. Hit size (`KART_R`), handling and everything else are unchanged; the tallest pieces (bumper-car pole, hot-dog umbrella, roof cone) sit at the back so they don't block the driver's own view, and a dressed kart keeps its own body during Turkey Week. **Knockout taunts:** when a kart with a Garage driver wrecks someone, every computer plays that driver's 1.6 s taunt from the `kill` event it already gets (a move such as a flip, a spin or a bow, a speech bubble like "HONK!", and the kart's horn); only then is the driver drawn on its own (two more meshes) so it can move. A wreck effect replaces the usual debris of a wrecked kart that wears one (the flash, floor ring and scorch mark stay). A captain with a horn honks with H (unless H is bound to something) or the Honk button on a touch screen; that sends an `hn` event (once per 1.2 s; older pages ignore it). In practice the bots wear random Garage looks while the Garage is open to you. `window.__rumble.GARAGE.shot(cos, view)` draws any combination for contact sheets. It has Spanish (`rumble-karts/es.js`, through `../lang.js`; EN | ES switch in the menu): menus, modes, weapons and their tips, banners, the feed, results and the Garage; the banner and objective pointers are translated in the page (`data-noi18n`), and bot names, map names, weapon names that are invented, drivers' taunts and the brand-like mode name Gun Game stay as they are.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/shell-crackers/`: Shell Crackers (Back Room), a first-person egg shooter with a free-for-all and a capture-the-flag mode (Three.js, Firebase Realtime Database rooms under `rooms/sc-…`, same project and rules as Rumble Karts). Maps are block worlds (72 × 72 m; blocks are 1 m wide and 0.5 m tall, so stairs are rows of half-metre steps) built by the functions in `MAPS` (`buildFarm`, `buildFactory`, `buildTown`, `buildFreezer`, `buildSands`, `buildHeights`) from the tools above them: `fill`/`clear`, `building` (walls, floors, windows, doors, flat or gabled roofs), `stairs`, `stairsDown`, `tunnel`, and small props. The ground is 3 m thick so tunnels fit under it, and every call is also placed turned 180° about the centre so both halves match. There are six maps: Farmstead, Yolk Works and Eggsburg, and three newer ones, each with its own idea. **Deep Freeze** is the tight indoor one: a cold store with a loading dock at each end, three lanes between them (corridor, freezer hall, corridor), a catwalk cross over the hall and a control room above each corridor; the snowy yard round it is scenery nobody can reach, and it has no small arena because it is already small. **Sunny Side Sands** is the open one: a fort at each end of a straight road, a 2 m deep dry wash across the middle (a plank bridge you can walk under, four ramps each side), a rock mesa on each side, ruins and an oasis. **Hardboil Heights** is the one with height: a three-tier hill (1.5, 3 and 4.5 m) with stairs on every face, two ground-level tunnels through it that cross in a chamber under the summit (a pillar stops a shot straight through), a hut at each end and two watchtowers. Their blocks (`SAND`, `SNOW`, `ICE`, `PANEL` and so on) are tints of textures the older maps already use, so they add no draw calls; they are cheaper to draw than the older maps (about 9,000 to 12,000 triangles of blocks against 11,500 to 22,500, and the same 45 to 60 draw calls in a bots match). A map can ask for more fill light with `hemiI`/`ambI` in its `MAPS` entry (Deep Freeze does, being indoors). Practice and the menu pick a map at random from all six; a room's first match is random and each next match takes the next map in turn (below for rooms with an older copy of the game in them). Bots path over a navigation graph of every spot an egg can stand; a bot that stops making progress hops, drops its path and plans again (it used to keep its old path, so one knocked off a ramp walked at the wall under its next waypoint). The Training range (menu button under Practice) is its own map, `buildRange` with `RANGE_MAP`, kept out of `MAPS` so it never comes up in practice or online rotation: bullseye boards at 10/20/30/45/55 m, balloons that pop and come back, and unarmed dummies (`DUMMY_ROUTES`) that strafe or patrol and respawn after being cracked. Every gun is on keys 1–5 (6 is the pistol) and T resets the stats; the number keys stay fixed there, while T is the rebindable "Reset training stats" action in the Controls panel (`keybinds.js`) and the Pistol and Swap keys work on the range too. Magazines still need reloading but never run out, yolk bombs refill, and it has no clock, no rating and no database writes; its hit, accuracy and headshot counts live only in the open tab (`Range` in the page). On touch screens it adds a button for each gun and a Reset button (`#tTrain`) to the usual touch controls. Ranked rating: Elo from each match's finishing order, multiplied by 1.25^(real opponents − 1) (×1 for one real opponent, capped at ×3; bots-only practice ×0.4; first 10 matches move faster; leaving an online match after 45 s counts as last place). Ratings are stored at `rooms/sc-ratings/players/<uid>`, which the existing rules allow; each player's own browser writes their rating, so it isn't cheat-proof. Online, each egg writes its own `players/<uid>` node about 12 times a second while anything changed (position, aim, health, shots), twice more right after it stops changing (`Net.wantSend`, so others see it stop at once instead of carrying it on into the wall or floor it stopped at) and once a second otherwise; other eggs are dropped after 9 s without an update. The match lives in `rooms/sc-<code>/round` (set by a transaction on joining, replaced when it is 20 s past its end); the last egg to leave the room removes it (`Net.dropRound`, only when no other egg is in the room and only the match it played), so an unused code doesn't keep a stale node, and `rematch` starts a fresh match if there is none. The round is the only thing that decides the map: its `map`, and `sm` for a small arena (below). Every browser keeps the newest value its listener has had from the server (`Net.cur`) and builds exactly that (`Net.takeRound`, `onMatch`); whoever wrote the round and whoever has left since doesn't matter. When a party lands together, every browser races to start the round with its own random map; the join and rematch transactions run with `applyLocally` off so nobody acts on a losing guess, and the join never acts on its own transaction result, only on what the listener delivers. Joining waits up to 8 s for the round; if it still hasn't come, the menu says "Waiting for the room's match…" (the old code dropped you onto the menu's random map, where everyone else was invisible), tries to start the round again after 12 s more and gives up with a message after 15 s beyond that. Each egg's node also carries the map and arena it has built (`mp`, `ar`), and another egg is only drawn, hittable or heard while its node carries the same match id (`m`) and, when it says, the same `mp`/`ar` as yours, so eggs on different rounds or layouts never see or hit each other. With every send (`Net.check`) the browser rebuilds at once if the map it has built isn't the round's, and if most of the other eggs in the match report a different layout for 2 s it reads the round again. If the last egg out removes the round while someone it hadn't seen yet is still in the room, that egg puts the round back (`takeRound`), so a later joiner doesn't start a new one on another map. Small arenas: an online match that starts with 4 or fewer eggs (`SMALL_MAX`, counted by whoever starts the round: on joining a room or in `rematch`) has `sm: 1`, and every browser builds that map with its far wings closed off (`smallArena`, from the map's `small` entry in `MAPS`: the box that stays open, a wall of the map's own fence round it from the tunnels up, and its own spawns nearer the middle). Outside the box every open block is `BARRIER`, which eggs can't walk into but which is never drawn and lets shots and sight lines through, so nobody jumps over the wall from a roof; buildings in the wings stay as scenery. If the room reaches 5 eggs, or an egg on an older copy of the game (no `ar` in its node) joins, in the first 15 s of a small-arena match (`SMALL_GROW_MS`), the round is switched to the full map (a transaction that only clears `sm`), so a party arriving one by one isn't stuck in a small arena; after that the size only changes at the next match. A small arena is only chosen when every egg in the room sends `ar`. Practice, the training range and rooms of 5 or more always get the whole map, built exactly as before. Maps and older copies of the game: each egg's node also says how many maps it can build (`mc`); a copy from before the three newer maps says nothing and counts as 3 (`OLD_MAPS`, `mapsOf`). A new match (joining an empty room, `rematch`) only picks among the maps every egg in the room can build, so a room with an older copy in it stays on the first three. If an older copy joins in the first 15 s of a match on a newer map, the match is moved to one of the first three (a transaction that only changes `map`, like the small arena opening up); after 15 s the match stays where it is and the older copy, which builds Eggsburg for any map number it doesn't know, is hidden from the others and they from it (the `mp` rule above) until the next match. Nothing in the round node changed. After changing a map's `small` box or spawns, check that the console shows no `spawn moved` or `spawn blocked` warning for its small arena too (`buildMap(i,1)`). Frame cost: hills and trees are merged into four meshes (one per quarter round the map, so the ones behind you aren't drawn), each cloud into one, each egg's face into two and each gun into one geometry shared by every egg and the view model (`mergeParts`, colours as vertex colours), and particles and shell shards are instanced pools (`instPool`), so a busy practice match is about 60 draw calls instead of about 220. A slow frame is played in steps of at most 1/30 s (`simulate`; only frames longer than `FRAME_CAP`, 0.25 s, lose time), so a slow laptop no longer plays in slow motion below 20 frames a second. If frames stay under about 30 a second for 8 s of play even at `autores.js`'s lowest resolution, and the player has never picked a graphics setting, the game switches itself to Fast (no shadows), says so in the feed and saves that; Pretty in Settings puts it back. Collision: an egg is a box 0.64 m wide and 1.45 m tall (`PR`, `PH`) against the blocks. Movement goes one axis at a time in steps under 0.2 m, steps up onto ledges of up to `STEP` (0.6 m) with head room, and falls are capped at 30 m/s (`MAX_FALL`), so normal movement never ends inside a block at any frame rate. If something does put an egg inside one, `pushOut` moves it out the shortest way that ends clear, passes through no other block and doesn't take the egg's middle through a wall: sideways, down from under a ceiling, or up only onto a top within a step. (The old code lifted it half a metre at a time until it was clear, which could launch it up to 12 m onto a roof or out of a building.) If there is no such way within 1.5 m, `rescue` puts it on the nearest spot an egg can stand. The webdriver-only test hook (`window.__sc`) exposes the movement code for the collision audit, and `botMatch(map, bots)` for a bots-only match on a chosen map. Spawns: each map builder places them with `spawn(x,z)` (mirrored like everything else; an egg hatches facing the middle of the map, or the point given as `spawn(x,z,y,faceX,faceZ)`, so no spawn looks at a wall a few metres off or straight at a spawn on the other side; a small arena's `sp` entries take the same five values), and each must be a spot an egg fits (`standV`: something solid underneath and 1.5 m of air above; the egg's box fits inside that block column). A spawn that a map edit has blocked is moved to the nearest clear spot on the same level within 3 m, with a console warning (`spawnSpot`), instead of being lifted onto whatever covers it, and `respawn` never hatches an egg inside a block (it shoves it clear or uses the nearest standing spot). All current spawns are clear; after changing a map, check that the console shows no `spawn moved` or `spawn blocked` warning. Other eggs are drawn with `netsmooth.js`, carried on for at most 200 ms (`REMOTE_AHEAD`) when an update is late, and `drawClamp` keeps them in the map and out of blocks (pushed out the same way as above, and when carried on, no more than half a metre below or a quarter metre above where they were heading), so nobody is drawn, or shot at, inside a wall or a floor. **Capture the flag** (the menu's mode switch, `gmode`; Quick match, Play in room and Practice all start the mode picked): two teams, Red and Blue, a flag each. Touch the other team's flag to pick it up and carry it to your own stand while your own flag is home to score; a dropped flag goes home when one of its own team touches it or after 20 s (`CTF_RETURN_MS`); first to 3 captures (`CTF_CAPS`) wins, or most when the five-minute clock runs out (level is a draw). Whoever carries a flag moves at 85% speed (`CTF_SLOW`), can't aim down sights or scope, and is marked for everyone: the flag is drawn above its head, its name tag shows at any distance and the HUD marker follows it. Shots and yolk bombs don't hurt team-mates (a bomb still hurts whoever threw it). It is unranked: no rating change, and leaving early costs nothing. Every map has a `ctf` entry in `MAPS`, the block where team 0's flag stands; team 1's is the same spot turned 180°. `buildMap` paves a 3 × 3 pad in the team's colour there (`ctfBases`), always builds the whole map (no small arena), gives each team the spawns clearly on its own side (`ctfSpawns`) and lists guard spots near each stand for bots (`ctfPosts`). The flags and score are one small object, `ctfS` = `{s:[red,blue], f:[flag,flag]}`, a flag being `{st:0}` at home, `{st:1,by}` carried or `{st:2,x,y,z,t}` dropped; every change is one of five moves (`take`, `drop`, `ret`, `lost`, `cap`) through `ctfReduce`, which refuses a move that no longer fits. Practice is 3 v 3 (you and two bots against three, `CTF_BOTS`) and applies the moves directly. Online there are no bots and no host: a capture-the-flag room is any room whose code starts with `ctf-` (`rooms/sc-ctf-…`, still under the game's `sc-` rooms; public lobbies are `sc-ctf-pub-1` to `3`; invite links like `#sc-ctf-yolk-123` work as before), the state lives in that room's match node as `round.ctf` (missing means both flags home, 0–0, so starting a match is unchanged), and each move is a transaction on the match node by the browser whose own egg did it (`ctfDo`), or, for a flag whose 20 s are up or whose carrier was cracked or left without its own browser saying so, by whichever browser notices first (the carrier's last seen spot, after 2.5 s). Nothing changes on screen until the node's listener delivers the result (`takeRound` → `ctfSync`, which also writes the feed lines and banners), so two eggs grabbing the same flag can't both get it and the state outlives anyone leaving. Teams have no lobby: you join the smaller team on the way in (`ctfPick`; your node carries `tm`), other eggs are drawn in their team's colour, and if one team is two or more bigger for two seconds the egg with the highest id on it that isn't carrying a flag changes sides and hatches again (`ctfBalance`). Older copies of the game: in a capture-the-flag room every egg puts the round's id plus a half in its node and its events (`Net.wm`) in place of the id, and an egg is only drawn, hittable or heard when its match id matches (the rule above), so a copy from before this mode that opens a `ctf-` room sees nobody, is seen by nobody, and none of its hits or yolk bombs land: it plays on alone until it leaves. Because the room's name decides the mode, a round that an older copy starts or restarts there is still played as capture the flag by everyone else. (Two older copies in one such room could fight each other to 25 cracks and start the next round early, which would reset a capture-the-flag match under way.) Bots (`ctfBotPlan`, called from `botThink` in place of its usual choice of where to go): each team's first bot guards (walks between spots near its stand, chases whoever has its flag), the rest attack; a carrier runs home and waits by the stand while its own flag is away; anyone near a dropped flag goes to it; when both flags are out everyone hunts the other carrier; they never target team-mates. HUD: the two score boxes become the two teams (yours first) with captures and that team's flag as home, taken or dropped; a marker for each flag (`#fm0`, `#fm1`) sits over it on screen or on the edge of the view with an arrow, showing metres away or, for a dropped flag, seconds until it goes home; a line under the top says when you have a flag. The flags are one mesh each (two draw calls in all), and the stands are map blocks. On touch nothing new is needed: flags are picked up, returned and captured by touching them. The test hook adds `ctf` (state, stands, team), `ctfDo`, `ctfReduce`, `setMode` and `ctfBotMatch(map, bots)`. **Rules** (`GMODES`; separate from the free-for-all / capture-the-flag switch, and work with both): **Classic** (the game as it was), **Snipers Only** (everyone, bots too, carries only the Sunny-Side: no pistol swap, no yolk bombs), **Low-Gravity Snipers** (the same at 40% gravity and 1.15× jump speed, about 4.2 m jumps instead of 1.3; yolk bombs would fall slowly too but nobody has any), **Pistols Only** (only the Cracker, no yolk bombs), **One-Shot** (any gun or whack cracks in one; yolk bombs and streak rewards hurt as usual, and the streak rewards need 5, 7, 9, 12 and 18 instead of 3, 5, 7, 10 and 15, since cracks come fast) and **Gun Game** (below). The menu's Rules picker (saved in this browser as `shellcr-rules`, Classic when missing) sets practice and any room match this browser starts. Online the room's match node carries them: `round.gm` (missing or 0 = Classic, so rounds look exactly as before in Classic) is copied by `rematch` into the next round, or replaced by `round.ngm`, which only the room's **host** can set, from the Next match picker on the pause and end cards (everyone else sees it greyed out with the host's name). The host is the egg that has been in the room longest: each egg's node now carries `jt`, when it joined (`Net.joinT`); an older copy sends none and is never host. Like capture the flag, a match with other rules adds a quarter to the match id in nodes and events (`Net.wm`), so an older copy of the game (which would play Classic) neither sees nor hits anyone there and plays on alone. The mode shows in the start banner, the clock box ("Snipers · 25" for "First to 25"), the pause and end cards, and the feed for a joiner whose own pick differs; on touch screens the Bomb and Swap buttons hide in the one-gun modes. Only Classic is ranked: every other mode leaves the rating alone (like capture the flag), says so where the rating is shown, and leaving one early costs nothing; play time and crack stats still count. **Map vote** (`Vote`): the end card offers the next map, two of the maps every egg in the room can build other than the one just played (the same two in every browser, picked from the match id) plus Random. Each click sends a `vote` event (`{id: match, v: map or -1}`); a newer one from the same egg replaces the older, bots don't vote, and the buttons show the counts. When the 12 s end screen runs out the host counts the votes and starts the next match on the winner through `rematch(map)` (the room's match node, so everyone builds it); a tie or no votes picks at random among the leaders, and Random picks any other map. "Next match now" is the host's only (it ends the vote early); if the host has gone, every other browser settles it the same way 5 s later and whichever transaction lands first wins. In practice the vote just picks the Play again map. An older copy in the room ignores votes and still starts the next match in turn after 12 s, which can beat the host's pick. The test hook adds `GMODES`, `rules`, `Vote`, `castVote`, `voteDecide` and `setNextRules`. **Gun Game** (`GMODES[5]`, its `ladder` list): everyone, bots too, starts on the first of ten guns, Cracker, Whisk, Skillet, Beater, Poacher, Sunny-Side, Whisk, Skillet, Poacher, Sunny-Side, with no pistol swap and no yolk bombs, and every crack moves the cracker to the next gun at once (`ggSet`; each egg's rung is `e.gg`). A whack counts as a crack and also sends the whacked egg back one gun (never below the first). The first crack made with the last gun (a Sunny-Side shot, not a whack) finishes the ladder (`gg` = 10) and ends the match with that egg first; when the clock runs out first, whoever is furthest up the ladder wins. Standings, the scoreboard and the end card sort by rung, then cracks, with a Gun column in place of K/D. The HUD's two score boxes show your place ("5/10") with your gun's name and the top rival's place; the slot chips under the ammo show this gun and the next one ("Last gun: crack to win" at the end); the clock box says "Gun Game · 10"; and the feed says when anyone reaches the last gun. Streak rewards are off (they would skip the ladder): none are earned or used, so the Yolk Launcher never replaces a ladder gun (and if one were ever in hand when the ladder moves, it is dropped for the new gun), and the streak box and reward button stay hidden; bots climb the same ladder, aim and perch for whatever gun they have, and whack an egg that comes within reach. Unranked, like the other non-Classic rules. In capture the flag the guns still change with each crack but you stay on the last one and the flags decide the match. Online, `round.gm` is 5, each egg moves up its own ladder when the usual `died` event credits it with a crack, moves itself back when it is whacked, and puts its rung in its own node as `gg` (only in Gun Game; the shared rules accept the extra field); every browser reads the others' `gg` for the HUD and standings and ends its own match once any egg in the match reports the whole ladder done, as when the clock runs out. A Gun Game match adds an eighth to the match id instead of a quarter (`Net.wm`): a copy from before Gun Game reads `gm` 5 as One-Shot, the last rules it knows, and adds a quarter, so it too is kept apart and plays on alone. The test hook adds `ggSet` and `ggTop`. **Streak rewards** (the `KS` list in the page): crack 3, 5, 7, 10 and 15 eggs in one life (`e.streak`, which being cracked resets) and you earn the **Candler** (3), **Quick Hands** (5), **Thick Shell** (7), the **Yolk Launcher** (10) and **The Big Fry** (15). A reward is held until it is used, even through being cracked, and the held ones are cleared when a match starts (`ksReset`, from `resetFx`); one of each kind at a time. The reward key (X by default, the rebindable "Use a streak reward" action in the Controls panel and the menu's key list) uses the oldest one held; on touch screens a round yellow button named after it (`#tKs`) sits above the move stick while you hold one and are alive. Being cracked ends any buff running and takes the launcher away (`ksEnd`, from `die`). The HUD (`#ks`, above the health readout; on touch screens beside the reward button with the phone sideways and above it when upright, hidden while you're cracked) shows the streak, a pip for each of 3, 5, 7, 10 and 15, and a chip for each reward running (seconds or rockets left) or held (the first with its key). Cracks made by the Yolk Launcher and The Big Fry (`rocket` and `fry` in `KS_KILL`) count like any other in the cracks, stats, rating and feed ("Yolk Launcher", "The Big Fry"), but don't add to the streak, so a reward never earns the next one. Practice and online only: the training range has none, and nor does the menu's background match. The **Candler** (candling is holding a lamp to an egg) draws every rival as a glowing orange egg through walls for 3 s (`KS_CANDLE`; a shared no-depth-test copy of the shell, `setGlow`, so one extra draw call per rival only while it lasts); name tags keep their usual distance, and the eggs it reveals are told in the feed. **Quick Hands** makes reloads and weapon swaps 25% faster (`KS_QUICK`, three quarters of the usual time) for 15 s (`KS_QUICK_T`). **Thick Shell** cuts the damage you take by 15% (`KS_SHELL`) for 12 s (`KS_SHELL_T`): eggs have no shield or overheal (health is 0 to 100 and comes back on its own), so a damage cut is what fits. The **Yolk Launcher** is a temporary weapon (`WEAPONS.launcher`, never a loadout; an orange tube with yolk-yellow bands) with 2 rockets (`KS_ROCKETS`) that replaces the gun in your hands; swapping is off while you hold it, it never reloads, and once both rockets are out, or you're cracked, it goes and you're back on the gun you had (with that gun's ammo as it was). Each rocket is a big egg that flies straight at 30 m/s (`KS_ROCKET_V`, trailing yolk) until it touches a block, the edge of the map or an egg, or for 3 s, then bursts like a yolk bomb: up to 100 damage at the centre falling off to nothing at 4 m (`KS_ROCKET_DMG`, `KS_ROCKET_R`), blocked by walls, 40% to whoever fired it (`KS_ROCKET_SELF`), team-mates spared in capture the flag. **The Big Fry** ends the match: a giant frying pan (three meshes, only while it lasts) comes down over the whole map for 10 s (`KS_FRY`) while the screen darkens, a rising siren sounds every second and a countdown with the caller's name shows above the crosshair for everyone. Then a white-hot flash cracks every rival of the caller (the other team in capture the flag), and 1.6 s later the match ends with the caller first in the standings (`fryWin`; in capture the flag their team wins), whatever the cracks. It lands even if the caller is cracked or leaves, and only one can run at a time. Its cracks count for stats and the feed as the caller's. Rating: a Big Fry ending is rated like any other ending, from the end standings with the caller placed first, since getting 15 in a row is the achievement; the usual rules (bots-only weight, too-late joiners) still apply. Bots earn rewards from their own streaks and use them a second or two later (the Yolk Launcher once they can see someone to aim at, and they fire it like any gun; a bot's Candler lets it choose and chase rivals it can't see). Online, using a reward sends a `ks` event on the room's usual `events` list (`{k:'candle'}`, `{k:'quick'}`, `{k:'shell'}`, `{k:'launcher'}`, `{k:'fry'}`) for the feed line and The Big Fry's countdown, and each rocket fired is `{k:'rocket',x,y,z,dx,dy,dz}`, which every browser flies; so nothing is added to the match node and no path or rule changes. The buffs need nothing from other browsers: Thick Shell is applied by the egg being hit and Quick Hands by its owner. Other browsers see the launcher in your hands (weapon index 6 in your node's `w`; an older copy draws a Beater). As with yolk bombs, each browser applies a rocket's damage to its own egg only and sends its own death (`w:'rocket'`). The Big Fry's flash is applied by each browser to its own egg only, which sends its own death (`w:'fry'`), and each browser then ends its own match (the host's next match and map vote follow as usual). A browser takes at most one reward of each kind from an egg every 2 s (rockets aren't limited), and ignores kinds it doesn't know, so events from older copies that still have the Egg Drop and Toaster Sentry (`drop`, `toaster`, `toastx`, and hits on a `<uid>:ts` sentry) do nothing. Older copies of the game ignore the new events: they don't see the launcher's rockets and aren't hurt by them or the Fry (they play on until the room's next match starts). The test hook adds `KS`, `useReward`, `creditKill`, `rockets` and `fry`. **Turkey Week** (`../events.js`, November 15 to 26, 2026): every other egg is drawn as a turkey in a pilgrim hat (`turkeyKit`, `dressEgg`): a tail fan, wings, beak, wattle, feet and the hat as one shared geometry added to the egg's body, so one more draw call per egg, and the menu's egg picture wears the same. There is no switch for it in the game: the one Turkey Week switch on the Back Room page (see `events.js` below) takes it off or puts it back for that browser, and the game reads it once, when it loads. It is only a look: the egg's hit box and headshot height and everything sent to the room are unchanged. **Locker** (`kit.js` beside the page; not public yet): 34 egg types, 17 hats, 20 gun skins and 8 crack effects (egg types 20 to 34, hats 8 to 17, gun skins 13 to 20 and cracks 6 to 8 were added on 19 Nov: sports balls and kits, pirates, suits and robots; the pirate egg's eye patch, the tuxedo's bow tie and the tin robot's wind-up key are merged into the face mesh like a hat, so they add no draw call, and Pirate Gold and Bolts and Springs use a third instanced pool, for coins, drawn only while one plays), numbered as in `../cosmetics.js` (`'shell-crackers'` in `GAMES`). Only the site's captains see it: `SC_PUBLIC = false` near the top of the script, and `lockerOn()` asks `captains.js`'s `may(false, 'locker', 'shellcr-locker')` (`?locker=1` works on localhost or in an automated browser only); flip `SC_PUBLIC` to open it to everyone. A Locker button under the shell swatches opens a screen with a turning preview and a tab for each slot; everything is free to wear while the shop is off, saved as `shellcr-kit` (four base-36 characters: egg type, hat, gun skin, crack effect, each the item's number, 0 for none). For everyone else `kit.js` is never fetched and the game is as before (the page now also loads `captains.js` and the switched-off `cosmetics.js`). Online the same four characters go in the egg's node as `ck`, only when something is worn; older pages ignore it, and a page that sees one fetches `kit.js` to draw that egg, checking every character against the catalogue (anything unknown is drawn plain). Looks only: every egg type is the same egg geometry and hit box (hits come from the egg's position, never from what is drawn), so the ostrich and emu eggs read as big only through their pattern and colour; none is darker than the Charcoal shell or glows like the spawn shield, and in capture the flag the shell stays the team colour (hats, gun skins and crack effects still show). Cost: an egg type is another picture on the egg's own material (a Phong material for the shiny ones), a hat is merged into the face's dark mesh and a gun skin is a recoloured copy of that gun's one geometry with a few small parts added (below or beside it, never above the sights; the muzzle point doesn't move), so none adds a draw call; the rotten egg's stink lines are merged too and its flies are one small mesh (one more draw call, on a rotten egg only). Crack effects play where an egg was cracked, for everyone who sees it, when the egg that cracked it wears one (bots and training dummies cracked by a captain too): each lasts under 2 s and stays low, using two instanced pools and two small meshes that are only drawn while an effect plays, and a 'died' event that arrives after the cracked egg's own node still plays it (`cracked` in `kit.js`). During Turkey Week an egg wearing a Locker hat gets the turkey kit without the pilgrim hat (`turkeyKit(true)`). The test hook `window.__kit` adds `sheet(slot)`, a contact sheet of every item drawn by the real code, plus `wear`, `parse` and `wireOf`. **Top Cracker:** the player with the most Shell Crackers kills of all time on the Leaderboards (25 or more; a tie gives it to everyone tied) holds an earned award, a cracked-egg medal on a cream ribbon shown after their tier badge everywhere badges show, and the game's Kills (all time) board says who holds it (see `miles.js` and `leaderboard.js` below). Nothing in the game changes: it follows the `kills` count the game already reports through `stats.js`. **Spanish** (lang.js): the game's own words are in `shell-crackers/es.js` (the Locker's too), text it puts together goes through `TX()`, HUD parts that change during play are `data-noi18n`, and weapon, map, bot, streak and reward names stay as they are.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/index.html` and `e63334287a3deaa3/games.css`: the Back Room page. Under the header, the What's new strip, the Party and Online panels, the games come in one `<section aria-label="Games">` (leaderboard.js puts the Leaderboards right after it, and the home page's `reveal()` copies this first `<section>` into the home page, so everything the games need is inside it). The last thing in the `<header>` is the Turkey Week switch (`data-turkey-switch`, filled in by `events.js` and shown only during the event; see `events.js` below): the one switch for every game's Thanksgiving look. The page reads, top to bottom: What's new, Playing now, Party, Online, then the games (playingnow.js puts its panel right above the Party panel). **The ad tile:** next to the What's new strip is one small sponsored tile, drawn by news.js (see its paragraph below for how to change the ad or switch it off): beside the strip on a computer, where it takes the strip's height and so doesn't move the games down at all, and a slim 44 px banner under the strip on narrower screens. At the top of the games is **the row of game types** (`<nav class="gs-nav">`): six equal buttons in one row, one per section, each in its section's colour; a click scrolls to that section (layout.js does the scrolling from the button's `data-go`, so the address never gets a `#`, which the home page would read as a room code on reload). Then six sections, **most played type first, and inside each the most played game first** (hours across all players from the Leaderboards data, counted on 13 Nov 2026: Cars 294 h, Shooters 159 h, Parkour 68 h, Party games 66 h, Puzzles 61 h, Arcade 15 h; re-count now and then from the admin page's play-time list and move sections, buttons and cards to match): **Cars** (Slipstream, Boostball, Rampline, Rumble Karts), **Shooters** (Shell Crackers, Backrooms 1v1–4v4, Driftfall, Blockstorm), **Parkour** (Updraft, Tumble Trials, Turbo Trail, Spire, Asteroid Run, Escapade), **Party games** (Chromaflage, Odd One Out, Showdown, Puffer Party, Putterby), **Puzzles** (12 small tiles: Deepword, Trivium and ten daily puzzles; then Stackfall, Melon Merge, 2048, Gridpop; the fifteen least played puzzles came off the list on 18 Nov 2026, see below) and **Arcade** (Coil, Lokop, Tidewick, Patchwork Tower, Flickerwood). Each game is in exactly one section. **Puzzle tiles** (`.pz-wrap` > `.pz-grid` > `<article class="pz">`, one full-width item at the top of Puzzles' `.gc-grid`): each links straight to the puzzle and says what it is in plain words first ("Guess the 5-letter word") with its name under that ("Hunch"), because the names alone don't tell new players what the games are; the picture is the puzzle's phone thumbnail (`m/thumbs/<folder>.jpg`). A new daily puzzle gets a tile here (and one on the phone page) as well as its `DAILY_GAMES` entry. Deepword and Trivium are tiles too, not cards (their links carry the `data-invite` their Play buttons had, so invite links still open them). **Puzzles taken off the list (18 Nov 2026):** the fifteen puzzles with the least all-time play time in the Leaderboards data (added up like the admin page's play-time list, from a read of 17 Nov). Mainstream, Thereabouts and Yardstick, launched that day, don't load stats.js and so couldn't be measured; they were kept (their tiles come last). Boxed doesn't load it either and came off the Back Room lists anyway, unmeasured rather than proven unplayed. The fifteen lost their tile here, their row in the phone page's `GROUPS` and their `DAILY_GAMES` entry (moved to `RETIRED` on the Daily Puzzles page): Unfurl, Sleight, Outline, Scout Report, Teamsheet, Deadline Day, Crossbar, Overland, Journeyman, Stat Duel, Back Four, Boxed, Crosslet, Top Bins and Quartets. Their folders, saves, boards, phone thumbnails and What's new entries are untouched, so old links, bookmarks and saved progress still work, and Boxed is still a public game on the home and Web Development Projects pages. To bring one back, put its tile back here (copy it from the git history), its row back in `GROUPS`, and for a daily puzzle move its line from `RETIRED` back into `DAILY_GAMES`; then update the counts (`.gs-n`, the type button, the intro). Under the Puzzles cards, a small tab (`.gs-ask.gs-ask-sm`, in the section's colour) asks "Miss a puzzle we took away, or want a new one?" with a Tell us button (`data-feedback-open="Puzzles: "`, which starts the suggestion box with that text; see `feedback.js`). There is no big Daily Puzzles card any more: the line above the tiles (`.pz-h`) has today's progress (`#dailyToday`) and a link to the Daily Puzzles page (`daily/`), which has every listed daily puzzle and the streak. Tiles aren't game cards, so Edit layout's sizes leave them alone. Each section is a `<section class="gs gs-<name>">` (`gs-cars`, `gs-shooters`, `gs-parkour`, `gs-partygames`, `gs-puzzles`, `gs-arcade`; not `gs-party`, because layout.js names a section's block after its class and `party` is the Party panel) whose heading has an icon, a one-line subtitle and a count (`.gs-n`), and its own colour (`--gs-c`, set per section in `games.css`). Each game is a launcher card, `<article class="project gc">`: the screenshot (`<a class="shot">` with `preview.jpg` and a genre badge, `.gc-genre`), the name in an `<h3>`, one short line (`.gc-blurb`, about 55 characters so it fits two lines), two or three chips (`.gc-chips`: the first, `class="on"`, says how it's played online, such as "Online · up to 12"; only true things, such as Bots or Solo too), a `<details class="gc-more">` whose "i" button shows the longer description and feature list over the card, and the Play button (`.actions .btn.primary`, with `data-invite="<prefix>"` for online games, which the home page's invite links look for). Some cards have `class="project gc wide"` (two columns wide, picture on the left) so every section's rows come out even with four across, and the wide ones are each section's most played: Parkour two wide (Updraft, Tumble Trials) and four regular (two rows), Arcade three wide (Coil, Lokop, Tidewick) and two regular (two rows), Party games three wide (Chromaflage, Odd One Out, Showdown; Chromaflage is also the section's `pw` card, the one that keeps a whole row on phones) and two regular (two rows), and Cars, Shooters and Puzzles (under its tiles) four regular each (Shooters stays one row of four down to 880 px wide, where the other sections drop to three); if a section's count changes, add or remove `wide` so its rows still come out even. On phones (560 px and narrower) only cards that also have `pw` (Coil) take a whole row; the others pair up two by two. A small script at the end of the Back Room page fills the `#dailyToday` span above the puzzle tiles with today's progress (how many daily puzzles are done and the streak, read from the `daily:<slug>` records, `daily-hub:days` and `deepword:days`; "done" counts only puzzles with a tile, the streak counts every record), and it stays hidden in the home page's copy, which doesn't run that script. The styles are in `games.css`, linked from inside the Games section rather than the `<head>`, so the home page's `reveal()` brings them along; they only build on `site.css`'s colours and fonts and are scoped to `.br-games`, so the other pages that use `site.css` don't change. Four cards across on computers, three on small laptops, two on tablets and phones. To add a game, copy a card into the right section, update the section's game count (`.gs-n`) and the intro's game count, and give it its chips. A brand-new game can wear a small orange "New" pill inside its genre badge and a "New · …" chip (inline styles on Driftfall's card); take them off once it's no longer new. A new daily puzzle doesn't get a card: it goes in `DAILY_GAMES` on the Daily Puzzles page and gets a small tile (above). The home page's game layer (and the phone page's frame) let the Daily Puzzles page open its games inside the same layer, and close it when a page links back to the Back Room itself. Under the games, a "Want more games, or have an idea?" note has a Tell us button (`data-feedback-open`, which feedback.js handles).
- `e63334287a3deaa3/trivium/`: Trivium (Back Room), a trivia game: a Daily ten, Quick play by category, Survival, a 60-second Blitz, and live contests (Firebase, same project and Firestore rules as Hype Cycle Exchange and Boxed; contests are stored in `games` with `kind: "trivia"`; the host's screen grades answers and writes scores, players only write their own answers; invite links go through the home page as `#tv-<code>`). The question bank is in `e63334287a3deaa3/trivium/bank/`, one file per category; each row is `[difficulty 1–3, question, right answer, three wrong answers, fact]`. Questions get a stable ID from a hash of their text, so editing a question's wording makes it a new question. To add a category, add a file and a `<script>` tag for it in its `index.html` (and a color in `CAT_COLOR`). Keep every question and fact in your own words, and avoid facts that go out of date (current office-holders, records likely to be broken). **Turkey Week** (`../events.js`, November 15 to 26, 2026): `bank/thanksgiving.js` adds a seasonal Thanksgiving category (33 questions, id `tgv`) only while the event is running. It is kept out of the usual daily draw (`DAILY_IDS`), so no other day's Daily ten changes; on Thanksgiving Day (`TG_DAY`, 2026-11-26) all ten daily questions come from it. There is no off switch here, since it is a category you choose, not a look. **Spanish:** `es.js` has its words (lang.js), category names included; text built in code goes through `TR()`. Questions, answers, facts and player names are English by design, kept out of translation (`data-noi18n`), with an "En inglés" tag on the logo; contests still store only answer indexes and names, so Spanish and English screens share a contest.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/boostball/`: Boostball (Back Room), rocket-car soccer like Rocket League, 1v1 to 3v3 with bots (Three.js, Firebase Realtime Database rooms under `rooms/bb-…`, public lobbies `bb-pub-1…6`, same project and rules as Rumble Karts; invite links `#bb-<code>`). Car and ball physics follow Rocket League's numbers (from RocketSim) scaled by `UU`; braking and coasting are `BRAKE_ACC` / `COAST_ACC`. Cars have their own, heavier gravity, `CAR_GRAV` (1.25 × the ball's `GRAV`, which the ball keeps, so ball flight and bounces are unchanged), so they hang in the air less and come down with some weight; the jump's kick and hold are raised by `JUMP_K` (1.1), so a full jump still reaches about 95% of its old height but lands about 13% sooner, and boosting straight up still out-pulls car gravity (net 6.25 against 9.5 u/s² before; jump plus boost reaches crossbar height in 1.21 s instead of 1.14 s). Every car use of gravity goes through `CAR_GRAV`: the air, the pull along walls and ramps, the sticky-force test, and the guess at other players' cars between updates (`updateRemotes`). A car's own recoil from the ball is worked out as if it were a quarter heavier (`CAR_HEFT`; what the ball gets, so every shot, is unchanged), soft car bumps push a little less (`BUMP_SOFT`), the car that was hit is pushed harder than the one that did the hitting (`BUMP_HIT` 1.2 and `BUMP_RAM` 0.8 times the usual push, by how much of the closing speed was the other car's; a head-on bump is the plain push for both), every bump is worked out from the speeds the cars had before any bump that step (`c.bv`, set in `physStep`), so you and a bot on the same computer each get your share whichever is handled first, and a car landing on its body on the floor bounces off at `BODY_BOUNCE.floor` (.08, was .25) so it no longer skips. Landings show a drawn-only suspension squash (`SQ_*`, other players' cars too), a thud that grows with the landing speed, and for your own hard landings a small camera dip (scaled by Camera shake, off under reduced motion). Older cached pages still play with newer ones: each page runs its own car, and the gravity difference only changes the guess at a remote car between updates (55 ms apart), by centimetres. A ball resting on the roof can be carried (soft car–ball contacts have friction and a gentler extra push: `CB_*` and `PUSH*` constants; hard hits are unchanged). The match lives in `rooms/bb-<code>/round` (score, clock, last goal; `tc` is the team colour pair, which the host sets), players in `players/<uid>`, touches and pads in short-lived `events`. A goal plays a fireball in the scoring team's colour on every client (one Points cloud, fewer puffs on touch screens). Settings → Team colours picks blue/orange, green/purple, red/teal, yellow/violet or pink/cyan (each pair checked under simulated colour blindness); online everyone sees the host's pick. Settings and garage are saved only in the browser under `boostball-` keys (`teams`, `marker`, `gfx`, `name`, `body`, `accent`, …; `kit` only with the garage switch on, see Kit below). **Look:** the three car bodies are sculpted from cross-sections in `BODY_SHAPES` (rows of `[z, half width, bottom y, top y]` for the lower body, the glass cabin and the roof), which `loft()` eases into smooth solids, with a wing, lights, grille and lathe-turned tyres added; all three still fill the one hitbox, so a new body is only a new row there (shared wheel geometry is in `SHARED_G`, which the dispose code skips). **Hidden garage:** twelve more bodies are finished but not offered yet: Boulder (lifted truck), Glide (aero coupe), Pip (little hatch), Sliver (wedge), Growler (long-bonnet coupe), Bounce (open buggy), Gravel (rally hatch), Hauler (pickup), Loaf (minibus), Needle (open-wheel racer), Flare (hot rod) and Rhino (plated truck), ids 3 to 14 in `BODIES` and `BODY_SHAPES`. They show only while `garageOn()` is true: the account signed in is one of the site's captains (`../captains.js`, see below), or, for testing, the page is opened with `?garage=1` on localhost or in an automated browser (remembered in sessionStorage for that browser tab, `?garage=0` forgets it; on the live site the address does nothing). A captain's answer arrives a moment after the page loads and goes away if they sign out (`garageSync` redraws the picker and puts the saved hidden body and kit on or takes them off). Then the picker lists all fifteen with small pictures drawn from the real models (`drawThumbs`, by the preview's own renderer, one body a frame), a hidden choice is saved as `boostball-gbody` beside `body` (which only ever holds a public body, so with the switch off, or on an older cached page, the last public choice is used), and bots pick from all fifteen. Without the switch the picker, the saved choice, the bots and what is sent are as before. **The one line that makes them public for everyone is `const GARAGE_PUBLIC=false;`: change it to `true`.** They are looks only: nothing in the physics reads `body`, so every body has the one hitbox, weight and handling. Some stand a little outside the box, about as far as the first three do (those reach 1.34 from the middle lengthways, 0.96 sideways with their tyres and 0.85 up): Rhino's ram and Boulder's bull bar reach 1.4, Needle's front wing 1.37, the big tyres 1.02 to 1.05 sideways, Loaf's roof rails 0.94 up; Needle's body is much lower and narrower than the box (its wheels and wings fill the corners) and Pip is 0.05 short at each end. Online a body travels as its id (`bd` in the player node, and in the host's `bh` row for bots): a new page draws any id it knows whatever its own switch says, an id it does not know draws as Comet (`bodyId`), and a page from before this change draws ids above 2 as Brick. So add new bodies at the end and never reorder them. A garage body is a row in `BODY_SHAPES` with `make(k)`, which builds it from the kit in `carKit`: `k.loft` (the same lofts; `lin:1` gives flat panels with soft creases), `k.patch` (a thin skin on a loft's surface, for windows, stripes, bands and flames), `k.box`, `k.cyl`, `k.tube` (cages, bull bars), `k.arch` (mudguards), in the materials `k.B` paint, `k.G` glass, `k.A` accent, `k.H` and `k.T` lights, or a plain colour number for trim (all trim is one vertex-coloured material, so a body is at most six meshes however many colours it has, the same as the first three); `wheels` gives each wheel's place, size and width (drawing only) and `glow` where the light glows sit on Pretty. To add one: a row at the end of `BODY_SHAPES`, a name at the same place in `BODIES`, keep it within about 0.86 wide and 1.3 long from the middle, and check its cost against Comet's (21 draw calls and about 6,170 triangles alone on Pretty; the twelve are 19 to 21 calls and 3,900 to 6,100 triangles). **Kit (hidden):** decals, boost trails, goal explosions and a few extras are finished but not offered yet: 62 decals, 49 boost trails, 42 goal explosions (numbers 1 to 21 and 23 to 43; 22 is Carbon Star), 36 antenna toppers, 12 roof hats, 5 sets of wheels and 4 engine sounds; the second wave in late November 2026 added the sports, pirate, suit and robot ones (Team Hoops, Varsity, Pinstripe Suit, Tuxedo, Circuit Board, Robot Plating and Jolly Oars decals; Gold Coins, Sea Spray, Circuit Sparks, Ticker Tape and Camera Flashes trails; Ticker Tape Parade, Treasure Chest and Floodlights goals; soccer ball, basketball, baseball, anchor, robot head and parrot toppers; pirate tricorn, fedora, football helmet, captain's cap, robot dome and ref cap hats; Black Chrome wheels), all made from the existing sprite sheet and shapes, so they cost what the first ones do. The third wave (also late November 2026) added decals 39 to 62 (sports numbers, stitches and pitch lines, pinstriping, paws and leopard spots, waves, a treasure map, an eye patch, a tracksuit and chain, a suit and tie, hazard stripes, synthwave, argyle and a title belt), trails 33 to 49 (green and violet flames, an afterburner, sports balls, cannon smoke, gems, paper planes, spare parts, pizza, a comet tail, blossoms, ghosts, pixel fire, ice, pucks and Fire and Ice, whose two strands twist round each other: `helix` in TRAILS), goal explosions 26 to 43 and toppers 13 to 36 (sports kit, a tricorn whose three walls arch up between the points, an eye-patch flag, hats in miniature, food, a saucer and more); the trails' new pictures are cells 36 to 48 of the sprite sheet. Four of the new goal explosions are big: Stadium Wave (about 1,900 crowd cards on both tiers of the bowl flip to the scorer's team colour in a wave that rolls twice round the whole stadium, with four floodlight beams sweeping the pitch and a pulse in the team colour running down it; the cards are one mesh of quads moved in its vertex shader), Tidal Wave (a wall of water rolls about 60 units out over the pitch and crashes; one bent plane), Light Dragon (a dragon with a horned head and open jaws, beating wings and a tail fan loops over the goal end and dives back in; its body is one tube whose rings are moved along the flight path each frame) and Disco Floor (most of the scoring half lights up in tiles from one additive plane); the rest stay near the goal mouth. They never flash the screen; with the browser's reduced-motion setting they do not shake the camera and the big ones fade in place instead of sweeping. Like the others, everything they draw is made the first time it is needed and kept hidden under the stage (Kraken, Piano Keys and Red Carpet use instanced meshes), so playing them again allocates nothing; the scene, geometries and shader programs measured the same after twenty more goals. A decal costs no draw call and a topper one (as before), and a goal explosion adds up to 6 draw calls while it plays. Each is a Boostball item in `../cosmetics.js` (name, rarity, price, packs; see that file below) and is drawn by `kit.js` beside the page, which is fetched only when something needs it, so today's players never download it: the page itself gained only the hooks (`KIT`, which stays null until then) and one deferred `<script>` for `cosmetics.js`, which it asks whether cosmetics are switched on. **What shows under each switch:** with both off (everyone today) nothing at all: no pickers, a saved kit is ignored, nothing more is sent and the bots are plain. With the garage switch on (a captain's account, `GARAGE_PUBLIC`, or `?garage=1` on localhost) a Kit section under the accent colours lists every item, all free to wear, saved as `boostball-kit` (`{decal, trail, goal, topper, hat, wheels, engine}` item ids). With the cosmetics switch on (`ENABLED` in `cosmetics.js`, or `?cosmetics=1` on localhost or in an automated browser) the same section shows with the items you do not own greyed out: you wear what your locker owns (bought in `shop/` in the Boostball packs, and also pickable in its Locker tab), the choice is saved in that locker, and the twelve hidden bodies stay hidden unless the garage switch is on too. With either switch on the bots this browser runs wear random kits. Whatever its own switches say, a page draws the kit of any car that arrives wearing one (fetching `kit.js` then), exactly as it does bodies. There is no third switch: to open it to everyone, flip one of those two. **How it works:** a car's kit is six item numbers (`c.kit`: decal, trail, goal explosion, topper, hat, wheels; 0 for none) and nothing in the physics reads it; the engine sound is its driver's alone. A decal is one picture per car, painted in code (`DECALS`: a side, a top, a front and a rear panel drawn in car units over the team colour, which always stays the base; they get the team's own darker and lighter shades and the accent colour) and wrapped onto the paint by where each triangle is and which way it faces, so it fits all fifteen bodies; the taxi, police cruiser and ice cream truck are made-up liveries with no words and no real emblems. A boost trail replaces the flame with sprites from one shared pool (`Sprites`: little pictures cut from one sheet drawn in code), 48 a car at most and fewer on Fast and on phones; trails keep most of the car's speed so they sit close behind it, where its own driver can see them. A goal explosion (`GOALS`) plays in the goal mouth in place of the fireball when its owner scores (`goalExplosion(p,team,by)`; an own goal, or a scorer without one, gets the fireball): sprites plus a few models welded from simple shapes (the saucer, the shark, the foot, the letters), 2.2 to 3 seconds long, and it is cleared when the kickoff is set up, so it cannot outlast the 3.5 s pause after a goal. The garage's preview shows the decal on the turning car, the trail behind the car while the Boost trail tab is open, and the goal explosion in a small goal mouth when Preview is pressed. **Carbon Star (an exclusive gift):** one goal explosion is made for one player, Carbon, whose Google account is the one entry in `../captains.js`'s `GIFTS` table; the captains may wear it too (`CAPTAIN_GIFTS`), and nobody else is offered it. It is Boostball goal item 22 in `../cosmetics.js`, marked `give: 'exclusive'` (in no pack or chest, never sold or gifted on, left out of the shop's Locker), and `GOALS[22]` in `kit.js` is only a placeholder `{gift: ...}` that lets the captains' Kit list it, labelled "made for Carbon" (a Stage never plays it, and bots never get it). It is drawn by `carbon-star.js` beside the page, fetched only when it can be needed: the account signed in here has it, or a car in the room arrives wearing it. **Who sees what:** Carbon gets a **Locker** button under the accent colours (`giftLocker()`, only while the Kit is not shown to him) that opens a screen with exactly one thing in it, Carbon Star under Goal explosions, with On/Off (saved as `boostball-gift`; on until he turns it off), a preview that replays (its own small renderer, made when the screen opens and thrown away when it closes) and the line "Made for Carbon."; a captain picks it in the Kit like any other goal explosion, and its Preview plays in the garage picture (`giftPreview`/`giftFrame`); everyone else sees nothing new. For testing only, `?carbonstar=1` on localhost or in an automated browser shows the Locker. **Online:** it travels like any goal explosion, as goal number 22 in the kit (`kt`, e.g. `'0.0.22'`), but a page draws it only when the scorer's account has it (`giftScorer`: for your own car, the account signed in here; for another player's car, the account id its room node is keyed by, checked with `gift(item, uid)`, after `giftsReady()` has fetched `miles.js` if a captain may need checking; never a bot), whatever the player's data claims; otherwise the scorer gets the plain fireball. A page from before this change knows no goal 22 and plays the fireball, with no errors (checked in a private room on the real database with four pages: Carbon's stand-in, another player, a page from before and a player sending a forged Carbon Star kit, whose goals showed the fireball everywhere but on the forger's own page). **What it looks like** (our own design): a dying star flares in the goal mouth with long thin rays and collapses (0.6 s); a ripple races out from the goal, reaching the far goal 0.48 s after the goal and the back of the far stands at 0.97 s, and behind it everything the camera can see is space: the pitch is a faint teal honeycomb lattice (carbon's own pattern) over the void, with the centre circle and halfway line and slow ember swells moving out from the goal; the walls, goals, stands, light towers and pads are thin glowing outlines; the sky is a slowly turning indigo nebula full of stars with a red-amber carbon star in a sooty shell high over the scoring goal; the cars and the ball are white glowing outlines with a little star dust behind them. It holds through the celebration and cross-fades back 0.8 s before the countdown, or before the final whistle after an overtime or last-second winner (`CSAPI.holdLeft()`); free play holds 3.4 s. The kickoff, the end of the match and leaving to the menu also stop it, and a second goal while it plays starts it again. A soft boom, a deep swell and a quiet shimmer are made in code. There is never a full-screen flash or a strobe; with the browser's reduced-motion setting there is no flare burst and no ripple, only a cross-fade into space and back. On Fast and on phones (`low`) the sky has fewer layers (three octaves of cloud instead of four, one layer of stars instead of two, no second cloud layer) and half the star dust. **How it is drawn:** the arena's own objects are never changed. `MAP` (everything in the scene once the stadium is built, plus the pads) is copied once into stand-ins: each solid part gets a mesh sharing its geometry, painted with the sky seen through it (so it hides what is behind it and lines up with the sky), small solid parts (posts, pads) get a glowing rim instead, every hard edge of the map is one merged line set (`EdgesGeometry`), and thin parts (the glass walls, nets, glows, field lines) are hidden as the ripple passes them. Once the ripple has covered everything, the arena itself is hidden, the stand-ins draw as opaque and the shadow map stops updating, so a frame costs less than a normal one. The cars' and the ball's meshes have their material swapped for one outline material while it lasts and put back afterwards; their blob shadows, the skid marks and the normal particles are hidden. The stand-ins, the line set, the star dust pool and the six shaders are made once (the shaders are compiled a moment after `carbon-star.js` loads, so a goal does not stall) and kept for the visit; after a goal they leave the scene, and nothing else is left behind (the scene, the map's visibility, the materials and the shadow setting are as before, checked after every test goal). Its cost, measured on the software-drawn test machine (1366 × 768, practice 3v3 on Pretty, the player's camera): a normal frame was 249 draw calls and 73,700 triangles; while the ripple crosses, 156 to 264 (the arena and the stand-ins are both drawn, but thin parts and shadows drop out); while it holds, 76 calls and 20,000 triangles; after it, 244 and 73,400 (the bots had moved). On Fast: 173, then 70, then 165. The map makes 105 stand-ins, 23 thin parts and one line set of 4,508 edges; its update is about a hundredth of a millisecond of script a frame.
