The real cost is the sky shader, drawn once over the whole screen, which is why Fast and phones get fewer layers; frame times on the test machine are software-drawn and mean little. **Cost:** a decal adds no draw call (a 3v3 frame on Pretty is 287 calls and 82,792 triangles with or without six decals): it is one 512 × 512 texture (256 on Fast and on phones), shared by cars that look the same and freed with the last of them, and the paint's triangles are stored unshared so each can sit in its own panel. Every trail and goal-explosion sprite in the arena together is one more draw call (one `Points` object of 1,104 sprites at most, hidden while none is alive; six trails took about a hundredth of a millisecond a frame of script on the test machine). A goal explosion adds its models while it plays, one to three meshes for most and eight for the disco ball; a set piece's models are built the first time it plays, a one-off pause of a few tens of milliseconds on the slow, software-drawn test machine. A topper or a hat is one more mesh on the car that wears it (the two share one, and it is drawn again for the shadow on Pretty); other wheels cost nothing. **Fairness:** looks only: the hitbox, the physics and the ball are untouched. A goal explosion only plays while the ball is out of play, stays near the goal it was scored in and is gone before the countdown. No trail can hide the ball: its sprites are small (the largest solid ones, the ducks, toast and bats, are about 1.5 across against the ball's 3.65; smoke rings grow to about 2.4 but are hollow), a car has 48 at most and none lasts much over a second. Measured by driving a boosting car straight across the line from a watching camera to the ball, 4 units in front of the ball, and comparing every frame drawn with and without the trail: the worst single frame changed 14% of the ball's disc (hearts, popcorn, ducks and toast; most trails stay under 10%), and no frame came near half. A decal keeps the team readable: measured over all ten team colours, between 55% (Ice Cream Truck, whose roof and upper sides are cream) and 100% of a decal's side and top panels stay the team colour or a shade of it; the next lowest are Honeycomb, Graffiti Drips and Donut Sprinkles at about 70%. **Online:** your kit travels as `kt` in your player node (`'5.18.15'`, written only when you wear something, so a node without a kit is what it always was) and a bot's as six more numbers at the end of its `bh` row. A new page reads up to 255 in each place and draws nothing for a number it does not know; a page from before this ignores both and draws the plain car, flame and fireball (checked in a private room on the real database with three pages: kit on, switches off, old page). The room accepted the new field as it is: no rule, config or path was changed. **To add an item:** add its painter, trail row, set piece, topper, hat, wheel or engine at the END of its list in `kit.js` and an item with the same number in `cosmetics.js` (and put it in a pack); never reorder either list, because the number is what is saved and sent. The stadium is swept around a rounded rectangle outside the arena by `ring()` in `buildArena`: two tiers of drawn crowd, LED boards with made-up sponsor names, and a roof with a strip of lights under its edge. On Pretty graphics (`glossOn()`: not on Fast, and not on touch screens) the paint is a clear-coated `MeshPhysicalMaterial`, trim, glass and the ball are `MeshStandardMaterial`s, and all reflect `stadiumEnv()`, a 128 px cube map drawn in code; the sun moves nearly overhead and casts real shadows of the cars and ball onto the pitch (one 2048 × 3072 shadow map over the whole field, only cars and the ball drawn into it; the old blob shadows fade to a faint contact shadow); and soft glows (`glowSprite`) sit on the floodlights, head and tail lights and the boost. Pretty also adds bloom and a colour grade (`bloom`, drawn by `drawScene()` after the scene): the finished frame is copied to a texture, its brightest parts are shrunk to a quarter size and blurred, and a last pass redraws the frame with that glow added, slightly more colour and contrast and darker corners, so floodlights, goal frames, boost and the goal fireball glow; the pitch switches to a per-pixel-lit material with a grass-grain normal map (`fineFloor()`), and faint beams fall from the floodlights; the scene is still drawn once straight to the screen, the HTML HUD never glows, and if the copy ever fails the bloom turns itself off for the visit. On every setting the pitch texture has mowing bands and a pool of floodlight fading to the corners, and the sky has stars (a fixed `Points` cloud). Pretty protects the frame rate by itself: `fxWatch()` (called from `drawScene()`) steps `fxTier` down when frames stay over 24 ms for 3 s after `autores.js` has already lowered the resolution to 0.7 or less, from 2 (everything) to 1 (no bloom or grade) to 0 (also no real shadows or fine grass; paint, reflections and glows stay). It never steps back up during a visit, and picking Pretty again in Settings starts from 2. `lookSync()` switches all of that when the setting changes, and `glossOn()` is part of each car's `meshKey` and the garage's key, so changing Graphics rebuilds the cars. On Fast and on phones the same bodies are drawn with the flat Lambert materials, blob shadows and no glows. Nothing about the look is sent to other players. Bots: practice offers Rookie, Pro and All-Star (`DIFF`); online, the host's browser runs Pro bots in the empty seats. A party launch (party.js's room `p` + 6 characters, `isPartyRoom`) is people only: the room's first round is made with `bo: 0`, later rounds keep it, and a host in a party room adds no bots even if an older page made the round with `bo: 1`; the menu shows Bots fill in locked to No bots with "No bots in party games" while the room box holds a party code (the saved choice is kept for other rooms). A party of one plays alone in the arena until someone joins. Bots drive with the same controls and car as players (`botThink` only sets the car's inputs) and see the ball a reaction time late (`react`); the levels differ only in the numbers in `DIFF` (reaction, aim, steering, boost and pad use, flips, aerials, keeping). They line up behind the ball, shoot toward the post away from a defender, pick up boost pads on the way back when short, go round a ball rather than through it when on the wrong side, clear straight up the field from in front of their own net, and get themselves unstuck from corners and the back of a goal. Before a change to the bots, play old against new headlessly through `window.__bb` over a couple of hundred matches per level: single results swing a lot. Each match the bots get random names from `BOT_NAMES` (no two alike, never a human player's name in the room; the host gives a bot a new name if someone joins with its name). Online the host sends each bot's name as its position in `BOT_NAMES` (in its `bh` row), so add new names at the end and don't reorder or remove any. The game loop runs from animation frames; when they stall (the tab is hidden) a 250 ms timer keeps `step` ticking in 100 ms slices without drawing, so a hidden host's bots, ball and node keep going (browsers run that timer about once a second in a hidden tab, so others then get its updates once a second) instead of freezing until the room picks a new host. **Smoothness:** the physics runs in fixed 1/120 s ticks and each frame is drawn part-way between the last two ticks (`tweenMark` / `tweenIn` / `tweenOut` around `stepVisuals`, by `acc/H_STEP`), because a frame is rarely a whole number of ticks long (two, give or take one, at 60 Hz; one or none at 144 Hz; two or three on a laptop at 45 frames a second), and drawing straight from the last tick made your car and the ball move unevenly even at a full frame rate. Only the drawing uses the in-between values (your car, the bots this page runs, and the ball; other players' cars are already moved every frame by `updateRemotes`): the physics, what is sent to other players and what the bots see are unchanged, so keep any new drawing code inside `stepVisuals` and any new game logic outside it. Each car is welded into a few meshes when it is built (`joinMeshes` in `buildCarModel`: one mesh per material for the body, three per wheel) instead of about 42, since every mesh is a separate draw call and is drawn again for the shadow on Pretty; add new fixed body parts before the `joinMeshes` call and they are welded in too, and give anything that moves by itself (like the wheels and the boost flame) its own mesh after it. In automated browsers only (`navigator.webdriver`), `window.__bb` exposes the game for tests. Other players' cars (`updateRemotes`) are carried on from their last update along the curve they were turning on (`tw`, the turn rate worked out from successive updates), for up to 0.4 s when an update is late; the drawn car moves at its own speed and closes the gap to that estimate no faster than `CORR_V`, so a late update makes it glide rather than jump. A host running no bots sends no `bh`, and everyone then drops the bots it ran before. **Turkey Week** (`../events.js`, November 15 to 26, 2026): the ball is drawn as a pumpkin (`drawPumpkin` paints the ball texture, plus a stem on the ball mesh) and the pitch in autumn grass colours (`drawField`). The ball's physics and the room data are unchanged. The game reads the one Turkey Week switch on the Back Room page (see `events.js` below) once, when it loads; it has no button of its own. **Score** (`points()`): goal 100, assist 50, save 50, demolition 25, shot on goal 10 (was 20) and 2 for every touch of the ball (`stats.t`; the same car again within 0.6 s is one touch), so a player who is in the play scores without a goal or a save; the end-of-match table has a Touches column. Touches travel in their own fields beside the stats (`tch` for a player, `bth` for the host's bots), because a page from before them reads `st` as exactly five numbers; such a page still shows the old score (no touch points, 20 a shot) until it reloads. It has Spanish (`boostball/es.js`, through `../lang.js`; EN | ES switch under Settings): menus, settings, controls, match calls, feed, results, pause and the Kit; team colour names are translated where messages are put together (`TN()`), only on screen (the room still sends ids and names, never translated text), and the cosmetics' own names and descriptions come from `../cosmetics.js` and stay English.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/coil/`: Coil (Back Room), a snake arena (one canvas, Firebase Realtime Database rooms under `rooms/cl-…`, public arenas `cl-pub-1…6`, same project and rules as Rumble Karts; invite links `#cl-<code>`). Rooms hold 10 people and the host (whoever has been in the room longest) runs bots to fill the arena to 12, sending them in its own node (`bh` positions, `bs` skins, names and bodies). A party launch (party.js's room `p` + 6 characters, `isPartyRoom`) is people only: there the host runs no bots (`wantBots` gives 0), so a party of one has the arena to itself. Each browser judges only its own snakes' deaths. The game loop runs from animation frames; when they stall (the tab is hidden) a 250 ms timer steps the world in 50 ms slices so a hidden host's bots keep moving for everyone (its own snake stays put, as before; browsers run the timer about once a second in a hidden tab, so the bots then reach others once a second, smoothed by netsmooth.js). **Turkey Week** (`../events.js`, November 15 to 26, 2026): food is drawn as corn kernels and cranberries (`tkFood`, two small sprites) and every snake wears a pilgrim hat seen from above (`tkHat`). Where the food is, its value and everything sent to the room are unchanged. The game reads the one Turkey Week switch on the Back Room page (see `events.js` below) once, when it loads; it has no button of its own. **Wardrobe (November 2026, captains only for now):** 99 looks, drawn in code by `wardrobe.js` beside the page: 29 colours (16 solid, 5 neon, 7 two-colour gradients, a rainbow and a flowing rainbow), 11 animal patterns, 21 design patterns and 22 object snakes (one pattern is worn at a time, and the ones marked "your colour" use the chosen colour), 13 heads (the second wave in late November 2026 added the sports, pirate, suit and robot ones: tennis ball, jersey stripes, racing checker, medal ribbon, pinstripe, necktie, LED strip, basketball and football patterns; tennis balls, rope and anchors, robot segments, cable and gold chain snakes; eye patch, tricorn, fedora, bandana, sweatband, bow tie and robot visor heads; a dark pattern such as pinstripe keeps a pale halo and ring so the snake stays easy to see), a sparkle trail and candy or coin death bursts; their names and numbers are Coil's items in `../cosmetics.js`. Nothing about it is on the page or read from storage unless `wardrobeOn()` says this player may: `COIL_PUBLIC` (the one line that opens it to everyone), a captain (`../captains.js`), or `?wardrobe=1` on localhost or in an automated browser. A captain gets a Wardrobe button under the skin picker and wears everything free; the look is saved in this browser as `coil:look` and sent to the room as `lk` in the player's own node, a short string of slot letters and item numbers (`c` colour, `a`/`d`/`o` animal, design or object pattern, `h` head, `t` trail, `b` burst; for example `c19.d7.h4`), sent only while something is worn. `lookOf` accepts only that shape (at most 27 characters); an unknown item number draws as nothing, older pages ignore `lk` and draw the player's skin, and the skin index `sk` is still sent as before. `wardrobe.js` is fetched only when the Wardrobe is open to this player or a snake in the room wears something, so for everyone else the page draws exactly as before. A look is drawing only: the same segments, radius, speed and hit area as every snake. The body keeps the same number of drawImage calls as the plain one (the shadow and outline disc every other segment, one ball per segment, the head, and one overlay tile every other segment in place of the sheen dot; patterns that follow the body turn their tile with one setTransform each), hats are one cached sprite, and every pattern keeps a bright halo and outline round the body so it never melts into the floor. Bots still wear the plain skins. It has Spanish: its words and the Wardrobe's are in `coil/es.js` (lang.js), with a Language switch on the menu and the pause screen; bot names, and Wardrobe item names from cosmetics.js, stay as they are.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/showdown/`: Showdown (Back Room), no-limit Texas Hold'em for up to 8 players (Firebase Realtime Database rooms under `rooms/sd-…`, same project and rules as Rumble Karts; invite links go through the home page as `#sd-<code>`). Everything travels through each player's own node: the dealer (the player with the newest dealer number, or with none yet, whoever has been in the room longest) runs the table and publishes it as JSON in `tb`, and seals each player's hole cards to that player's key in `hc` (ECDH P-256 + AES-GCM via Web Crypto), so the database never holds readable hole cards; players write only their own action (`ac`), sit-out (`sit`), rebuy (`rb`) and chat (`ch`). The dealer's browser does see every card, so it isn't cheat-proof against a dealer with developer tools. If the dealer drops, the next player takes over from the last table it saw and refunds the hand in progress. Bots (Easy, Normal, Hard) fill empty seats and run in the dealer's browser. A party launch (party.js's room `p` + 6 characters, `isPartyRoom`) is people only: the room starts with `bots: 0` in the table settings whatever this browser's Fill seats says, a new dealer that finds bots in a party room turns them off and lets its bots go, and the menu shows Fill seats locked to People only with "No bots in party games" while the room box holds a party code (a party of one waits at the table for another player, as in any People only room). Play chips only; nothing is stored between sessions except practice stats in the browser. The look is all page elements and CSS, with no pictures: the rail, felt, cards (`.cd`), chips (`.chip`) and seat plates are drawn with gradients and shadows, and the felt's weave is one small speckle tile made once in the script (`feltGrain`, which has its own random-number generator so it never touches the one the cards and bots use). `layout()` sizes and places everything; on a short, wide screen (a phone held sideways: wider than tall and at most 520 px high) it adds `.dock` to `#app`, which turns the action bar into a column on the right, shows the table message in `#dockmsg` above the buttons instead of in the middle of the table, and gives the table the space to the left. The moving parts are the deal, the card flip, winning cards lifting (the `translate` property, so it can run with the flip, which uses `transform`), chips sliding to and from the pot, and a slow halo on whoever's turn it is; all of them are switched off under `prefers-reduced-motion`. None of this is sent to other players. It has Spanish: its words are in `showdown/es.js` (lang.js), with a Language switch on the menu; the hand log and table messages still travel between players in English, and each page shows them in its own language (`SD_TR()` in es.js). **Wardrobe (hidden: captains only for now).** `SD_PUBLIC = false` and `wardOn()` (captains.js's `may(false, 'wardrobe', 'sd-wardrobe')`; `?wardrobe=1` for tests on localhost or in an automated browser) decide whether the menu shows a Wardrobe button; for everyone else `showdown/wardrobe.js` is never fetched and the table is exactly as before, unless someone at the table wears something, in which case it is fetched to draw them. It has 35 items in four slots, numbered as in `../cosmetics.js` (`sd-…`): 18 seat looks (a small portrait drawn in the seat's circle on the player's own colour, in place of the initial: sports kits, two robots, two pirates with eye patches, suits, a wizard), 7 card backs (the player's face-down cards as everyone sees them; faces never change), 5 felts (the table cloth on the captain's own screen only, kept about as dark as the green so cards, chips and numbers read the same) and 5 win flourishes (a burst of about 1.6 s at the sides of the seat plate when that seat wins a pot, drawn behind the cards and plate; off under `prefers-reduced-motion`). Everything is HTML, CSS and small SVG pictures made in the script. Saved in this browser as `sd:wardrobe` (JSON `[seat, back, felt, win]`); online, a player's own node carries `cz: 'seat.back.win'` only while something is worn (the felt is never sent). Older pages ignore `cz`, the dealer's table JSON is unchanged, and a page draws the usual look for numbers it doesn't know. Looks only: no rule, timer, card face or chip changes. The `?test` hook `window.__sd` adds `WD` (with `wear(slot, n)`), `wardOn`, `wdLoad` and `wearOf`. Its words and item names are in `showdown/es.js`.
  Other players' hole cards only ever go into `tb` (each seat's `sh`, hand name `h` and best five `c`, plus a "shows" line in the log) at the showdown: an all-in run-out deals the rest of the board with every hand still face down, and at the showdown hands are turned over in order (whoever bet or raised last on the final betting round first, else the first player after the button), and a hand that can't win or tie any pot it's in is mucked face down instead (its action `l` becomes `Muck`, the log says it mucks, and its cards are never published). Hands that end with everyone else folding are never shown.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/chromaflage/`: Chromaflage (Back Room), paint-yourself hide and seek for up to 8 players (Three.js, Firebase Realtime Database rooms under `rooms/cf-…`, same project and rules as Rumble Karts; Quick match uses `rooms/cf-pub-1` to `cf-pub-4`; invite links go through the home page as `#cf-<code>`). Hiders paint their own bodies in a paint studio (brush, an eyedropper that reads the true colour of whatever surface you click, fill, undo, glossiness) and pose to hide (crouch under tables, lie under beds, flatten, or stick to a wall with G or the Stick button and slide up, down and along it at any height, upright or sideways, until they jump to let go); seekers tag them with a blaster that fires a shot every 0.18 s (`FIRE_GAP`) for as long as the mouse button or the touch Tag button is held, with no reload pause: a miss costs one of 10 ink (`INK_MAX`), a hit costs nothing, and ink trickles back all the time at one shot every 2.5 s (`INK_REGEN`), so a seeker can keep firing at a hider who jumps about but blind spraying soon runs the tank dry and then gets a shot every 2.5 s. Fire rate and ink live only on the seeker's computer, which also decides hits (a ray against the hiders as it draws them) and sends a tag claim (`tg`) for the host to check, so changing them needs no change to the room data. Modes: Classic and Infection. Like Showdown, everything travels through each player's own node at `rooms/cf-<code>/players/<uid>`, because the shared rules expect `n, c, x, z, h, ts` there and refuse other paths (such as `events`) for new prefixes: position and pose (`y, po, fl, p`, and `wl`: 0, or 1 to 4 for the way the wall a hider is stuck to faces, with `y` its height), body (`s` shape, `sz` size, `rf` glossiness), paint (`pv` version and `pd`, the 256 × 256 body texture as a WebP data URL, or JPEG where the browser can't make WebP; about 1 KB for a plain colour and 10–20 KB for a detailed pattern, re-sent at most every 1.2 s and only when it changed, so late joiners read it straight from the node), the last shot (`sh`), squeak (`ta`) and tag claim (`tg`). The host (newest host number `he`, else whoever has been in the room longest) publishes the round as JSON in its own `rs` and checks tag claims loosely; if it leaves, the next player carries on from the last round it saw. Anything read from another player's node is untrusted (any player can make itself host with a high `he`): `sanitizeRS` rebuilds a received round state field by field (known phases, numbers clamped, player ids checked and lists capped, settings through `cleanSet`), `remoteTarget` keeps positions inside the map and angles finite, and scores are shown as numbers, so keep new round-state fields going through `sanitizeRS` and escape anything put in `innerHTML`. A tab in the background gets no animation frames, so a 1 s timer keeps its node's `ts` fresh and, for the host, keeps the round ticking. Bodies are modelled about 1.7 units tall and drawn at `BODY` (0.64) times their size setting, so Normal is about 1.08 m. The Doll is a soft, rounded figure (a big round head on a short neck, a plump body, thick arms and legs with rounded ends, two small eyes), each part turned on a lathe (`latheGeo`, `dollParts`) with the texture spread evenly up the profile and normals taken from the profile so there are no shading seams; the Block is a slim slab. Each body shape has a 256 × 256 texture atlas where every texel knows its 3D spot on the body, so brushes paint by distance in 3D (`atlasOf`, `Paint`). Keep each part's region of the atlas (the `rect` passed to `latheGeo`) when reshaping a body, or saved and synced paint jobs land on the wrong parts; the Doll was reshaped inside the same regions, so paint saved before that lands on roughly the same places. Maps are axis-aligned boxes plus round props, built by `buildManor` (Hollyhock Manor: 48 × 38 m, a party night in a country house, with an 8 m grand hall whose staircase climbs to a gallery, ten rooms under 4.2 m ceilings), `buildMuseum` (Marlowe Museum: 58 × 50 m, a 7 m lobby, a top-lit grand gallery with free-standing walls, six galleries, a gift shop, a café and the storeroom) `buildRyokan` (Kirinoya Inn: 50 × 40 m, a Japanese inn round a glass-roofed garden courtyard, with four tatami guest rooms, a banquet hall, a tea room, a futon store, the hot-spring bath, the kitchen and a games lounge), and six everyday places: `buildSchool` (Birchwood High: 44 × 32 m, a locker-lined corridor, two classrooms, a science lab, a library, an art room, the staff room, toilets and the PE store), `buildOffice` (Larkspur & Co: 48 × 34 m, a reception, an open-plan floor of desk pods, a meeting room, the manager's office, a kitchenette, a server room, a store, a lounge and toilets), `buildMarket` (Greenleaf Market: 50 × 36 m, a 4.2 m supermarket floor of aisles, produce tables, chillers, freezer islands, a bakery, a deli and six checkouts, a café, the bakery kitchen, a stockroom with pallet racking and a forklift, the staff room, an office, toilets and a cold room), `buildHotel` (Hotel Bellamy: 48 × 36 m, a marble lobby with columns, a piano and a reception desk, a bar with a pool table, a restaurant, and off a carpeted corridor five guest rooms, each with a bathroom whose bath you can climb into, plus housekeeping and the laundry), `buildLeisure` (Riverside Leisure Centre: 50 × 38 m, a 7 m sports hall with bleachers, hoops and a climbing wall, a gym, a dance studio, the reception and café, two changing rooms with lockers and showers, first aid, the equipment store, an office and the plant room) and `buildFlats` (Maple Court: 46 × 31 m, four flats off a landing, each with a living room and kitchen, a bedroom, a children's room with bunk beds, a study, a hall and a bathroom, plus the residents' laundry and the bike and bin store; the north flats are the south ones mirrored by `flat()`). The everyday maps share painters, `everydayMats` and the `everyday` furniture (desks, office and school chairs, lockers, gondola shelving, counters, divans and bunk beds, bathroom pieces, gym kit, bleachers, trolleys, pallets). Walls, floors and ceilings come from `kit`: `wallX`/`wallZ` build a wall as two half-thick slabs, one per room, each reaching 1 cm past the centre line so the two overlap and leave no seam to slip into, each from a side spec (`w` wallpaper, `lo` and `lh` lower panelling, `h` height, `base`, `rail`, `crown`, `casing`), with door openings and their casings; `win`, `pic`, `flat` and `sconce` hang windows (an evening view), framed pictures, signs and wall lights, and `ag` places furniture with its back to a wall. Wall slabs are marked `wall`, and trims, pictures and curtains are not solid, so a hider can still stick to the wall behind them. The `Builder` helpers are `box`, `at` for turned furniture, `prop` for round things from the shared `geo()` cache (baked with the boxes into one mesh per material, so a map is about one draw call per material), `ceil` for ceilings drawn from below only so the menu's view from above looks into the rooms, `spot` for places bots hide under tables, beds and the inn's futon cupboard, and `lamp` for point lights, of which only six spread-out ones are used, three on the Fast setting; the furniture helpers are in `furn` (tables, round tables with cloths, chairs, benches, sofas, beds, a grand piano, a fireplace, plants and palms, lamps and chandeliers, washing lines, curtains you can walk into, vending machines, plinths and mannequins, which make good decoys). The bots only walk the floor: the manor's gallery and the inn's raised entrance step are for players. Each map gives `spawns`, a `seek` start and an `attract` spot for the menu's doll. Boxes marked `soft` (cloth and leaves) block the view and the seeker bot's line of sight but not the blaster's pellets. The boxes are also the colliders, the bots' navigation grid, line of sight and the surfaces the eyedropper and bots sample; they are filed in a 2 m grid (`buildGrid`, `solidsIn`, `rayMap`) so rays and collisions stay quick. Every pattern is drawn on a canvas by the `P` painters (damask and striped wallpapers, wood panelling, marble, tatami, shoji and fusuma, rugs, portraits, landscapes and modern paintings, and so on), from a fixed seed so all players get the same map. Every room must keep a ceiling. To add a map, write a `build…` function and add it to `MAPS`, then check it the way the existing ones were checked: every spawn, the seeker start and every bot `spot` free and reachable from the seeker start, a ceiling and a floor everywhere, no ray escaping the shell, and no solid furniture cut into a wall. Bodies collide as their pose's footprint circles against the boxes (`moveEnt`): each move is cut into steps shorter than the body's radius and anything it runs into pushes it back out the face it came in through (`pushOut`), so nothing can be crossed in one frame; a box whose top is within a step of the feet (`stepOf`: 0.45 m standing, half the body's height crouching or lying) is climbed only when there is room to stand on it, otherwise it is solid (a chair seat over its legs, a bed frame under its quilt); a box whose bottom is within 1 cm of the top of the head doesn't block, so a body that just fits under a table fits; low furniture is solid down to the floor unless the gap under it is at least as tall as the smallest lying body (`minGap`, the Petite Block, about 0.15 m: `Builder.finish` adds an invisible collider under anything lower); a body that finds itself inside something (after growing, say) moves to the nearest free spot that isn't across a wall (`freeSpot`, `wallBetween`), and a move never ends on the far side of a wall. The practice seeker bot "sees" by rendering a small view of you with and without you and comparing the colours (`perceive`, delta E), so better paint really does keep you hidden; it sweeps its gaze up and down so it can spot hiders high on walls, and furniture blocks its view. Practice hider bots sometimes hang on walls or tuck under furniture. Online rooms follow a daily map rotation: `dayMaps` shuffles all the maps with the day number as the seed (days start at midnight US Eastern standard time, `DAY_SHIFT`) and keeps the first three, and the map is `dayMaps(now)[floor(now / 15 min) % 3]` (`rotMap`), worked out from the shared server clock (`Net.now`). The host publishes it as `rs.map` in the lobby and when it starts a round, so a round in progress finishes on its map and the next round or the lobby moves on; the lobby shows the map now and "Next map in m:ss" with the next map's name (`rotText`), and the menu names today's three. The host of a private room can still pick a single map instead of "Daily rotation" (the `map` round setting, -1 for the rotation); Practice has a picker of every map; Quick match and invite links are unchanged. Nothing is stored online between rounds; your name, body, last paint job and settings are saved in the browser under `chroma-…`. Parties can launch it (its row in `GAMES` in `party.js`). On touch screens the page adds `touch` to `body`, and the `body.touch` rules at the end of the stylesheet place the HUD, the lobby card and the paint studio for phones held upright or sideways (keep them clear of the stick and buttons when adding HUD pieces). The lobby card and the paint studio scroll an inner box (`.lb-body`, `.st-body`) instead of the card itself, because Chrome can leave a blank patch in the 3D view under a scrolling box with a solid background. Effects (`splat`, `pellet`, `confetti`, `puff`, all under `fxRoot`) are drawn only on each player's own computer and are hidden from the practice bot's eyes (`perceive` hides `fxRoot`), so they can be restyled without changing what can be hidden against: a pellet is a short streak with a pale tail and a small flash at the blaster, the mark it leaves is one of five splat shapes made once by `splatGeo` (a wobbly blob with lobes and stray drops, in the same pink and about the same size as a plain round mark) that pops open and throws off a few drops, and a find adds a gold ring and a sparkle to the confetti. The Fast setting gets fewer drops and less confetti (`fxHigh`). On the HUD the crosshair kicks on each shot, the FOUND banner, the "Found …" text and the Still hidden number pop when they change (`hudPop`), the clock turns pink and beats in the last ten seconds of hide or seek time, and the results card rises into place; all of these are skipped when the device asks for reduced motion. Lighting, shadows, map colours and patterns, the paint palette and how paint is sampled were deliberately left alone, because in this game any change to how a surface is lit changes how well a paint job matches it. **Turkey Week** (`../events.js`, November 15 to 26, 2026): seekers wear a pilgrim hat as part of their gear; hiders never do, because a hat would give them away: one shared hat geometry and material from `events.js` (`tkHat`), one extra draw call each. The game reads the one Turkey Week switch on the Back Room page (see `events.js` below) once, when it loads (off means no hats are made); it has no button of its own. **Seeker kit** (hidden: captains only for now; `KIT_PUBLIC = false` and `kitOn()` in `index.html`, and `?seekerkit=1` turns it on for testing, on localhost or in an automated browser only): seven hats (a detective deerstalker, a safari hat, a painter's beret, a museum guard cap, a hard hat, an exterminator cap and goggles), four tools that take the blaster's place in the hand (a magnifying glass, a paint scraper, a spray bottle and a feather duster), plus, from 19 Nov, twelve more hats (8 to 19: team cap, football helmet, sweatband, referee cap, pirate hat with an eye patch, fedora, top hat, robot antenna, champion headband, hockey helmet, bowler hat, mascot ears) and eight more tools (5 to 12: foam finger, pirate hook, walking cane, whistle, tennis racket, hockey stick, spyglass, robot claw), each still one merged mesh, a paint splash when the wearer catches someone (a ring of see-through droplets that flies outward and is gone within 0.6 s, and a small SPLAT! above the caught hider's head) and a caught animation for the hider they catch (it hops and pops along its own up direction, spinning only when it was upright), all drawn by `kit.js`, which the page fetches only when this player may open the Seeker kit (a button under the body choices on the menu opens a screen with a model of you as a seeker) or someone in the room wears a kit; for everyone else it never loads and every hook (`if(CK)`) does nothing. Seekers only: hats and tools are added inside the seeker gear (`Avatar.gear` and `Avatar.gun`), which the page shows only while a body is a seeker, and the two effects start only once the host has marked a hider found, are drawn under `fxRoot` for at most 1.2 s and leave no marks, so nothing changes how a hider looks or how easy a hider is to see while hiding. Shooting is still the camera's ray, so a tool changes neither reach nor tagging (the pellet still starts at the hand), hats stay low and above the visor, and your own hat and tool are hidden while the camera is pulled in close to them. A kit is four item numbers, the `no` of Chromaflage's items in `cosmetics.js` ([hat, tool, splash, caught], 0 for none; `kit.js` keeps its lists in the same order, so never reorder them), saved as `chroma-kit` and sent in the player's own node as `kt` (`'3.1.1.0'`, only when something is worn; older pages ignore it, and `kit.js` checks every number and draws the plain gear for one it does not know). Each hat and tool is one mesh (its pieces merged with vertex colours, one geometry per item, one shared material); a dressed seeker's blaster becomes one merged mesh too and a hat replaces the Turkey Week hat, so a dressed seeker is never more draw calls than a plain one. It has Spanish: its words are in `chromaflage/es.js` (lang.js; map names and the signs inside the maps stay in English), with a Language switch in Settings and the pause menu.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/backrooms-1v1/`: Backrooms 1v1–4v4 (Back Room; the folder keeps its original `backrooms-1v1` name), a third-person build-fight shooter in Level 0 of the Backrooms (Three.js). Practice is you against the Entity, a bot that runs only in your browser. Online rooms hold up to 8 players split into Red and Blue (1v1 to 4v4, uneven teams like 4v3 are allowed and never filled with bots) in the Firebase Realtime Database under `rooms/br-…` (same project and rules as Rumble Karts; invite links go through the home page as `#br-<code>`). Quick match waits in `rooms/br-queue/players` and pairs two people into a `br-q…` room for a 1v1. Each player writes only their own node `players/<uid>`: `n`, `c`, `x`, `z`, `h` and `ts` always (the shared rules expect that shape), plus height, pitch, health, shield, weapon, a dead flag, the match `m` and round `rd` the position belongs to, the team picked in the lobby `t`, kills this match `k`, join time `j`, `mv`, the newest map version the page can build (3), and `ev`, set to 1 by a page that can edit builds; about 20 times a second during a round (15 in rooms of 5 or more) while something changed, and twice a second otherwise or while nothing has. The host is the live player who has been in the room longest (the next in line takes over when they leave; a newcomer only starts judging who the host is once the room's players and round have loaded, so a slow phone joining mid-match can't take over and reset it) and is the only one who changes `round`: `{id, ends, seed, ns, ph, pt, to, rn, sr, sb, ro, dd, rw, mw, fm, rz, mv, ed}`, where `id` is the match, `ph` the phase (`lobby`, `cd` countdown, `fight`, `over`, `end`), `pt` when the phase ends, `to` the rounds to win (3, 5 or 10, host's choice), `rn` the round, `sr`/`sb` the team scores, `ro` the roster (`uid:r;uid:b`), `dd` who is out this round, `rw`/`mw` the round and match winners, `fm` a forfeit, `rz` the host's "Randomize teams" draw (each player then moves themself), `mv` the map version for this match (see below) and `ed` whether builds can be edited in it (see Editing builds). The host writes with transactions guarded on `id`, `ph` and `rn`, and keeps `ends` at the time of writing because the rules cap how far ahead it can be. Shots, builds and deaths go through `events/<push>` (deleted after 5 s): the shooter's rays pass through teammates and the shot lists only the enemies it hit; the victim applies damage only from someone on the other team of the same match, and reports its own death naming the last shooter, which gives the kill credit. A round ends when one team has nobody left: dead, or silent for 10 s (a closed tab removes its node at once). A team with nobody left in the room (disconnected, or silent for 20 s) forfeits the match. A host whose own tab has just frozen waits a moment for the queued updates before judging anyone. Players who join mid-match wait in the lobby or watch until the next one, and dead players watch a living teammate (click, or tap Next on a touch screen, for the next one). **Find tags:** once a round's fight has run for 1 minute 30 seconds (`FIND_AT`), every 15 seconds (`FIND_EVERY`) a name tag shows over every living player for 2 seconds (`FIND_SHOW`), through walls, with how far away they are, so players who can't find each other can; it pops in white, holds in the team colour and blinks out (a plain fade with reduced motion), with a soft ping. A tag for someone off the screen is pinned to the nearest edge with an arrow showing which way to turn. Nothing is sent for this: the clock (`findClock`) is the round's own, online the server-clock time since the fight began (the round node's `pt`, which is when the countdown ended and is left alone for the whole fight phase), so every browser flashes at the same moments, and in practice the seconds fought this round (the tag is the Entity's). Between flashes nothing changes: teammates' tags show all the time, as before, and an enemy's only while in sight. From ten seconds before the first flash a small chip (`#find`, under the score; on a phone, under the health bars sideways and in the top right corner upright) counts down to the next one, and the first flash of a match adds one line to the feed. The tags are the same HTML labels as the ordinary name tags (a fixed size on screen, so readable far away and not huge up close), so they add no draw calls. An older copy of the page in the room just never shows them. **Maps:** every round is a new, randomly generated Level 0, in practice and online, on a grid of 4 m cells with walls on the cell edges and posts on the corners (`layoutV3`, map version 3). Every map is built the same way, so they all feel like the same place: a 3 × 3 room round each spawn, then rooms of 2 × 2 to 2 × 4 cells dropped at random until about two fifths of the floor is rooms, and everything else one-cell-wide (4 m) corridor. Every wall is full height: no low partitions, stray wall slabs, open halls or pillar fields (the most a room gets is one column in the middle, about one room in three). The rooms and corridor cells are joined into a tree and then given a few loops (a second or third way into some rooms, the odd extra link between corridors, most dead ends opened onto something), with three rules: never two openings side by side in one room wall, never four corridor cells open as a square (corridors stay one cell wide), and never a short piece of wall left standing on its own. Where a room opens onto something else the opening is a doorway, with a 0.8 m jamb of wall (`JAMB`) each side wherever the wall carries on past it. Any straight line of more than six cells (`V3_RUN`, 24 m) gets a wall across it near the middle, and the corridor jogs round it. The lighting is the same everywhere: about one ceiling panel in twelve is dead (the floor under it a little darker), up to three flicker on their own, and most maps have one or two banks (a room or a stretch of corridor) that flicker together and dim the walls, floor and ceiling around them; there are no tinted wallpapers, damp carpets or special rooms. Then each spawn is cleared to an open, lit 3 × 3 area, anything that can't be reached on foot is opened up, and the layout is checked for fairness (`fairV2`: both teams' spawns about as far from the middle on foot, the two halves within about a third of each other's cover, no huge detour between the teams); one that fails is redrawn (about one in four), and if sixteen in a row fail (in practice never), the closest is made point-symmetric, which is fair by construction but can leave a few open patches along the middle. Checked on 600 generated maps of every size and roster: no low partitions or free-standing wall pieces, no 3 × 3 block of cells without a wall inside it outside the spawn areas, no straight run longer than six cells, every cell reachable, the same seed always gives the same map; the longest sightline on a map is 23–53 m (v2: 42–69 m). A v3 map has more walls, so its collision boxes are joined up along each wall line (`solidsV3`: walls and jambs that touch end to end become one box, and posts in the middle of a straight run are left out), which leaves fewer boxes than a v2 map (about 210 against 310) and less work per frame for movement and shots. Size depends on the number of players and a little chance: 11–13 cells across for a 1v1 (and practice), 12–14 for 3–4 players, 14–15 for 5–6, 15–16 for 7–8. Red spawns along the west edge and Blue along the east. Online, the layout of round `rn` comes from the match's `seed` and `rn` (`roundSeed`), and its size from that and the roster size, so every player builds the same rooms without anything new being sent; a short blackout covers the change between rounds. **Older copies of the page** (still open in someone's tab after an update) only know earlier layouts, which are kept unchanged in the page and checked to build exactly the same walls, pillars and lights for the same seed: the original maze (`layoutV1`: one per match from `seed`, 12, 14 or 16 cells by room size (`ns`), Red's and Blue's spawn areas cleared), and the previous random layout (`layoutV2`, map version 2: the floor split into areas furnished in different ways (twisty corridors, small rooms, open halls with stray wall slabs, pillar fields, office cubicles behind 1.25 m grey partitions you can see and jump over, solid blocks of wall), joined by doorways or with no wall at all, each lit its own way, sometimes with a different wallpaper or a special room). Each player's node says which maps their page can build (`mv`), and the host writes into a new match the newest version every player in the room has: `mv: 3`, `mv: 2`, or no `mv`, in which case everyone, new pages included, plays the original maze for that match, so a mixed room always agrees on the walls (an older page that joins in the middle of a newer match only watches, and may see the wrong walls until the next match). A host on a v2 page writes `mv: 2` at most, and one on a v1 page never writes `mv`, so the same applies. The collision rules below hold for every layout: automated walks and jumps into walls, doorway jambs, partitions, big pillars and wall blocks, spawns for every roster shape and shots at every kind of solid, run on hundreds of generated layouts, found nothing passing through or stuck inside a wall; and scripted runs and jumps at built walls, floors and ramps from every side, at 60, 30 and 20 frames a second and thrown at up to 20 m/s, with random building and with the Entity's own code, found nothing inside or through a build. Builds can't be placed inside a solid block of wall. **Editing builds:** aim at a wall, floor or ramp your team built (in practice, one you built; never the other team's or the Entity's) from within 6 m (`EDIT_RANGE`) and press Edit (G, or the Edit touch button): the piece turns into a grid of tiles, 3 × 3 on a wall and 2 × 2 on a floor or a ramp (a ramp's are its low and high halves, left and right). **Edit on release** (a setting, on by default, in the menu under Look sensitivity and on the pause screen, saved as `br1v1-editOnRelease`; a `br1v1-editRelease` saved by the first version is still read; the Edit key itself is rebindable in Change keys like any other): hold Edit and the tile under the crosshair is picked at once, every tile the crosshair then sweeps across is picked too (no need to hold Shoot / place, though it does the same), and letting go of Edit confirms (`editHold`, `editKeyUp`, `paintTile`). The first tile of a sweep decides whether it opens or closes tiles. A quick tap of Edit (under 0.25 s, `EDIT_TAP`) that did no more than pick that first tile leaves the grid open with that tile still picked (so two taps make a one-tile window), the setting-off way: Shoot / place opens or closes the tile under the crosshair (hold and sweep for several) and Edit again confirms. A longer hold that changes nothing cancels. Either way Reload resets the piece to whole (the touch button reads Reset) and Aim cancels; on a touch screen the buttons read Tile, Done, Reset and Cancel while editing, and a finger put down on the grid picks the tiles it touches instead of turning the view (`editFingerDown`, `screenRay`), so you hold Edit with one thumb, drag the other over the tiles and let go. While you edit, the line of help names the shape so far (Door, Window, Arch, Half wall, Narrow ramp…, `edName`) and what to press next; until your first confirmed edit it spells the whole thing out (`br1v1-editHelp`). The tile under the crosshair is worked out on the grid's own plane, and a point up to a third of a tile off the edge counts as the edge tile (`edTileAt`, `EDIT_EDGE`), so the bottom row of a wall you stand against is easy to pick; when the line of sight finds none of your builds, eight lines just around it are tried (`EDIT_FAN`), so an edge or a seam up close still opens. Your own body is hidden while the grid is up, so it doesn't cover the tiles. That gives a window, a door, a half wall, an arch, a floor with a corner cut, a half-width ramp and so on. At least one tile has to stay. Nothing changes until you confirm, and then the new shape is there at once (it used to land 0.4 s later, `EDIT_T`, now gone); you can't shoot while the grid is up, and a Shoot / place still held from picking tiles doesn't fire until it's let go (`editLatch`). The build message is the same as before. A piece's shape is `ed`, one bit per tile cut out (tile (u,v) is bit `v*n+u`; 0 is the whole piece). The tiles left are joined into as few rectangles as it takes (`edRects`), and each rectangle is one ordinary solid box, or for a ramp one ordinary ramp sheet with a smaller footprint (`pieceShape`), so there is no second collision system: movement, `pushOut` / `rampOut` and shots run the same code as for a whole piece, you walk through a door and shoot through a window because no box is there, and what is left blocks from every side (tiles that come back on a body move it out, as a new build does). The picture is one mesh made of the same rectangles (`pieceGeo`), so an edited piece is still one draw call; the grid you see while editing is three, in place of the piece, and only on your screen. Checked with the game's own movement and shots against the tiles themselves: every one of the 511 wall shapes both ways round (shots straight and slanted through every tile from both sides, walks at it along its length, thrown and jumping bodies), all 15 floor shapes and all 15 ramp shapes in all four directions, at 60, 30 and 20 frames a second, and whole pieces compared number for number with the page before editing. Online an edit travels as the build message for that spot again with `ed` added, the host answers anyone who comes into a round late (`bq`) with every piece standing, its health, shape and team (`bs`), and only the team that built a piece may reshape it (a message from anyone else is ignored). A page from before editing ignores a build for a spot that is taken, so it would keep the whole piece; to keep a mixed room agreeing on the shapes, every page says in its player node whether it can edit (`ev`), and the host turns editing on for a match (`ed: 1` in the round node, written like `mv`) only if every page in the room can: otherwise the Edit key says a player needs to refresh. The Entity does not edit; when a build stops it, it shoots the part it walked into (`bump`), whatever the shape, and it sees and shoots through openings only. Keys and mouse buttons can be changed in the Controls panel (`keybinds.js`, saved as `keybinds:backrooms-1v1`; the Change keys button under Controls in the menu, and Controls on the pause screen): move, jump, shoot / place, aim (Shift and right click), the three guns, reload, the three build pieces (two keys each), Edit (G), the arrow keys that look around when the mouse can't be locked, and pause (P; Esc is fixed). Clicking to watch the next teammate stays on the left mouse button, and the mouse wheel still switches guns. On touch screens it has a move stick, drag-to-look, Fire/Place, Jump, Aim, Reload, Edit and pause buttons, with the weapon and build slots as buttons along the top; while you watch, those give way to a Next button, and the lobby, pause and results screens are plain buttons that fit a phone either way up. Each browser is trusted for its own health and hits, so it isn't cheat-proof. A test hook (`window.__br`) exists only when `navigator.webdriver` is set (its `findShift` moves the find-tag clock forward so a test need not wait a minute and a half). **How it's drawn** (the picture only: nothing here is sent to other players or changes collision, damage or movement): the scene is lit by a soft overall light plus a warm light from one side and a weak cool one from the other, so walls facing different ways, and the two sides of a character, come out in different shades. The carpet is darker than the walls, and its texture repeats once per cell with a pool of light baked into the middle, so the floor is brightest under each ceiling panel and darkest along the walls. Walls, posts, pillars and doorway jambs have a dark skirting board, which is part of the same geometry (`joinGeos`, `skirtW`), so each kind is still one instanced mesh and the collision boxes are unchanged; working ceiling panels have a soft glow round them (one more instanced mesh). A character's suit is a few meshes, each made of several boxes joined together (`parts`), and each of the three guns is one shared mesh coloured per corner (`GUN_GEO`; `GUN_TIP` is how far each muzzle is from the hands), so the detail costs no extra draw calls; every character has a round shadow on the ground under it, which shrinks during a jump and lies along a ramp. The detail stays inside the outline the plain characters had, so nobody is drawn bigger than before: the suit's body, legs and head are the same boxes, each gun is squeezed along its barrel to the length of the plain one it replaced (`gunFit`: rifle 0.78 m, shotgun 0.86 m, sniper 1.18 m), and the shadow is 1 m across, about the width of the figure (a wider one made characters read as larger). Keep to that if you add more detail. A shot shows a muzzle flash at the end of the shooter's barrel and kicks the gun back; tracers are thin glowing beams sized to stay about two pixels wide near and far, and are drawn from the end of the barrel, though the shot itself still starts at `muzzleOf` as before; where a shot lands there is a flash and three sparks. The tracers, flashes and sparks come from small pools made once (`pool`; the sparks are one instanced mesh), so shooting creates no new objects. With the browser's reduced-motion setting on, the muzzle flash, gun kick and sparks are left out. **Turkey Week** (`../events.js`, November 15 to 26, 2026): every suit wears a pilgrim hat: one shared hat geometry and material from `events.js` (`tkHat`), one extra draw call each. The game reads the one Turkey Week switch on the Back Room page (see `events.js` below) once, when it loads (off means no hats are made); it has no button of its own. **Locker** (hidden: captains only for now): `locker.js` holds 36 outfits (18 sports kits, 4 robots, 4 pirates with eye patches, 6 costume-party suits, astronaut, wizard, firefighter and knight), 19 gun skins (7 of them animated: Gold, Chrome, Neon Pulse, Frostbite, Lava, Galaxy, Rainbow Flow and Circuit Board shine, pulse, flow or twinkle on one shared clock), 6 build skins, 8 elimination effects and 8 round-win celebrations, all drawn in code; the same items, by number, are Backrooms' block in `../cosmetics.js` (`backrooms-1v1`, prefix `br`). `BR_PUBLIC = false` near the top of the page is the one switch that opens it to everyone; until then `lockerOn()` asks `../captains.js` (`BackRoomCaptains.may(false, 'locker', 'br1v1-locker')`, so `?locker=1` works on localhost or in an automated browser), and a Locker button and screen appear in the menu only for those players. `locker.js` is fetched only for them, or when someone in the room wears something; for everyone else it is never loaded, `LK` stays null and the page draws, sends and saves exactly what it did before. While the shop is off everything is free to wear and what is worn is saved as `br1v1-kit`. Online it travels in the player's own node as one optional field, `lk` (a letter per slot and the item's number, e.g. `o12g3c2`: o outfit, g gun skin, b build skin, e elimination, c celebration), checked strictly; an unknown number draws the default for that slot, and a page from before the Locker ignores the field and draws the plain suit. Looks only and fair in ranked play: every outfit is built on the plain suit's own boxes (legs, body, head, sleeves, gloves) with a pack the size of the air tank on its back, so none is drawn smaller; the main colour is the team colour online (an outfit's own colours only in practice). The test hook's `__lk.fair()` draws each outfit flat from four sides against the plain suit in both team colours: outline 100–120% (hats add height), brightness never lower, colour distance from the walls and carpet never more than 0.02 lower, no more of it close to the wall or carpet colours, at least 40% of it in team colours (the rest is bold white, black, gold, silver and bright accents, never wall yellow, carpet brown or the other team's colour). Locker items use their own shared material (`lockerMat`), which adds a little of each item's own colour as light, because the dim yellow room light turns bright colours muddy; the plain suit and plain guns keep the game's own materials. Gun skins recolour the same three guns (`GUN_SRC`, cut into stripes or patches, through the same `gunFit`, so length and shape are unchanged; the sniper's lens stays). Build skins only change the picture on a piece (opaque, the same frame and brace; each measured at least as far from the walls, carpet and team colours as plywood), and a piece takes its builder's skin (a late joiner's `bs` copy of the round draws plywood). Elimination effects play where the player falls (centred on the body), for everyone, when the player who got them wears one, and are over in 1.5 s (the falling body takes 2.2 s). Celebrations play only while the round is over and the wearer's team won it, and stop as soon as they move or shoot; they move only the picture. An outfit is fewer draw calls than the plain suit (6–7 against 12) with geometry cached per outfit and team colour; skins and effects add none except two instanced meshes while an effect plays. During Turkey Week an outfit with its own hat hides the pilgrim hat. **Spanish** (lang.js): its words are in `backrooms-1v1/es.js`, text it puts together (banners, lobby, help line, results) goes through `TX()`, HUD parts that change during play are `data-noi18n`, and only team and room keys go over the network, never words.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/drop-zone/`: Driftfall (Back Room; the folder is `drop-zone`), a third-person battle royale for up to 30 players on Gullrock Isle (Three.js; Firebase Realtime Database rooms under `rooms/dz-…`, same project and rules as Rumble Karts; invite links go through the home page as `#dz-<code>`; Quick match uses the public rooms `dz-pub-1` to `dz-pub-4`, the fullest one still boarding first; online matches are people only, at least 2). Everyone rides the Drifter (a big blue cargo plane, see The Drifter below) across the island, jumps when they like on a line that changes every match, jumps when they like (Space, or Jump on a phone; anyone still aboard drops automatically as it leaves the island), falls, opens a steerable glider (Space again, or automatically about 44 m up) and lands anywhere, on roofs and hillsides too. Then it's loot, build and fight until one is left. **Island:** 240 × 240 m, with real terrain since map version 5 (hills, a ridge, cliffs, a reservoir and a shore: see Land) (it was 320 m until map version 2: the seven places were moved closer together as whole blocks on the 4 m grid, the buildings themselves are unchanged), about 40 s' run across and 57 s corner to corner at 6 m/s, built by `buildWorld()` from a few tools; each place is built inside `grp(dx, dz, dy, …)` with its shift from the old layout and the height of its pad in `OFS`, so the place code still reads in the old coordinates: `house()` (walls with doors and windows, a floor per 4 m level and a flat roof, both sitting between the walls rather than under them, zigzag ramp stairs from `zigzag()` that need a free cell at each end, a parapet, and a trim band round the outside between levels; how each is drawn is in Buildings below), `wallRun`, `slab`, `staticRamp`, props (`crate`, `bale`, `car`, `fence`, `pine`, `oak`, `rock`, `bush`). Seven places: Haybale Farm (barn with a loft and roof stairs, farmhouse, silo), Signal Point (a 20 m lattice tower with zigzag ramps), Pinecone Plaza (a two-level mall round an atrium, shops along the north side, car park), Spillway Dam (a 12 m dam wall with stair towers at both ends, a pump house), Moonbeam Motel (two wings of rooms, a balcony reached by a ramp, an empty pool), Starfall Crater (a ring of boulders round a fallen star, two sheds) and Timber Camp (log cabins and a sawmill shed), plus sheds along the tracks. Every floor and roof is at a multiple of 4 m on the same 4 m grid as builds (each place's pad is too). All static geometry is merged into 16 chunk meshes with vertex colours (one material; on the plain look no textures except the builds', the ground's and the buildings' own (see Buildings), which are mipmapped and anisotropic, and no shadow maps), the ground is one mesh over the height grid, chests, floor loot, each build kind, tracers and particles are one instanced mesh each, so a view with 30 players in the match measured 16–55 draw calls; each player is one mesh (the merged body, plus the glider when it's out), and everyone's legs are one instanced mesh and the held items one instanced mesh per kind (`legIM`, `gunIM`, filled by `syncLimbs()` just before each render from the models' leg and item `Object3D`s, which only carry the pose), so 30 players close together cost about 36 draw calls instead of about 120 (legs and items are hidden past 70 m). Solid boxes sit in a spatial hash of 8 m cells (`hash`, `rhash` for ramps), which movement, `groundAt` and the ray walk (`raycast`, cell by cell) use instead of the whole list. **Look:** characters are built from `ROUND_BOX` (a box with rounded edges and smooth shading, added with `bagRBox`), still one merged body per player with instanced legs; hitboxes were unchanged by that (they shrank with the characters later, see Character size). The Graphics setting (`driftfall:gfx`, Pretty or Fast) and `fxWatch()` pick one of three levels, applied by `lookSync()` from `drawScene()`: 0 is the plain look above (always on touch screens and on Fast); 1 adds a surface grain to the one shared material (`MAT.onBeforeCompile` lays a drawn noise texture over every face from three sides using each mesh's own coordinates, compiled in only while `MAT.defines.FINE` is set), real shadows from the sun (one 2048 px map covering 92 m around the camera, moved in whole-texel steps by `followSun()`; everything using `MAT` and the builds cast and receive, flagged by `shadowFlags()`), a sky dome with a soft sun (hidden inside the storm, where the flat purple sky takes over) and a sea that catches the light; 2 adds a light colour grade (`grade`: the finished frame is copied to a texture and redrawn with a little more colour and contrast and darker corners; there is no bloom, because in daylight most pale surfaces are already at full brightness). Because it is a shooter the frame rate comes first: it starts at level 1, steps up to 2 only after about 6 s of frames under 12.5 ms, and steps down after 2 s over 18 ms once `autores.js` has lowered the resolution to 0.8 or less; a level it had to leave is not tried again that visit (`fxMax`). Level 1 roughly doubles the draw calls near buildings (the shadow pass draws the chunks around you again). Nothing about the look is sent to other players. **Flicker fixes (map version 2):** no two faces share a plane any more (floors and roofs used to share the walls' outer faces, partitions reached into the floor above, posts shared faces with slabs and railings, and the trim band was a solid slab over each upper floor that also hid the mall's atrium and the stair holes); the camera's near plane follows the view (0.08 m pulled in against a wall, 0.15 m on foot, 0.5 m in the air, 1 m on the Drifter) and the far plane is 800 m, so distant faces a few centimetres apart don't fight; the farm's stripes were 4 m wide to match the ground's 2 m vertex grid (they are 1 m furrows now that the ground is a picture, see Ground). Keep new boxes off each other's faces (stop a box 1–2 cm short, or let it pass through the other) rather than flush with them. **Movement, building and shooting** started as a copy of Backrooms 1v1–4v4 (the same jump, step and `moveEntity`/`pushOut`; the speed, the collision box and the ramp rules have since changed, see Collision, Character size and Pace), the same wall/floor/ramp binds (Q/Z, F/X, C/E) and pieces on the 4 m grid (up to 8 levels above the land under them; see Land for how they meet a slope), the same over-the-shoulder aim from the camera with rays from the muzzle, headshots and damage numbers. Builds cost 10 scrap (you start with 30); the Crowbar (V or 5, or an empty slot) gathers scrap from trees (14 a hit), rocks and walls (6–10), and loot has scrap bundles. **Destruction:** builds and most of the map break. Each weapon has its own structure damage `sd` per bullet (Pistol 24, Heavy Pistol 55, SMG 12, Drum SMG 15, Pump Shotgun 13 and Auto Shotgun 9 a pellet with their close-range falloff, AR 22, Heavy AR 40, Bolt Sniper 150, Scout Sniper 80; the Crowbar 50), scaled by rarity and distance like player damage. Builds have 150 (walls) or 140 health and darken in three stages. On the map, `buildWorld()` makes breakable *pieces* with `brk(health, scrap, fn)` (everything `fn` adds is one piece): each 4 m cell of a house wall per level (with its trim band and, on top, its parapet; 240), each 4 × 4 m cell of a floor or roof (200), partitions (150 a cell), trees (170), rocks (80 + 60 per metre of size), cars (260), the farm truck, crates, bales, fence sections, railings (in up to 4 m lengths), the motel's balcony, posts and sign, the sawmill's posts and roof and the log pile. The ground, the dam's wall, ribs and stair towers, the tower's posts and floors, the silo, the star, stairs and ground-level decorations never break, so the map stays playable. A piece is a range of vertices in the chunk meshes plus its boxes; breaking it squashes the vertices to a point (one small buffer update a frame, `mpFlush`, no extra draw calls) and takes its boxes out of the hash, so players, shots, builds and bots go through; the pieces left standing round a broken wall, floor or railing keep a ragged edge (see Buildings). Damage darkens a piece in three stages the same way. Boxes of pieces in a line (a wall's cells, a floor's row) are merged into one solid per unbroken stretch (`runs()`), so movement and rays check about as many boxes as before. `mpLink()` records which pieces touch and which touch the ground or something unbreakable; when a piece breaks, anything that has lost every path to the ground breaks too (a roof whose walls are gone), and chests and loot on it drop (`mpBreak`). The breaker gets twice the piece's crowbar scrap. Pieces are numbered in build order (1,185 of them), the same in every browser; a new match restores them (`resetMap`). **Loot** is decided by the match seed, so nobody has to send it: about 82% of the 40 chest spots have a chest each match (`spawnLoot`), and each floor spot has a 72% chance of an item (`FLOOR_R` odds); a chest gives a weapon (`CHEST_R` odds, better than the floor's) with its ammo, often a heal and sometimes scrap (`chestLoot`). Weapons (`WEAP`): ten, two in each of five families (`fam`: pistols, SMGs, shotguns, rifles, snipers), each with its own model, colour, sound, damage, fire rate, spread, range and falloff, magazine and reload, and ammo type (light, rifle, shells, heavy); see Weapons below; rarity (Common to Legendary, grey/green/blue/purple/gold) adds 7% damage a step and trims the reload. Heals (`HEALS`): Bandage +25 health (2.5 s), Medkit to full (6 s), Shield Fizz +25 shield (2 s), Shield Jug +50 shield (4.5 s); shield caps at 100 and you walk slowly while using one. Four slots (1–4, the mouse wheel, or tapping the slots); ammo, scrap, heals and weapons are picked up by walking over them when there's room, and G (or the touch Open/Take/Swap button) opens chests and swaps the item in your hand for one on the floor (with nothing in reach it edits the build you are aiming at, see Building and editing). Knocked-out players drop everything. **Storm** (`STORM`, `makeStorm`): the circle (180 m radius, `R0`) covers the island at first, then closes in six phases toward seeded centres, each pulled toward the middle if it would fall at sea (first close 70 s after the Drifter sets off; the last closes to nothing about 4¾ minutes in), and damage outside (health only) grows from 1 to 15 a second. The Drifter's line starts and ends 230 m either side of a point within 37 m of the middle, so it's always over the sea at both ends. The minimap (tap it, or M, for the big map with place names) shows the circle, the next one (dashed), the Drifter's line with a little plane on it, and you. **Rooms and matches:** each player writes only their own node `rooms/dz-<code>/players/<uid>`: `n, c, x, z, h, ts` always (as the shared rules expect), plus `y`, `p` (pitch), `hp`, `sh`, `st` (state: `l` lobby, `b` on the Drifter, `f` falling, `g` gliding, `a` on the ground, `d` knocked out, `o` watching), `w` (what's in hand), `m` (the match), `j` (join time), `k` (knockouts), `pl` (placing), `dm` (damage), `oc` (chests they opened this match, dot-separated) and `pk` (loot they took, comma-separated, so a late joiner or a new host sees the right island), and `ev/e0`…`ev/e23`, a ring of their recent events (each with a number `i` that only goes up, the match `m` and a time `t`): `s` shots (muzzle, tracer ends, the new health of builds hit `pd` `[key, hp]` and of map pieces hit `md` `[id, hp]`, hits on players `hs`), `b` builds and edits of them (see Building and editing), `o` chest opened, `u` item taken, `dr` item dropped, `x` knocked out (by whom, placing, the dropped items). Writes are `update()`s of only the fields that changed: about 12 a second while you're moving in a match (10 with 10+ others in the room, 8 with 20+), a heartbeat every second or two otherwise. Every node also carries `mv`, the map version (2 since the island shrank, 3 for the slower pace, 4 for the November 2026 fixes: solid ramps, and later the smaller characters, the pace and the new weapons, 5 for real terrain, 6 for edited builds; a node without it is version 1). The host is the live player on the same map version who has been in the room longest; its node also carries `ms`, the match state (`{id, ph: 'lobby'|'play'|'end', ce: countdown end, sd: seed, t0: when the Drifter sets off, ro: roster, nh: people at the start, wn: winner, we: when the results end, mv: map version}`), and during a match `br: {m: match, b: …}`, the broken map pieces as a base64 bitset with trailing zeros dropped (about 200 characters with everything broken). Players apply the shooter's reported health for builds and pieces, the lowest anyone reports, but never more than one shot of that weapon could take; a late joiner, anyone who missed an event, and the next host take the rest from `br`. The host's node also lists the builds standing (`bd`, see Building and editing), so a late joiner gets them too; there's no list of broken builds, because a broken one is just not in it. **Map versions:** the host only counts people on its own version for the lobby, the countdown and the roster, and only those elect a host; a page that sees someone on a newer version says "This match uses a newer map, reload to join." with a Reload button and sits matches out; the host's lobby says how many people have an older copy. Pages from before map versions existed don't know any of this: they're simply left out of the new pages' matches. Next time the island changes, raise `MAPV`. A match starts when the host presses Start (with 2 or more people) or 60 s after a second person joins the lobby; a lone player can't start one; the Drifter line, the storm and the loot all come from `sd`, and the clock from `t0` (server time), so everyone agrees without sending them. **Bots** are in Practice only (11 of them; online matches are people only, so no bot slots, no bot list in the host's node and no bot hand-over). They're marked BOT in tags, the feed and the results, and this browser runs them: they jump, glide to a chest, loot, fight, heal, run from the storm and now and then wall up when shot; they walk straight at things where the way is clear, round cliffs, boulders and buildings by `landPath` (see Land) and in and out of buildings by the nearest ground-floor door (`setGoal`), stepping to one side when something holds them up, shoot through breakable cover (builds, walls, trees) at someone within 35 m, break through a breakable thing they're stuck against (up to ten tries) or a line of builds they can't get round (see Building and editing), and leave loot they get stuck on alone for 25 s; they don't use stairs, so upstairs loot is for people. They're tuned to be beatable: a reaction of 1–1.6 s, loose aim (0.085–0.135 rad error, more until they've watched you a while), a little over half their weapon's fire rate and rare building; a rifle bot 20 m from someone standing still does about a quarter of the damage it used to. **Hits:** the shooter's browser decides what its shots hit (`resolveShot`) and lists hits in the shot event; the victim's browser applies them only after a sanity check (`acceptHit`: a real weapon, no more than one shot of it can do, from within 16 m of where the shooter really is, within the weapon's range, and not faster than it fires), and reports its own knockout, which credits the shooter; hits on bots go to the host. If the host leaves, the next in line carries on the same match from the last `ms` it saw, with the broken pieces it already has. Someone who joins mid-match waits in the lobby panel or watches (Watch the match), and drops in at the next one; a room holds 30 (`MAX_ROOM`). Other players and bots are drawn with `netsmooth.js`. Results: placing, knockouts, damage and time, with the room's final order when the match ends. Leaderboards hooks (online matches only, which always start with at least two people): `add('matches', 1)`, `add('wins', 1)`, `max('kills', n)` (most knockouts in a match) and `min('place', n)` (best placing; `place` is in stats.js's `LOWER`). Saved in the browser under `driftfall:` keys: `name`, `col` (your colour), `sens`, `room`; keys in `keybinds:drop-zone`. Touch: a move stick, drag-to-look, Fire (Place, Use or Hit as it fits), Jump, Aim, Reload, an Open/Take/Swap button that appears when something's in reach, an Edit button that appears when the crosshair is on a build of yours, and the Crowbar, four slots and the scrap chip as buttons along the top (tap the chip to build: the three pieces appear next to it; tap it again, or a slot, to stop); the map is top right. Practice (offline) is the same match against 11 bots with no database at all, on its own clock that stops while paused. A slow frame is played in steps of at most 1/20 s (up to a quarter of a second), since the Drifter and the storm run on the clock. **Keeping it smooth (November 2026 audit):** every shader is compiled while the page loads (`warmShaders`, with the hidden meshes shown for a moment; the storm wall's used to compile, and freeze a frame, the first time it closed in), and the minimap's island picture is drawn during the menu rather than on a match's first frame (it takes about 30 ms). Sounds have a budget (`voice`: 14 nodes per 1/20 s, then only loud ones, and your own hit, hurt and pick-up sounds always), so a big fight or a building coming down doesn't build hundreds of audio nodes in one frame. The HUD only writes a value to the page when it changes (`hudSet`), name tags reuse their vectors and only move when they move a pixel, banners restart their slide-in without forcing a layout, and the storm (`stormAt`), hitboxes, the camera and movement no longer make new objects every frame. The lobby list is only drawn while the lobby is showing (every update from anyone used to redraw it, four times a second, mid-match), player names go through the name filter once (`cleanName` keeps them), and an update with no new events doesn't sort the event ring, so taking in another player's update costs about a fifth of what it did with 30 in a room. Writes are unchanged (`update()`s of the changed fields, about 50 bytes each, 10–12 a second while moving), so it plays with pages from before the audit. Each browser is trusted for its own health and hits, so it isn't cheat-proof. A test hook (`window.__dz`) exists only when `navigator.webdriver` is set. **Turkey Week** (`../events.js`, November 15 to 26, 2026): every player's cap is a pilgrim hat, built into the same body geometry (`bodyGeo`), so no extra draw calls; the game reads the one Turkey Week switch on the Back Room page (see `events.js` below) once, when it loads, and has no button of its own. **Collision (November 2026 fix):** ramps used to be a walking surface only, so a body could walk into one from the side or from under its high end and jump up through it from below, which is how people came up through stairs, ramp builds and the floors the stairs lead to. A ramp is now also a solid slab 0.36 m thick under its surface (`rampIn`, `rampBlocked`, `rampUnder` next to `moveEntity`): its side and its underside stop a body, its underside is a ceiling for a jump, and like a box it carries a body whose footprint overlaps it at all. So the map's zigzag stairs have to be walked round to the foot of each flight (the free cell at each end is for that), and two ramps built in one cell facing opposite ways no longer make a staircase. A body on the ground follows a ramp up as it moves rather than afterwards. Something built on a body (`pushOut`) moves it out the side its middle is already on, by no more than 1.2 m, never across the piece and never into a ramp; while a body is inside something it can only move back out, and can't rise. A piece built by this browser (you, a Practice bot) isn't placed if someone here would be left inside it (a floor through the head of someone on a ramp used to pop them up through it); a ramp built on someone stands them on it. Pieces arriving from other players are always placed. Loot heights come from `groundAt`, which now counts a ramp's edge, one more reason old and new pages mustn't share a match (see map versions). **Character size (November 2026):** characters were a fifth too big for the world, so everything about them is 0.8 of what it was (`BS`): the model is 1.72 m tall with its cap instead of 2.15 m and about 0.8 m across the shoulders instead of 1.0 m (the group is scaled, so the legs, the held item and the glider shrink with it); the collision box is 0.6 m wide and 1.6 m tall (`R` 0.3, `H` 1.6; was 0.72 and 1.95); the hit areas are a head box 0.45 m wide from 1.2 to 1.67 m up and a body box 0.67 m wide up to 1.2 m (were 0.56 m, 1.5 to 2.08 m, and 0.84 m up to 1.5 m), so everyone is a smaller target by about a third in area; the eye and bot-aim heights (`EYE` 1.3, `AIM_Y` 0.9), the muzzle (`MUZ_Y` 1.1) and the name tag (2 m up) follow. The camera sits 4 m behind instead of 3.5 m and 0.7 m to the side instead of 0.78 m, so a character takes about a third of the screen's height instead of nearly half. Jump height (1.7 m), step height (0.6 m), doors, walls and builds are unchanged, so doors and windows are roomier and a jump clears relatively more than it did. **Pace:** everything that sets how fast a match feels is in one block near the top (`SPEED`, `AIR`, `BUS_V`, then `WEAP`'s `dmg` and `STORM`'s times). In November 2026 it was all slowed by a sixth to a fifth (running 6.6 to 5.4 m/s, weapon damage about a sixth lower, the storm's times 1.2 times longer so the last circle closes 5:41 in rather than 4:44); running then felt sluggish, so the movement speeds came most of the way back while damage and the storm's times stayed: running 5.4 to 6.0 m/s (bots still 0.92 of it; aiming 0.7 and healing 0.45 of it as before), steering while falling 13.5 to 15 m/s and falling 25 to 28 m/s, gliding 9.2 to 10.3 m/s forward and 5.5 to 6.1 m/s down, the Drifter 20 to 22.5 m/s (it crosses the island in about 20 s instead of 23 s). **Weapons (November 2026: ten instead of five):** each family has a quick, light one and a slow, hard-hitting one, so two finds of the same family play differently.
