Damage a shot (headshot), seconds between shots, magazine, reload: Pistol 20 (35), 0.2, 12, 1.3 s and Heavy Pistol `hc` 42 (74), 0.55, 6, 1.7 s (silver, a fat cylinder, wood grip); SMG 12 (18), 0.075, 28, 2 s and Drum SMG `ds` 15 (23), 0.11, 40, 2.6 s (yellow, a drum under it; slower but tighter, longer range, a bigger magazine); Pump Shotgun 9 pellets of 8, 0.9, 5, 2.1 s and Auto Shotgun `as` 7 pellets of 6, 0.4, 8, 2.6 s (dark red; wider spread, 32 m instead of 40 m); AR 18 (27), 0.12, 30, 1.9 s and Heavy AR `hr` 34 (54), 0.28, 18, 2.2 s (dark teal; the most accurate when aimed, 180 m); Bolt Sniper 82 (164), 1.3, 4, 2.5 s and Scout Sniper `ss` 44 (88), 0.5, 8, 2.8 s (pale slate, a small green scope, less zoom). The old five keep their ids (`pi`, `sm`, `sg`, `ar`, `sr`) and numbers; the Shotgun and Sniper are now called Pump Shotgun and Bolt Sniper. **Names (November 2026, clearer guns):** every name is "<word> <type>" with the type one of Pistol, SMG, Shotgun, AR or Sniper, so it always says what kind of gun it is (the Rifle and Heavy Rifle became AR and Heavy AR; ids, saved data and network messages are unchanged); `name` is shown everywhere (hotbar, pickup prompt, pickup line, kill feed, ammo panel) and `short`, the type alone, only in a hotbar slot too narrow for the whole name (`fitSlotName`, so on a phone a slot reads PISTOL, SMG, SHOTGUN, AR or SNIPER and the ammo panel above the slots gives the whole name of the gun in hand). The two of a family share a colour, light and dark (pistols cream and silver, SMGs orange and yellow, shotguns red and dark red, ARs teal and dark teal, snipers slate and pale slate). Chests and floor loot roll all ten (`W_ODDS`: 12, 10, 11, 9, 11, 9, 15, 11, 7, 5 in the order above), so the loot a seed gives is different from before; the new ids travel in `w` (what's in hand), in shot events and in dropped and taken items, and a page from before them would reject them, which is why the map version went to 4: old pages are kept out of new matches and told to reload. To add a weapon: a row in `WEAP` (with `fam` and `col`), its id in `WIDS` and `W_ODDS`, a model in `GUN_GEO` (with the end of its barrel, which goes into `GUN_TIP`), a rarity-band position in `RTAG`, a sound in `sfxShot`, and optionally `TR_W`, `FLASH`, `KICK`, `PUNCH`, `RECOIL` and `RELOAD_ST`; then raise `MAPV`. Bots pick and use weapons by family. `ICONS` holds a small picture of every weapon, the crowbar, the two heal models and the three build pieces, drawn once at load from the same geometry the game uses (`makeIcons`). **Aiming (November 2026):** holding Aim is different for every weapon, set by four fields in its `WEAP` row: `fov` (the zoom; the normal view is 72), `ad` and `as` (how far behind and to the side the camera sits; the normal view is 4 m and 0.7 m) and `pose` (where the gun is held while aiming, seen over the shoulder: since the models were remade it is only a small lift from the normal hold, because the arms stay in the pose the body is drawn in while a weapon is held). Pistol fov 58, Heavy Pistol 50, SMG 62, Drum SMG 56, Pump Shotgun 60, Auto Shotgun 66: these six stay over the shoulder at 3 to 3.4 m; the two ARs go to the eye (`ad` 0, your own character hidden), AR fov 46 and Heavy AR 34. **Reticles (November 2026, clearer guns):** one style per family, the same from the hip and aimed (aiming only pulls it in, because the spread is smaller and the view zoomed), drawn by `RET` into `#cross` (an inline SVG, one CSS pixel per unit, put on a whole pixel in the middle of the view, 2 px white lines over a 1 px dark edge on each side so they stay sharp and read on bright sky and in dark rooms): Pistol a dot in a thin ring; SMG a small ring with four short ticks outside it; Shotgun a wide ring broken into four arcs; AR the classic four lines and a dot (the dot turns red when aimed, like a red-dot sight); Sniper from the hip a ring with cross hairs through it; the crowbar, heals and building a dot. Rings and gaps are the real spread of the shot in pixels at the current zoom (`RET.size`: `hip` or `ads`, more while moving, from `curSpread`, the same numbers the shot uses), plus a short kick of a few pixels after each of your own shots (`retKick`, `RET_KICK`) that eases back in about a tenth of a second. The snipers aim through a real scope (`#scope`: a round lens in a black surround with a rim and a darker edge to the glass, `SCOPE` sets its size and colours; Bolt Sniper fov 16, Scout Sniper fov 30 with a wider, green-rimmed lens), both with the same plain reticle (`SNIPER_RET`): thin cross hairs 1.5 px wide at any size with an open middle and a red centre point, two small marks on each arm and thick posts from the edge, with a faint pale edge so they show in a dark room. (Before, every weapon had its own sight picture in `SIGHT` and `#sight`; both are gone.) Look sensitivity already scales with the zoom. Nothing about aiming is sent to other players, so they don't see your gun raised. **Weapon models and feel (November 2026 remake):** all ten were rebuilt to the standard of Shell Crackers' guns; only looks, motion and sound changed, so damage, fire rates, magazines, spread, ranges and everything sent to other players are as they were and the map version is still 4. Each is still one merged mesh with its colours in the corners (one draw call for everyone holding that weapon and one for all of them on the ground, as before), now 300 to 770 triangles instead of 110 to 240 (Pistol 304, Heavy Pistol 508, SMG 504, Drum SMG 536, Pump Shotgun 612, Auto Shotgun 552, AR 556, Heavy AR 536, Bolt Sniper 772, Scout Sniper 676). In the clearer-guns pass (November 2026) each was pushed towards its stereotype so the family shows at a glance: the Pistol lost its under-barrel lamp (a plain slide on a frame); the Pump Shotgun's barrel is thicker; the Auto Shotgun lost its carry handle (it read as a rifle) and got shorter and chunkier: a fat barrel with a big square brake, a low rail, a fat box magazine and a solid stock; the AR has the classic black carry handle with the rear sight on it, a tall front sight post, a round ringed handguard and a black stock; the Heavy AR has a top rail with a small boxy red-dot sight, a long square handguard with a front grip, a big curved magazine and a shorter, thicker barrel with a brake (no bipod or wood any more, which made it look like a sniper); the Bolt Sniper's bipod legs now hang down and forward instead of lying folded. The Heavy Pistol stays a revolver (its reload works the cylinder) and the SMG, Drum SMG and Scout Sniper keep their shapes. `GUN_GEO` builds them from a few kinds of part: `bx` a box (optionally tilted), `ob` a bar with its long edges cut off and shaded smooth (so a body reads as rounded plastic), `cyl`, `pin` and `peg` a cylinder along the barrel, across it or upright, and `ex` a side outline pushed out sideways with bevelled edges (the stocks); `grip` and `guard` add the grip and the trigger guard every weapon shares. In a weapon's own coordinates -z is forward and the right hand (a 0.146 cube) is centred on (0, 0, 0.12): the grip goes down through the hand, the body sits on top of it, and a stock runs back over the forearm and stops at the chest (z 0.4), so with any outfit the weapon is in the hand and nothing passes through the arm or the body (before, the hand was inside the body of the gun and the stock ran through the forearm). `HOLD` is where a weapon is held: a little outside and above the middle of the hand and turned 4° outward, so its top shows over your shoulder from behind; it also tips up and down with the aim (yours, bots' and other players', from the pitch already sent). From directly behind your own character you still only see the back half of it past your shoulder (the colour, the scope, the drum, the carry handle of the AR), because the arms hold every weapon in the pose the body is drawn in; the whole outline shows on other players, on the ground, in the hotbar and when you aim over the shoulder. `GUN_TIP[id]` is the end of the barrel: the flash, the tracer and the spent case are drawn from the weapon as it is held (`gunTip`, `gunPoint`), while the shot itself still starts at `muzzleOf` and what is sent is unchanged; looking down the rifles' sights the flash and the tracer start just under the sight, and a scope shows no flash. `animModel` adds the motion, all of it turning about the hand: `KICK[id]` (how far a shot shoves the weapon back, how far the muzzle jumps and how fast it settles; the crowbar has its own swing, `SWING`, see Body and animation), the pump of the Pump Shotgun and the bolt of the Bolt Sniper worked between shots (0.2 to 1.1 s after the shot, two sounds and the case hopping out, `gunAct`; other players' too, from their shot events), a reload motion for each kind (`RELOAD_ST`: magazine out, magazine in, rack; the Pump Shotgun a shell at a time; the Heavy Pistol's cylinder; the Bolt Sniper's bolt) with a sound at each stage, and a swing up from below when an item is taken out (`userData.sw`). Reloads show for you and for bots only, since a reload is not sent. `FLASH[id]` is the size of the muzzle flash and the length of its tongue (shotguns wide and short, rifles and snipers long, SMGs a flicker; the big ones add a dimmer glow), `TR_TL` how long each family's tracer is, `shell` the spent case (a bit from the shared pool, only within 30 m; shotgun shells are red, the Heavy Pistol keeps its cases), and `PUNCH[id]` widens your view by up to 1.7° for a moment with each of your own shots (zoom only, so the aim point does not move; `updateCamera` eases it back). With reduced motion there is no kick, action, swing, case or view punch. Sounds are still made on the spot: `sfxShot` has ten shots that differ in pitch, length and tail (`burst` can now start late and sweep its filter), and `sfxPump`, `sfxBolt`, `sfxReload` (out, in, rack, shell, open, shut), `sfxSwitch` and `sfxDry` (the click of an empty weapon with nothing left to load) are new. Floor loot is the same model, turning about its own middle (`GUN_MID`) and nearly level so the outline reads, over a glow in the rarity colour with a clearer ring; past 75 m a weapon is only its glow. The rarity band on a held weapon is a cut-edged sleeve to match the bodies (`RTAG`, drawn by `rarIM` as before). **Hotbar (November 2026):** every slot shows a picture of what is in it, taken from `ICONS` (so it is the item's real model: the ten weapons, the crowbar, a first-aid box for Bandage and Medkit, a bottle for the two shields, and the three build pieces), with the rounds loaded or the count in the top right corner, the name underneath (a weapon's whole name, or just its type where the slot is too narrow) and the rarity as a bar along the bottom (`slotIcon`, `slotLabel`). Scrap has its own chip in the hotbar (`#matsBox`: a stack of planks and the count in big figures, which jumps when it changes and turns red below the cost of a piece) instead of a small line in the health panel, and each build piece shows its cost (10) next to the same little plank, greyed and red when you can't afford it. The three build pieces are only in the hotbar while you are building (`#slots.building`): the new Build key (B, `build` in the key settings, `toggleBuild`) or a tap on the scrap chip goes in with the piece you last used and comes back out, and Q/Z, F/X and C/E still go straight to a piece as before; a slot key, the crowbar or Aim leaves build mode and the pieces go away again. **Land (November 2026, map version 5):** the island is no longer flat. `designLand()` fills a grid of heights (`TH`, one every 2 m, reaching 16 m past the 240 m square for the sea bed) and `terrainH(x, z)` reads it with the very triangles the ground is drawn with, so what you see is what you stand on. Everything asks that one function: the drawn ground (`landGeo`), walking, falling, gliding and landing (`walkH`, `groundAt`, `moveEntity`), shots, sight lines and the camera (`rayTerrain` inside `raycast`), where loot, chests, props, trees and bots stand, builds, the storm's centres, the bots' paths and both maps. The grid is made from whole-number hashes, + − × ÷ and square roots only and rounded to 1/32 m, so every browser has the same land to the last bit and loot lies at the same height for everyone. Sea level is 0. Shape (`designH`): rolling noise for the low ground; smooth mounds measured from points pushed about by noise, the highest winning where they meet (Signal Point's hill at 16 m, a ridge from it east to the top of the dam at 20 m, the plateau that holds the reservoir, the farm's tableland at 8 m, a knoll on the west shore, a hillock south of the plaza, the timber shelf at 8 m); creased, ridged noise on the high ground; the crater is a real bowl whose rim is lower at four gaps; a wandering coast with round corners and beaches, and cliffs where the high north and east meet the sea. Each place stands on a level pad (`PADS`, `LVL`: farm 8 m, Signal Point 16, plaza 4, dam yard 8, motel 4, crater floor 4, timber camp 8; the five sheds on the multiple of 4 m nearest the land under them, `SHED_Y`) that blends back into the land over 6 to 12 m, and `grp()` raises everything the place adds by its level (`OFY`), so buildings, doors, stairs and ramps are exactly as they were. Single props (`onLand`: trees, rocks, bales, crates, fence sections, the crater's boulders) stand on the land under them, sunk a little. What moved: the farm's field is smaller and lies on the slope below the yard; the dam's paved yard is shorter (the valley closes in at both ends of the wall); the reservoir is real water 9 m above the yard and narrower than its old painted rectangle; one shed moved 12 m south; trees stand in clumps; two crater boulders that sat across a shed's doors are gone; a chest against a wall keeps its loot inside the room. **Slopes:** the steepest ground a body can walk up is 45° (`SLOPE_MAX`: 1 m up per metre along). Steeper land is a cliff: it stops a body like a wall, and a body that lands on one slides down it at 7.5 m/s (`SLIDE_V`) and can't jump until it is off (`e.slide`). Gentler ground is followed up as you move and stuck to on the way down, so nobody hops downhill. Where the sea is deeper than 1 m a body stays 1 m under the surface (`WADE`), and wading waist-deep is slower (0.72 of running). The camera is pushed in by the land as by a wall and is never under it. **Surface** (`landMaterial`; it replaces the 2048 px picture of the whole island, which was a blur up close): the shader decides what each pixel of ground is from its height, its slope and a small control map drawn once at load (`paintControl`: 1024 px, 512 on Fast and on phones; red is dirt, green is paving, blue is how much darker): grass with broad paler and darker patches, sand below about 1.4 m that is wet at the waterline with a line of foam, bare rock on faces steeper than about 40°, dirt for the tracks, yards, clearings and the ploughed field, paving with its joints drawn on the 4 m grid. Fine detail comes from one tiling texture made in code (`landDetail`: 512 px, 256 on Fast; blades, grain and pebbles, rock strata and concrete in its four channels), read once every 5 m and, for broad variation, at a 47 m scale, so it is sharp at your feet and the tiling never shows from the air; where two materials meet the grain decides which wins, so a track's edge is a crisp ragged line of pebbles and tufts rather than a fade. Every corner of the ground carries a baked shade: darker in hollows and where a hill or cliff stands between it and the sun. Pretty reads the 47 m scale from the texture, adds a finer layer of detail, strata mapped from both sides on cliffs and wind in the grass (six reads a pixel, as many as the old Pretty ground, one of them filtered at a slant); Fast reads two textures a pixel (the old ground read one) and takes its broad variation from a value stored at each corner of the ground. Water (`waterGeo`): the sea and the reservoir are sheets whose colour and clearness are set at each corner from the depth under them (clear turquoise shallows, solid blue further out). Scatter (`scatterLand`): grass tufts where the ground is grass (4,400 on Pretty, 1,600 on Fast, 1,200 on phones; they shrink away between 40 and 65 m) and small stones (420, or 150), two instanced meshes, neither solid. **Cost,** at the same five views before and after: draw calls are up by one to four (Pretty 18–32 before, 20–35 after; Fast 8–22 before, 9–24 after: the ground is still one mesh, the sea and the reservoir replace the sea and its shallow ring, tufts and stones add two); triangles went from 55–84 thousand to 84–114 thousand on Pretty and from 51–80 thousand to 64–94 thousand on Fast (the ground's own 37 thousand are unchanged; the rest is tufts, stones and a few more trees); the ground's textures went from about 21 MB of graphics memory to 7 MB on a computer and from 5.3 MB to 1.8 MB on Fast and phones. Frame times could only be measured in software drawing, where they were too noisy to rank (between the same and half as long again in both looks), so try it on a real school laptop before trusting it. Nothing new runs every frame except height lookups (one grid read and a few multiplies each) and the wind's clock; a 150 m ray over the land costs about 8 millionths of a second (6 before). **Builds on slopes:** the grid stays level, at multiples of 4 m, so builds still line up with buildings and each other. A wall must show at least 1.5 m above the highest land along it, and where its foot is above the land a skirt of darker planks fills the gap down to the ground (`skirtOf`, `skirtIM`: part of the same solid, one more draw call while any exist); a floor may not be more than 0.3 m under the land at the highest point of its cell; a ramp's top must stand 0.5 m clear of the land, its foot may run into the hillside and you walk onto it where it comes out; nothing may be more than 8 levels above the land under it (`landOk`). `playerBuildSpec()` and the bots take the lowest level that passes (`landFit`), so a piece is never placed underground; on a place's pad the levels are what they always were. The rule that a piece isn't built where it would trap someone is unchanged and counts the skirt. Every browser has the same land, so everyone agrees which pieces are allowed and nothing new is sent. **Bots on the land:** `buildNav()` cuts the island into 4 m cells and closes the ones that are cliff, inside a building, or filled by a boulder, the dam's wall or the foot of a stair; when the straight line to a goal crosses a closed cell (`landClear`), `landPath()` finds a way round (A*, run only when a bot picks a goal) and hands back a few corners to walk through. Deep water costs three times as much as land and high ground a little less than low; of two places to wander to, a bot usually takes the higher. Held up by a trunk or a corner, a bot steps to one side, then the other, before giving up. In stepped bots-only matches (tests only: `__dz.step`, `STUCK_LOG`) no bot gave up on a goal. **Maps:** `buildMapBg` draws the minimap and the big map from the land itself: sea by depth, sand, grass that pales with height, rock on steep faces, tracks and paving from the control map, lit from the north-west so slopes read, and a darker line every 4 m of height. To change the land: edit `designH` or `PADS` (keep every pad a multiple of 4 m), check that no cliff cuts a place off, then raise `MAPV`. **Buildings (November 2026, still map version 5):** every building, the props round them and the light they stand in were remade, without changing what is solid. `house()` makes exactly the boxes it always did, in the same order, so the 1,185 breakable pieces keep their numbers, sizes and neighbours, and stairs, doors, loot spots and the bots' paths are where they were (checked with a fingerprint of every box, piece, ramp, loot spot and nav cell before and after; a page from before the change and a new one play in the same room and break the same pieces). What changed is what is drawn. Each building has a style (`KS`: barn, farmhouse, hut, mall, pump, motel, office, mine, cabin, shed; pass `st` to `house()`): an outer and an inner wall surface, trim, a plinth, corners (boards, log ends, steel angles or concrete pilasters), window and door frames with sills, the door (a sliding barn pair, a leaf folded back against the wall, a steel leaf with a hazard band, or none), shutters, flower boxes, a canopy or porch roof over doors, the roof edge (eaves on rafter ends, a stepped cornice, or a fascia with a neon tube), a capped parapet, the roof, floors, a floor laid on the ground indoors, ceilings and skirting. `kitWall()` adds all of that to a wall cell inside the cell's own `brk()`, so it breaks with the cell and nothing is left hanging; a building can add its own extras per wall cell (`x`), roof cell (`rx`) and floor cell (`fx`): the barn's hatch, hoist, beams and vents, the mall's name, skylights, coolers and strip lights, the hut's cooler and dish, the pump house's risers, the office's and motel's signs, the cabins' chimney breasts. The drawing tools: `kb()` (a detail box: drawn only, never solid, only the faces asked for), `wk()` (the same in a wall's own terms: along it, up, out from its face), `deco()` (any other shape, turned any way), `sign()` (raised block letters from a small dot font, `FONT`, drawn cell by cell so each letter breaks with its piece of wall), `lampPost()` and `bays()`; `FK` makes `sbox()` draw its box with a different surface on each face, and `NOVIS` makes it count toward its piece's size without being drawn (the kit draws it instead). Detail drawn while `DECO` is set, and everything from `kb()`, doesn't count toward a piece's size, because that size decides which pieces touch and hold each other up. To add detail: draw it with these inside the piece's `brk()` and never call `solid()`; keep anything that isn't solid thin, low (under about 0.4 m), flat against a wall or above head height, so nobody can hide inside something shots pass through. Anything that changes a solid box, a doorway, a stair or the number or order of pieces needs `MAPV` raised. **Surfaces** (`KIT_TEX`, `TX`): one array texture with eighteen layers, each a 2 m tile drawn in code at load (upright boards, clapboard, brick, stucco, corrugated sheet, cast concrete, shingles, logs, floor tiles, roofing felt, glass, riveted panels, straw, stone blocks, hazard stripes, and since the audit natural rock with cracks and lichen for `rock()`, bark for trunks and leaf clumps for canopies and bushes, `TX.RK`, `TX.BK`, `TX.LV`), laid on in metres from each face's own position, so a plank or a brick is the same size on every wall (128 pixels a metre; 64 on phones). A layer is a pale pattern tinted by the wall's paint (its vertex colour), except where its alpha is 0, where it keeps its own colour (mortar, nail heads, stripes). The island's chunks carry one more attribute, `dzA`: the layer, the height above the foot of the thing (the shader darkens and dirties the bottom metre or so of every wall, post and trunk) and how far indoors a face is (indoor faces are dimmer, more so on Fast, which has no shadows to do it). Each building has its own slight colour cast (`TINT`). It needs WebGL 2; without it the island keeps plain paint. One texture read a pixel on both looks, and no new passes. **Stairs, railings, props:** the map's stairs are drawn as sixteen steps over a soffit (what is walked on is still the smooth ramp); railings are panels with a cap rail and a post; cars and the farm's tractor are rounded toy vehicles with wheels, lights and bumpers inside the same solid boxes; crates are banded planks and bales are straw; the motel has loungers, a tiled pool with lane lines and a ladder and its name in raised letters with neon round it; the plaza's planters have a timber seat round the rim and its cars stand in painted bays under lamp posts (drawn only: a pole is 12 cm thick); the silo is ribbed and hooped with a ladder; the tower has white bands, cross braces above the first floor and a mast with drums, panels and a beacon; the dam is cast concrete with sluice gates, its name and lamps along the walkway; the sawmill stands on stone pads under a tin roof on rafters, with plank stacks and stumps; shards stand out of the fallen star. **Broken edges:** a wall, floor, roof or railing piece that breaks no longer leaves a clean hole: the pieces still standing round it keep a few jagged stubs of it where it joined them, and a wall that stood on the ground leaves rubble along its foot (`shardsFor`, one instanced mesh, `shardIM`, up to 1,200 stubs, a draw call only while something is broken). Stubs are drawn only; a stub goes when the piece it hangs on goes (`shardsOff`); which stubs exist depends only on what is broken, so everyone sees the same and a late joiner gets them from the broken list. **Light:** the sun is where it was (a roof, the two sunlit sides and the two shaded sides of a building are different tones); the fill light's ground colour is pale stone instead of grass green and a little stronger (0.58, ambient 0.11), so walls in shade keep their own colour instead of going olive. Shadows (Pretty) are unchanged and sit under the new eaves, canopies, sills and the balcony; the darker foot round every building on the ground was already there. **Cost,** at five views, before and after: draw calls are unchanged (16–25 on Pretty, 16–24 on Fast, not counting the shadow pass, which draws the chunks near you again); triangles went from 96–111 thousand to 121–150 thousand on Pretty and from 79–94 thousand to 99–125 thousand on Fast (Fast, as set when the page loads, and phones leave out the kit's smallest pieces, `KLITE`); the chunks' vertices went from 109 thousand to 224 thousand (about 4 MB to 11 MB of graphics memory with the new attribute); the surfaces add one texture of 5.2 MB (1.3 MB on phones). Frame times could not be measured here (software drawing), so try it on a real school laptop and a phone. Not done: a simpler version of far buildings (the island is 240 m across and already 16 meshes), filmic tone mapping and sRGB output (they would change every colour in the game, guns and characters included), ambient occlusion and bloom passes, glass in windows (windows stay open holes because shots pass through them), pitched roofs (roofs are walked on), benches and big roof clutter such as tanks (they would need new solid boxes, which needs a new map version). **Building and editing (November 2026, map version 6):** building was a copy of Backrooms 1v1–4v4's from the start (the same placing from your own cell and the way you face, the same 0.09 s build clock, so holding Place while you move or turn puts up about ten pieces a second, the same ghost and sounds), and it now has Backrooms' editing too, on Driftfall's own pieces, so there is still one build system. Also carried over: a new piece pops in from 0.82 of its size in about a ninth of a second (`piecesTick`; not with reduced motion); the ghost shows what you will get, a wall's skirt included (it used to show only the 4 m box, which floats above a slope); every piece knows who built it (`owner`: a player's id, a Practice bot's, `me` in Practice). Kept from Driftfall: 10 scrap a piece (an edit is free), the land rules and skirts, the rule that a piece isn't built where it would trap someone, the 4 m grid, health and the three shades, build mode and the hotbar. **Editing:** aim at a wall, floor or ramp you built from within 6 m (`EDIT_RANGE`) and press Edit (H, `edit` in the key settings, because G is Open / Take here and T is the chat; G does it too when nothing is in reach to open or take; on a touch screen an Edit button appears while the crosshair is on a build of yours): the piece turns into a grid of tiles, 3 × 3 on a wall and 2 × 2 on a floor or a ramp (a ramp's are its low and high halves, left and right). It works as in Backrooms (one copy of the code, see there): **Edit on release** is a setting, on by default, in the menu next to the faces switch and on the pause screen (`driftfall:editOnRelease`): hold Edit (or G when it is doing the editing), the tiles the crosshair sweeps across are picked, and letting go confirms; a quick tap leaves the grid open, and then (or with the setting off) Fire opens or closes the tile under the crosshair and Edit again confirms. Reload resets the piece to whole and Aim cancels; the touch buttons read Tile, Done, Reset and Cancel meanwhile, and a finger put down on the grid picks the tiles it touches, so on a phone you hold Edit, drag the other thumb over the tiles and let go. The line of help names the shape and spells everything out until your first confirmed edit (`driftfall:editHelp`); targeting is as generous as Backrooms' (`edTileAt`, `EDIT_FAN`), and your own body is hidden while the grid is up. At least one tile has to stay. Nothing changes until you confirm, and then the new shape is there at once (it used to land 0.4 s later); you can't shoot while the grid is up, and a Fire still held from picking tiles doesn't fire until it's let go. Driftfall has no teams, so your side is you: the only builds you can edit are the ones you placed, never another player's or a bot's. Picking a slot, the crowbar or a build piece, opening the big map, being knocked out or going more than 10 m away closes the grid and changes nothing. **Shapes:** a piece's shape is `ed`, one bit per tile cut out (tile (u,v) is bit `v*n+u`; 0 is the whole piece), with Backrooms' own `ED_N`, `edRects` and `pieceShape`: the tiles left are joined into as few rectangles as it takes, and each rectangle is one ordinary box in the spatial hash, or for a ramp one ordinary ramp sheet with a smaller footprint, so there is no second collision system: you walk through a door and shoot through a window because no box is there. For that, every ramp (the map's stairs too) is now a sheet with a footprint `ax0`…`az1` and a range `t0`…`t1` up its slope (`addRamp` gives the map's the whole cell), and `rampH`, `rampUnder`, `rayRamp` and the ceiling test in `moveEntity` read those instead of assuming the cell; a whole piece is the one box or sheet it always was. A wall's skirt is not part of the grid: under an edited wall it is a box of its own, the full width, from the land up to the wall's foot, so a door cut where the land is lower has a sill (walked over if it is under 0.6 m, jumped onto if it is more). The trap rule covers edits: tiles are not put back onto someone in this browser who can't simply be nudged clear (`wouldTrap`; you are told someone is in the way), and a door put back with you standing in it moves you out. **Drawing:** whole pieces stay in the three instanced meshes; an edited piece's rectangles are unit boxes in one more instanced mesh shared by every edited piece of every kind (`partIM`, `partsOf`; each box's window of the plank texture is a per-instance attribute, `uvw`, so the planks run on across a cut), which makes thirty edited pieces one draw call, not thirty (measured: 36 calls with thirty whole pieces in view, 34 with the same thirty edited, the three kinds' own meshes then being empty); the grid you see while editing is three more, in place of the piece, and only on your screen. **Online:** an edit travels as the build event for that spot again with `ed` and `n` added (`n` counts the piece's edits, so an older shape never replaces a newer one), and is taken only from the player who built the piece. The host's node also carries `bd: {m, s}`, the builds standing, nine characters a piece (kind and direction, x, z, level, `ed`, `n`, and the builder as a place in the roster; `buildList`), written again at most every two seconds: a late joiner gets every piece in its shape from it, and a page that missed a build or an edit catches up (`applyBuilds`). A page never removes a piece because of the list (a build the host itself missed stays missing for the host), and doesn't put back one it saw break in the last 8 s. A page from before this would keep the whole piece where a door was cut, and the two would disagree about what is solid, so the map version went to 6: older pages are kept out of new matches and told to reload, the usual way. **Bots** don't edit. They see and shoot through openings only (their sight line and their shots are the game's rays), walk through a door that is in their way, and break the part of a build that holds them, looking at head and shin height as well as chest height (`botBreak`), since an edited piece can hold a body by its top or its bottom alone. A bot that slides to and fro along a line of builds for two seconds without getting nearer its goal now breaks through it (stepping aside never came to a stop, so it never counted as stuck; that was so before editing too). **Checked** with the game's own movement and shots against the tiles themselves: on flat ground every one of the 511 wall shapes both ways round (275,940 shots straight and slanted through every tile from both sides, 14,308 walks, 840,000 moves of thrown and jumping bodies at 60, 30 and 20 frames a second), all 15 floor shapes and all 15 ramp shapes in all four directions; on slopes 14 walls with skirts of 0.5 to 3.7 m in 20 shapes each (shots at every tile and at the skirt, 1.7 million moves, doors walked through where the land allows and stopped by the sill where it doesn't), and 8 floors and 8 ramps in every shape; whole pieces compared number for number with the page before (37 pieces on flat and sloping ground, 444,000 moves and 11,100 rays, all identical). Weak or not done: the first wall on rising ground can still show as little as 1.5 m above it (the land rule from map version 5 was left alone); you still start with 30 scrap, three pieces, so a build fight needs the crowbar first; on a phone the build pieces are still behind the scrap chip; a body that another player's build lands on can still be left inside it (as before: only this browser's own builds and edits are refused). **Spanish (November 2026):** the whole game has Spanish through `../lang.js` (switch in the menu's settings and the pause menu), with Driftfall's words in `drop-zone/es.js`; the menus, lobby, results, Creative tools, Face and Locker are translated by lang.js, while the HUD, banner and kill feed are `data-noi18n` and put their words through `T()` (an exact lookup in es.js's list, cheap enough for every frame), and player names, room codes and place names are never translated, nothing translated is sent, and a weapon's rarity is written out per weapon in es.js because Spanish adjectives agree with the noun (`'Rare Pump Shotgun'`), with an extra shorter word (`'Shotgun (tight)'`) for the narrowest phone slots.
  **Locker (November 2026, captains only for now):** skins are built but players can't see them yet. Nothing about the Locker is on the page or read from storage unless `lockerOn()` says this player may see it: the account signed in is one of the site's captains (`../captains.js`, see below), or, for testing, the page is opened with `?locker=1` on localhost or in an automated browser (remembered for that browser tab only: `sessionStorage` `driftfall:locker`; `?locker=0` forgets it; on the live site the address does nothing). A captain's answer arrives a moment after the page loads and goes away if they sign out (`lockerSync` shows or hides the button and puts the saved outfit on or takes it off; an outfit saved by anyone who may not see the Locker is left alone and the Noob is worn). Everyone else wears the Noob and has only the Face button (see Noob and drawn faces, below). To make it public, change one line: `LOCKER_PUBLIC` to `true`. With it on, the menu gets an Outfits button beside the name box (the box is moved into a row with it, so nothing below moves when it appears), which opens the Locker: a grid of the skins (each card's picture is drawn once, from the skin's own geometry, by `pics`) and your character turning in front of the island (drag to turn it; it uses the game's one renderer, with the camera moved to a character standing at `SPOT` and the picture shifted into the free part of the screen by a view offset; on an upright phone the character is on top and the cards scroll underneath). The choice is saved as `driftfall:skin` (the skin's id) and worn in Practice and online; while the Locker is on, the practice bots wear skins too, so each can be seen in a match. `OUTFITS` holds Standard and 24 skins. First twelve built to the contributor's November 2026 sketches in the sketches' own colours (they don't use the player's colour): Pilgrim, Turkey Person, Noob, Feathercap, Pilot, Golden, Fishface, Army Soldier, Airline Pilot, Soccer Player (Red/Blue), Soccer Player (All Red), Clown. Then twelve that take the player's colour for the main garment or the trim: Hooded Jacket, Racing Suit, Overalls, Toy Knight, Space Suit, Raincoat, Chef, Diver, Robot, Varsity Jacket, Builder, Wizard. Bare skin (face, hands, the footballers' arms and knees) is the player's own tone, except Noob (yellow), Golden (gold) and Fishface (orange), whose colour is the costume. A skin is a different set of boxes on the same body (`fitBase` gives every one the same torso, head, arms and hands, which `bodyRig` then shapes and gives bones: see Body and animation), built by `bodyGeo(hex, skin, fit)` into the same one merged mesh with vertex colours, so there are no textures and no extra draw calls for a body: stripes, the Golden sheen and camouflage are painted square by square on rounded boxes (`bagPaint`, `bagCamo`), and shirt numbers, buttons, feathers and the tail fan are small boxes (`bagTurn` for tilted ones). Legs: the standard legs are untouched (`legIM`); skins that set `legs` are drawn by one more instanced mesh for all of them together (`fitLegIM`: one extra draw call for the whole view while anyone in view wears one, none otherwise), whose leg has the standard leg's shape cut into ten bands (`fitLegGeo`) and takes four colours, a shoe colour and a pattern per leg (`legSpec`: kit with shorts, bare knee, sock top and a hoop; two-part for boots or the turkey's orange shins; spots; camouflage), read in the shader of its own copy of the material (`FLMAT`). Triangles per character, legs included: standard 3,988 (4,192 with the pilgrim hat); skins 2,740 to 3,976 (3,328 to 4,156 with the hat), so never more than the standard (counted again after the elbows and knees: see Body and animation). Fairness: the collision box and the hit areas never look at the model, so they are identical for every skin, and every skin has the same body, height to the top of the head and pose. What differs is only the look, and where the sketches and strict sameness pull apart the sketches won: the twelve sketch skins carry no backpack, so from the side the outline of the bare-backed ones is 85 to 98% of the standard's (bare-headed ones are 6 cm shorter than the standard cap); Turkey Person's tail fan reaches about 18 cm past each shoulder (it is not a hit area); Pilgrim, Pilot, Airline Pilot and the two football kits are darker than the darkest standard colour (average brightness 0.26 to 0.34 against 0.35); Army Soldier is green camouflage, shifted a little bluer than the sketch so it is not the grass's own green, but it is still the hardest to spot on grass. The second twelve are held to the standard: as wide and deep (each has something on its back as deep as the backpack; since November 2026 everything on every back, the standard's and the Noob's packs included, is pulled in by the same amount, see Body and animation), 0 to 15% larger in outline and never smaller, at least 0.98 of its brightness, no ground-coloured parts. During an event the event's hat (`eventHat`, Turkey Week's pilgrim hat) takes the place of every skin's own headwear, helmets, caps and the clown's wig included, so everyone keeps the same outline; costume heads stay (Turkey Person's hood and beak, Fishface's head), with the hat on top. A later event's headwear (the game has no winter look yet) goes in `eventHat` and then works for every skin. Online, a skin travels as one small number in the player's own node, `sk` (its place in `OUTFITS`; left out for the Noob). A page from before the Locker ignores it and draws the standard body; a page that gets no `sk`, or one it doesn't know, draws the Noob (`fitOf`); the map version was not raised, so old and new pages still share matches. A new page draws another player's skin whether or not its own Locker is on. To add a skin: add an entry to `OUTFITS` with `id`, `name`, `body(B, c)`, optionally `hat(B, c)`, `legs` and `skin`; add only at the END and never reorder or remove one, because its place is the number that is sent (`NOOB` is found by its id, but pages already out there read the numbers). Keep it on `fitBase`, at or under 3,988 triangles with its legs (`R` is 192, `L` 48, `X` 12; legs 1,024 a pair, outfit or standard), and away from the ground's colours.
  **Locker extras (November 2026, captains only for now):** the Locker has more tabs beside Outfits: Backpacks, Emotes, Victory, Gliders, Trails, Gun skins and Builds, and sixteen more outfits (Jet Pilot, Lumberjack, Park Ranger, Scuba Diver, Head Chef, Hazmat Suit, Castle Knight, Astronaut, Farmer, Mail Carrier, Lifeguard, Ninja, Scarecrow, Skeleton, Banana Suit, Cardboard Box Robot: `OUTFITS` 25 to 40, held to the second twelve's rules: something on the back as deep as the backpack, average brightness at least the standard's, no grass greens; bodies 1,884 to 2,820 triangles plus the outfit legs). They show only while `lockerOn()` is true, like the Locker. Everything but the outfits is in `locker-extra.js` beside the page, which is fetched only when the Locker is on for this player or another player arrives wearing an extra; until then `LX` is null and every hook in `index.html` that asks it (search for `LX`) does what it always did, so for everyone else the page, its storage, its node and a frozen practice frame (pixels, draw calls, triangles) are the same as before. What a captain wears is saved as `driftfall:lx` and sent as one string in the player's own node, `lx`: a letter per slot and the item's number (`g` glider 1-24, `t` trail 1-6, `w` gun skin 1-19, `b` build skin 1-6, `p` backpack 1-8, `v` victory pose 1-4, `o` pickaxe, and `e` emote 1-6 only while one plays; each letter once, numbers 1 to 99, at most 24 characters). `lxOf` lets exactly that through and turns anything else into nothing; a page from before ignores `lx`; numbers a page does not know draw the standard look. The numbers are the `no` of Driftfall's items in `../cosmetics.js` (game `drop-zone`); never reorder `locker-extra.js`'s lists, add at the end. Gliders (24): each is one merged mesh with vertex colours like the standard one (the same one draw call), and each has its own hold (`HOLDS`: a pose from `pose()`, where each hand goes, put there by `armIK` at every steering angle, the body's pitch, the legs, how it opens, any sway or slow spin): the umbrella in one raised hand with the other arm out, popping open; balloons and the maple seed held overhead; the lawn chair and the broomstick sat on; the carpet, picnic blanket, surfboard, pizza slice, UFO and cloud stood on, arms out; the pigeon and pterodactyl carry you by the shoulders; cardboard, dragon and bat wings and the flying squirrel suit spread along your arms, lying flat. Moving parts (spinning, flapping, bobbing, rippling) are done in the gliders' own copy of the material (`GLX`, `gan` per vertex). The standard glider and its hold are untouched. Trails (6) are one pool of sprites for everyone (one draw call while there are any), only while skydiving or gliding, fading out within a few metres of any camera. Gun skins (19) repaint a weapon's body in its shader from where each point is on the model; the orange toy tip (where the muzzle flash starts), lenses, red dot and gold beads, the shape, the rarity band and the reticle are untouched; skinned weapons are drawn by one more instanced mesh per kind of weapon while any is in view. Build skins (6) are one 1024 x 512 picture read by the plywood's own shader when a piece's `bsk` is not 0 (no extra draw calls); a piece takes its builder's build skin when it is made (`bsOf`, from the builder's node), so nothing new is sent with a build; health, size and the damage darkening are unchanged, and every skin is as opaque as plywood (the glass is frosted). Backpacks (8) are built into the body's own merged mesh in place of the outfit's own pack (`pack`, `back`, `PACK_NOW` in `bodyGeo`), each keeping the player's colour somewhere. Emotes (6) play on the ground while you stand still: J on a keyboard (wave four moved it off T, which party chat takes first inside a party frame; while the Locker is on when the page loads it is the Emote row in Controls, `emote` in `KB`, so it can be rebound; otherwise J while nothing else is on J: `isEmoteKey` in `LXAPI`) or the Emote button that appears under the pause button on a touch screen; moving, jumping or shooting stops one. A victory pose plays on the winner while the match's end is shown. Both are poses on the page's own pose system (`LX.emote`, called just before `posePut`). With the Locker on, the practice bots wear extras too (`botLx`). Fairness: the hit areas, collision box, speeds and the glide never look at any of this. **Wave two (19 November 2026, still captains only):** 43 more outfits, `OUTFITS` 41 to 83: sports in the player's own colour (Goalkeeper, Basketball Player, Linebacker, Baseball Batter, Hockey Player, Tennis Pro, Boxer, Cyclist, Swim Team, Golfer, Skateboarder, Surfer, Skier, Pit Crew, Referee, Coach, Team Mascot, Fencer, Karate, Cheer Captain, Jockey), pirates with eye patches (Pirate Captain with a parrot, Pirate Deckhand, Pirate First Mate with a hook, Ghost Pirate), costume-party suits (Pinstripe Suit, The Boss, Tracksuit and Chain, Bodyguard, White Tuxedo, Business Suit, Secret Agent, Detective), robots (Android, Mech Pilot, Junkyard Bot, Vacuum Bot) and Firefighter, Rock Star, DJ, Superhero, Masked Wrestler and Samurai Armour; and in `locker-extra.js` backpacks 9 to 14 (Sports Bag, Briefcase, Robot Power Pack, Bag of Balls, Spyglass and Map, Lucky Mascot), gliders 25 to 30 with their own holds (Pirate Sail and Soccer Goal held by their struts, Giant Football on cords, Hockey Stick ridden like the broom, Robot Rotor, Briefcase Parachute), gun skins 20 to 24 (Team Stripes, Pinstripe, Treasure Map, Circuit Board, Basketball) and emotes 7 to 10 (Goal Celebration, Tip the Hat, Pirate Arr, Power Down). The outfits are built on `fitBase` with the helpers beside `bands` (`num` for shirt numbers, `patch`, `tie`, `lapels`, `shades`, `fedora`, `tricorne`, `gloves`, `hairUp`) and held to the sixteen's rules, checked with numbers for every outfit against the standard in every player colour: never more triangles than the standard (2,328 to 2,744 for the body), front and side outline 99.5 to 116% of the standard's, as tall as the standard cap, at least 0.97 of its brightness (so the suits are light cloth: grey, sky blue, white), and no more grass-coloured surface than the standard; bare arms and legs are the player's own skin tone. Shirt numbers are plain numbers with no names. A page from before draws the Noob for outfits 41 and up and the standard look for the new extras. **Wave four (19 November 2026, still captains only):** the 21 sports kits and the 8 suits were redrawn under the same numbers so they no longer look alike at a distance: each kit got a bold second colour beside the player's (neon yellow and pink keeper, gold-trimmed basketball vest with a big 12 and a tall puff of hair, gold face guard and pants, red-pinstripe batter, polka-dot cyclist, pink-trimmed tennis whites with a visor and ponytail, loud golfer, red bobble-hat skier and more), a big number on the back where it fits and new headgear; the suits each have their own colour (cream pinstripe with a red tie, a long gold coat with white fur for The Boss, an electric-blue tracksuit, an all-white bodyguard with mirror shades and a coiled earpiece cord, white tails with a red bow tie and a tall white top hat, a sky-blue business suit, a white agent with an orange harness, a rust-orange trench coat). 28 more outfits, `OUTFITS` 84 to 111 (Race Car Driver, Beach Volleyball, Rugby Player, Baseball Catcher, Sprinter, Bowler, Snowboarder, Hockey Goalie, Golden Robot, Arcade Bot, Wind-Up Robot, Pirate Queen, Pirate Lookout, Pirate Cook, The Don, Getaway Driver, Card Dealer, Winter Tracksuit, Retro Rocketeer, Star Hero, Golden Knight, Jester, Disco Star, Shark, Unicorn and Dino Onesies, Burger and Hot Dog Mascots). A new slot, pickaxes (`o`, `PICKS` in `locker-extra.js`, 12: Hockey Stick, Golf Club, Baseball Bat, Foam Cutlass, Robot Claw, Giant Foam Finger, Trophy, Guitar, Anchor, Giant Wrench, Closed Umbrella, Giant Lollipop): drawn in place of the crowbar in its own frame by one more instanced mesh per kind in use (`pickIM`, through `LX.gunIM` with the entity's `lxO`); the swing, reach, timing, damage and scrap are the crowbar's. Gliders 31 to 42 (Rainbow, Polka Dot, Paper Parasol and Bubble Umbrellas held like the first, Team Flag, Hot-Air Balloon, Robot Wings, Star Kite, Dragon Kite, Hoverboard, Toy Rocket, Paper Boat), trails 7 to 14 (Confetti, Hearts, Music Notes, Fire Sparks, Snowflakes, Gold Glitter, Team Colours, Comet Tail; two more sprites in the trail picture), gun skins 25 to 37 (Tiger Stripes, Polka Dots, Race Check, Watermelon, Denim, Comic Pop, Sunset, Gold Chevrons, and moving ones on `uT`: Ocean Waves, Rainbow Flow, Heartbeat, Electric, Molten Gold), backpacks 15 to 22 (Giant Soccer Ball, Pirate Barrel, Boombox, Robot Buddy, Ice Cream Cone, Trophy Cup, Rocket Pack, Penguin Plush) and emotes 11 to 22 (Slam Dunk, Touchdown Spike, Home Run, Chicken Dance, Arm Wave, Salute, Star Jump, Pirate Jig, Big Shrug, Mic Drop, Thinking, Air Guitar). Every changed and new outfit was measured against the standard look in every player colour (triangles, outline from the front and side, brightness, ground colours) with no event on; with Turkey Week's hat several sit a little over the standard's triangles or just under its outline, as wave two's did. In `cosmetics.js` Driftfall has a new slot `pick` and its 246 items.
  **Noob and drawn faces (November 2026):** everyone starts as the Noob (`NOOB`, its place in `OUTFITS`): players, the practice bots and anyone whose node has no `sk`. Players without the Locker can't see or pick anything else, and the outfit's name is never shown to them. So that players can still be told apart in a fight, the Noob carries the player's own colour where it shows from every side: a backpack as deep as the standard one, its straps (front, back and over the shoulders) and the belt (the colour is the same one as the dot beside the player's name in the lobby). Name tags and the minimap are unchanged (tags show names only; the minimap shows only you or the player you watch), so nothing there disagrees with the look. The menu has a Face button beside the name box for everyone (a captain's Outfits button goes after it), which opens the Face screen (`Face`): a grid 12 squares wide and 10 high to draw on in black only, with a pencil, an eraser, undo, clear and six starter faces to stamp (`FACE_STARTS`), and your character's head beside it on the island (the Locker's stage, `STAGE`; drag to turn it). Mouse (the right button rubs out), finger (drag draws a line, the page does not scroll) or keyboard (arrows, Space or Enter fills or empties a square, P pencil, E eraser, Z undo, Escape is Done). The grid's left is the face's left as you look at it. The drawing takes the place of the outfit's own eyes and mouth (an empty grid is the outfit's own face); for a captain it goes on every outfit, on the face or on the front of the headwear (each outfit's `face` or `hatFace` spot says where; Racing Suit's dark visor opens to the face, Toy Knight's eye slit and Robot's eyes go, Diver wears a full-face mask with the drawing on its glass, Wizard's beard is trimmed so a mouth shows). Turkey Person's beak and wattle stand in front of the middle of the lower part of the grid (20 of the 120 squares can't be seen from straight in front). In the world the black squares are flat quads a centimetre in front of the face, added to the body's own merged mesh (`bagFace`): vertex colours, no texture and no extra draw call. Squares side by side are one strip, cut every four squares so it follows the curve of the head: at most 120 triangles more per character (a checkerboard), 60 for a fully black grid, usually 10 to 40. The Noob is much cheaper than the standard look (2,872 triangles with legs against 3,988), so a frozen practice frame with 12 characters has about 13,000 fewer triangles than the standard look would and the same draw calls. A body is rebuilt only when its colour, outfit or face changes, and another player's new face is taken at most once every 3 seconds. Saved as `driftfall:face` and sent in the player's own node as `fc`, only when there is a drawing: 20 characters from `A-Z a-z 0-9 - _`, six squares to a character, row by row from the top left. `faceOf` lets through exactly that and nothing else (anything else, and the empty drawing, is the outfit's own face). A page from before this ignores `fc`, and since a Noob sends no `sk` it draws new players in its standard look in their own colour; a new page draws an old page's player as a Noob in their colour with the default eyes. The bots are Noobs wearing the starter faces, picked by their number (with the Locker on they wear the outfits instead); a player's drawing is never put on a bot. Each viewer has a switch, "Show other players' drawn faces" (on the menu and the pause screen; saved as `driftfall:faces`, `0` when off): off, every other player has their outfit's own face on that screen. Nobody checks the drawings: the grid is coarse and black only, but someone can still draw something rude, and a viewer can only hide everyone's drawings, not one player's.
  **Body and animation (November 2026; elbows, knees and the new moves later that month):** the body has a shape, elbows and knees, still one draw call a body. **Shape:** `bodyRig` takes whatever an outfit drew on `fitBase` and narrows the waist and widens the chest toward the shoulders (`BODY_W`, a width factor at each height: 0.76 at the waist, 0.99 at the belt, 1.06 at the chest), and presses the top of the chest down, most near the neck (`SQ_Y`, `sqK`), so the shoulders slope to the neck. The neck, head and hair (drawn while `BW` is 1), the headwear and anything in front of the face are left alone, so drawn faces, hats, Turkey Week's hat and the height don't change, and every outfit's coats, straps, vests and belts follow the same body without any change to the outfits themselves. Anything an outfit puts on its back (packs, tanks, shields, the cape, the tail fan) is pulled in toward the body by about a quarter (`BACK_Z`, `BACK_K`: a standard-depth pack now ends 6 cm nearer the back), so outfits sit close to the body. `fitBase` draws the arms with `armsOf`: each sleeve is a rounded box cut into eight along its length (`ROUND_ARM`) so the elbow bends smoothly, and each hand is a mitten with a thumb on the inside; since the audit `bodyRig` stretches every arm 1.3 times along its own line (`ARM_K`; `ARM_H0` is where the hands are drawn, `ARM_H` where they end up, and the bones, the elbow, the crowbar's default grip `HAND0` and held heals follow `ARM_H`), so whatever an outfit draws on an arm lengthens with it and the hand and cuff move out unchanged in shape; the neck is a little longer. Hair is `hairOf`: a shell over the back of the head and the sides down to the ears, and for the two footballers (`hair: 'top'` in `fitBase`'s options) the crown and a short fringe as well (not under Turkey Week's hat); every piece hugs the head instead of standing off it as a slab. Legs are shaped (`LEG_TX`, `LEG_TZ`: a full thigh, a narrower knee, a calf and a slim ankle) and the standard leg is now the same ten-band shape as the outfit leg (`LEG_GEO` is made from `fitLegGeo`'s shape with its colours baked in). **Bones:** the body is a `THREE.SkinnedMesh` with six bones (hips, chest, left and right upper arm about the shoulder `ARM_P`, left and right forearm about the elbow `ARM_E`, halfway to the middle of the hand `ARM_H`) and its own copy of the material, `BMAT` (the same shader with skinning on, shadows and Pretty grain included). `bodyRig` gives each corner its bones: arm parts (marked `BW` 2 by `armsOf`, or centred on an arm's line at shoulder height, `ARM_SEG`, like cuffs and bands) follow the upper arm or the forearm by how far along the arm they are, easing from one to the other round the elbow; the rest blends from the hips to the chest between 0.96 and 1.12, so the chest turns over the hips without a seam. The elbow is a hinge (`ARM_AX`). **Knees:** the legs are still one instanced mesh each (`legIM`, and `fitLegIM` for outfit legs), with one more number per leg, `legK`, the knee's bend; the vertex shader (`kneeVS`, in the legs' materials `LMAT` and `FLMAT` and in the shadow pass's `LDEPTH`) turns the part below the knee (`KNEE_Y`) back about the knee, easing in over a few centimetres. `syncLimbs` copies each leg's `k` into it. **Poses** (`POSES`, built by `pose()`, set by `posePut`) are twenty numbers: the chest's lean, turn and roll, each upper arm's turn about its shoulder (`ARM_P`), a slide outward, each elbow's bend, the crowbar's pitch and turn and where the right hand holds it, and how much each hand reaches for a handle. 0 is the pose the body is drawn in (both arms forward on a weapon), which every weapon still uses, so held weapons, aiming, reloads, the pump and the bolt are exactly where they were and the hands stay on every grip; `heldFollow` carries a weapon along with the hips, chest, upper arm and forearm. **Reaching** (`armIK`): a two-bone solve turns an upper arm and forearm so the middle of the hand is at a point, with the elbow toward a hint (`GLIDE_POLE`, `CB_POLE`, `BAR_POLE`), and blends it in; it puts both hands on the glider's handles at every steering angle, the right hand where the crowbar pose holds it, and the left hand on the crowbar's handle during a swing. `animModel` blends toward the pose for what someone is doing over about a fifth of a second. **Glider** (`GLIDER_GEO`, its origin between the handles at `GLIDE_Y`, now 1.76 so bent arms reach it; the wing stayed where it was, `GLU`): the left hand grips the left handle and the right hand the right one, arms up and bent at the elbow, elbows out. Since later in November 2026 it is a skinned mesh (`BMAT`, still one draw call) with a bone for each side panel, hinged at `GL_HX`, `GL_HY` (`gliderSkin` weights any glider shape by where its corners are, so a new glider drawn the same way bends the same way; a glider geometry without skin weights gets them the first time it is used): it opens on a spring that overshoots and settles (`u.gO`, `u.gV`; it snaps open past its size, the side panels unfolding from hanging down beside the struts, with a rising whoosh and a snap as it fills, `gliderOut`), the panels flex in the air and load up in a turn (the outside lifts, the inside drops), it banks with the body (0.55 of its roll on top), and it folds away fast and without a bounce as you land while the arms come down (the hands follow the handles all the way); the body hangs 0.3 rad forward under it with the legs trailing, bent a little, kicking slowly, and swinging like a pendulum out of a turn behind the bank (`u.ls`), and leans into turns, worked out from how fast the heading turns and how fast it slides sideways. **Leaving the Drifter and the skydive:** a jump pose (upright, arms up, one knee up) blends over about half a second into the skydive: belly to the ground (1.38 rad), upper arms out to the sides with the forearms bent up toward the head, legs apart and bent at the knees, a small shake in the arms, elbows and legs from the air (not with reduced motion), and a bank of up to 0.35 rad when steering; looking well down (the pitch everyone already sends) tips it head-first into a dive with the arms swept back along the body and the legs straight and together (`POSES.dive`, `u.dv`; only the look changes, the fall is the same speed), and widens your view a little; the glider opening blends out of it. **Flutter:** in the air the back of the clothes, the hair and whatever is worn on the back ripple (`flutVS` in `BMAT`'s vertex shader: which parts is decided from where a corner sits on the body as drawn, behind the line of the shoulders and more on the back of the head, so every outfit flutters unchanged; how hard is the distance of a seventh, unweighted bone from the hips, so nothing extra is sent to the GPU per body; hardest in a dive, gently under the glider, none on the ground or with reduced motion). **Wind** (`windFx`, `windIM`): while the one the camera follows is falling, thin streaks rush past them the way the air goes: one instanced mesh with a fixed budget (24 streaks; 12 on Fast; 8 on a touch screen; none with reduced motion), each fading in and out, drawn added to the picture; one draw call while falling, none otherwise. A soft rushing sound (`windHum`, a looped noise like the storm's) is loudest while you fall and quieter while you glide; in a dive it is louder and higher and the streaks run longer and brighter. **Crowbar** (`SWING`, remade later in November 2026 for weight; keyed in seconds from the press, with an easing per stretch, and joined by a Catmull-Rom curve, `crom`, so the bar's head travels in arcs rather than straight lines between keys): carried with the right hand up by the chest and the bar standing up and back over the right shoulder, turned out so its head shows beside the head from behind, the left arm hanging. A swing: anticipation, both hands bringing the bar up over the right shoulder and cocking it back behind the head (0.08 s, chest turned right and leaning back), a hang cocked a little further (0.10 s), the strike speeding up through straight overhead (0.12 s) to contact in front at chest height (0.14 s, `SWING_HIT`, unchanged), follow-through down and across to the left with the chest bent over (0.27 s), a small rebound (0.33 s), up outside the right arm (0.39 s) and back to the carry by 0.45 s. The hips turn with the chest, and the body dips and the knees give at contact. Each swing starts from whatever pose was showing (`u.sf`), so swings that cut into each other don't jump, and one that ends or is cut short blends back over `SWING_REL` (0.16 s) from where it stopped; with the button held (or, for others, swings less than 0.75 s apart) the recovery goes to a half-cocked pose instead (`SWING_CHAIN`), so held swings run into each other. On a hit the swing holds at contact for `HIT_STOP` (65 ms; `e.swHit`), the bar's head leaves a pale streak for a tenth of a second (short arcs about the shoulder in the tracers' mesh, close to the camera only), your own camera is nudged in and down with a slight roll and settles over a fifth of a second (`shakeAt`, in place of the old random shake), and what was hit decides the effect (`cbImpact`, `CB_FX`, `hitMat`): every map piece now knows what it is made of (`mt`, set while the island is built from `MATK`: trees and fences wood, rocks stone, cars and railings metal, crates, hay bales; buildings by their style, `HOUSE_MT`: barn, farmhouse, cabin and shed wood, hut and mine metal, the rest stone), builds are wood and the ground dirt; wood throws splinters and sounds a dry tok, stone grey chips with dust and a hard chink, metal fast hot sparks and a ringing clang (inharmonic partials), a crate a hollow bonk, hay and dirt a soft thud, a body a thump (`sfxCb`, each a few percent different every time). Every swing has a whoosh timed to the strike (`sfxSwoosh`, which replaced the old whack). Nothing about the hit changed: the damage still lands at the press, the spark, the sound and the nudge at contact as before. Other players' crowbar events carry no hit point, so where their bar lands is worked out here from where they stand and look (one ray, within 40 m), only for the effect; their swings hold on a hit too. With reduced motion there is no swing, streak, nudge or hit-stop (the arms still take each pose). **Running and walking:** a stride on the ground is as long as the ground it covers (`LEG_L`, hip to sole; the swing grows with the square root of the speed), and each leg's half-cycle on the ground moves its foot back at a steady pace (the angle whose sine runs evenly, measured from straight down so the forward lean doesn't tip it), so a planted foot stays put: measured on the sole with the knee bend, it drifts 0.12, 0.23 and 0.38 m/s on average at 2, 4 and 6 m/s, 0.32 m/s strafing at 5 m/s and 0.26 m/s backing up at 4 m/s (before, the leg swung as a sine, so the planted foot slid to and fro by up to about the body's own speed, though it averaged out over a step); the legs swing the way the body moves (forward, backward or sideways, so strafing and backing up step the right way), the knee of the leg moving with the body bends to lift its foot (more the faster), the body drops by as much as the straight standing leg would lift it so both feet stay on the ground (which is also the bob), and leans forward a little more the faster it runs; the hips turn with the legs and the chest turns back against them; with the crowbar out the left arm swings from the shoulder against the legs with the elbow bent and the bar arm moves a little; holding anything else the arms stay on it and only the body moves (its lean is kept small so the weapon barely dips). Touching down from the glider or the skydive is a crouch of 0.36 s with a ring of dust from the feet and a thump (`touchDown`, heavier straight from the skydive); landing on foot from a jump or a drop bends the knees by how hard it was (`u.landA`, from the fastest fall since leaving the ground), and your view dips with the knees. On foot in the air (a jump or a drop) one knee comes up on the way up and both legs reach down on the way down (for others only a real jump counts, since their height is smoothed; and others now count as on the ground, for drawing only, when they are within 15 cm of the ground or ramp under them, so someone walking up a ramp or a slope is drawn walking rather than with trailing legs as before). The body leans forward into a speed-up and back out of a stop (`u.acc`), and into turns while running; turning on the spot takes small sideways steps instead of sliding the feet round (`u.tip_`). **Holding a weapon (`GHOLD`):** weapons are now held by the hands (armIK), not by the straight arms the body is drawn with: rifle-type weapons (SMGs, shotguns, ARs, snipers) two-handed at the right shoulder (since the audit later in November 2026: `h` 0.44, 1.42, -0.28, a little outside the shoulder so the back half of the gun shows past it from behind instead of hiding behind the head), the chest bladed 0.7 rad to the right and the left shoulder slid in 0.22 so the left hand really reaches the front of the gun, 0.3 in front of the grip (`f`; the SMGs by the magazine, `ff`), measured from the bones at 0 cm off on all eight long guns at every pitch (before, it stopped 12 to 13 cm short), and goes to the magazine during a reload; pistols in the right hand alone, the left arm hanging and swinging when running, and (since the audit) the left hand comes up under the grip while you aim one (`GHOLD.one.PA`; yours only, since aiming isn't sent). The weapon goes with the hips rather than the chest, so the chest's sway doesn't tip it, and aiming lifts it toward the eye line. The weapon itself is where `gunTip`/`gunPoint` say, so flashes, tracers and cases follow it; shots still start at `muzzleOf`. Heals (`HHOLD`, since the audit) are held in both hands in front of the chest, a hand on each side of the box or bottle, elbows bent, and lifted toward the face with a small rocking while one is being used (no rocking with reduced motion); before, they sat on the straight arms the body is drawn with. The glider and the skydive hide whatever is in the hand; it comes back as the glider folds away. Past 70 m a character's arms keep their last pose. **Effects (November 2026, with the swing):** the hit marker pops in large and settles (gold and bigger for a headshot), a knockout of yours gets a big red one that holds a moment and its own two-note sound (`hitmark(head, kill)`, `sfxKO`; Web Animations, so no layout); damage numbers pop in (more for a headshot) and rise on an arc that slows; a knockout is a bigger burst (a white star in a wider flash of the player's colour, confetti up and a flat ring of it, pale puffs, and a slow column of gold sparkles), and the one knocked out topples with a little hop, overshooting a touch, then after a second shrinks away into a puff (reduced motion: they topple and stay until they go, as before); the big guns (shotguns, snipers, Heavy Pistol, Heavy AR) leave a wisp of smoke at the muzzle, and every muzzle flash is a little different in size and length, the long ones with a second, smaller burst further out; out in the storm the edges of the view go a cold violet that slowly breathes (`#stormv`, from the same `stormK` that turns the sky slate). Fast and phones throw fewer bits; reduced motion drops the pops, sparkles and smoke. **Cost:** In a frozen practice frame with 12 Noobs: draw calls unchanged at 34 on a laptop (characters alone 15); one more on an upright phone (25 to 27, characters alone 12 to 13) and on a sideways phone (34 to 35), which looks like a character at the edge of the picture no longer being culled now that its bounds grew with the hands and hair (not checked further); triangles 166,086 to 168,102 on a laptop. Per character, legs included: the Noob 2,704 to 2,872 (the eight-segment sleeves and the thumbs, +168); every outfit +168 for the arms and, for the ones on the standard legs, +256 because the standard leg is now the ten-band shape (1,024 a pair, as the outfit legs always were); the hair is up to 144 more. Standard 3,988 (4,192 with the pilgrim hat); outfits 2,740 to 3,976 (3,328 to 4,156 with the hat), still never more than the standard. Wind: one draw call while the camera's character falls (24 thin boxes, 288 triangles), none otherwise. `animModel`, best of seven runs of 300 frames for 12 characters on this (busy, software-drawing) machine: 1.0 to 1.3 millionths of a second a character before, 2.2 to 3.8 after (idle 2.5, running with the crowbar 3.3, with a rifle 3.8, swinging 3.4, falling 2.2, gliding 3.5), so about 0.1 ms a frame with 30 players in view; each body uploads six bone matrices a frame instead of four; building a body takes about 1 ms more. Frame times could not be measured here (software drawing). Hit areas and the collision box are unchanged and still never look at the model (`hitboxes` reads only the position). Weak: the head is still the big toy head and the torso one box shaped by `bodyRig`, so the body is more like a person only in its joints, hands, neck, legs and the way it moves: height, hats, drawn faces and the hit areas all hang on the old proportions; the arms are short next to the head, so the wind-up can only lift the bar beside the head and cock it behind the shoulder (it is above the head only on the way over, at about 0.115 s); hands are mittens with a thumb, no fingers, and do not turn to the handle (no wrist); the elbow is a fixed hinge; held weapons still use the straight-armed pose the body is drawn in (bending those elbows would move every grip); the clothes don't flutter in the skydive (only the limbs shake, the body is one rigid mesh); wind streaks are only drawn round the one the camera follows; the spark and whack of a crowbar hit now come 40 ms later after the press than before (140 ms instead of 100), the damage itself still at the press; packs and other things on the back were pulled in for every outfit, the Noob included, so the side outline is a little smaller for everyone; a planted foot still drifts a little (above) and sinks up to about 5 cm at the ends of a long stride; the very first shadow drawn on Pretty may compile the legs' and the skinned body's shadow shaders on the spot. **Cost of the later remake (swing, glider, holds, flutter, effects):** in a fixed frame with 12 characters in view, draw calls are unchanged standing, holding rifles, swinging, gliding and falling (upright phone 27, 28, 27, 37 and 26 before and after; on a laptop one piece of scenery differed between the two runs in every state alike; the characters alone 15, 13 and 14 at the three sizes as before); `animModel`, best of seven runs of 300 frames for 12 characters on a busy software-drawing machine, per character: running 2.9 to 3.2 millionths of a second, holding a rifle 2.2 to 3.9 (two reaches a frame), swinging 3.3 to 5.0, gliding 3.0 to 3.7, falling 2.3 to 2.6, so at most about 0.15 ms a frame with 30 players in view; an open glider uploads seven bone matrices; the flutter is one bone read and a few sums per body corner in the vertex shader, and only moves anything in the air. **Weak (later remake):** the arms were short next to the toy head and body, so the left hand couldn't reach the front of a long weapon and a rifle hid behind the head (both fixed by the audit, see below, though from right behind you still see a long gun mostly end-on); pistols stayed one-handed even when aiming (two-handed while you aim since the audit); the wind-up is only 80 ms (contact stays at 140 ms so nothing about the hit's timing changed), so it reads best in held swings, where the recovery goes half-way back up; others' crowbar chips, sound and hit-stop come from a ray worked out here and may show a different spot or material from what they really hit; the flutter is a ripple on the back half of the body, not cloth, and packs ripple a little too; the dive is only a look (the fall is the same speed); dust, chips and splashes are small lit boxes from the shared pool; the storm wall itself is unchanged (only the violet edge of the view is new); none of the new sounds (material hits, the whoosh, the glider opening, landing, the knockout) has been heard by a person yet.
  **The Drifter (November 2026):** the bus everyone rides is a chunky four-engined cargo plane of our own design (`Plane`, built in code like everything else; it replaced a yellow airship), painted in three blues with a pale belly: ASCODING in big white stencil letters low on both sides (below the line of the engines, so the nacelles never cover it from the side) and, split as ASCO and DING, on top of the wings to read from the riding camera and under them to read from below; a shark mouth on the nose, a lightning bolt, a star, two spray-painted tags (ZOOM and AS11) with drips, the number 11 and GULLROCK on the fin, panel lines with rivets, airflow streaks and soot over the wings and nacelles. No real aircraft's markings, roundels, serials or school logos: those belong to their owners. It is two draw calls (the plane, about 4,700 triangles, one material with vertex colours and one drawn 2048 × 1024 paint texture, 1024 × 512 on phones and Fast; and one see-through, two-sided mesh of about 290 triangles for a faint blur disc in front of each prop, the engine haze, the wing-tip trails and the glow of the lights), up from one (the airship, about 340 triangles); the props spin (thin blades plus the blur disc, its bands still with reduced motion) and the lights blink in the shaders (`plGlow`: red and green on the wing tips, white strobes on the tail, red beacons top and bottom, steady with reduced motion), so a frame only moves it. It flies the same line at the same speed and height as before (`busPos`, `BUS_Y`, `BUS_V` unchanged), placed so the lip of its open rear ramp (`PLANE_LIP`) is on that line, so people drop off the end of the ramp; it banks and bobs gently from the match clock, flies in from before the start and on out past the end, and nothing about it is sent, so every page draws it in the same place. The jump light over the ramp is red until you can jump and green while you can ("Red light · green over the island in 5", "Green light · Space to jump"); the match opens with WHEELS UP. While riding, the camera sits behind, above and well to the right of the tail, so the side lettering and the wing tops are in view (straight behind and further back on a phone held upright) and still turns with the mouse or a drag. The engines' drone (`planeDrone`: two slightly mistuned low notes, a buzz and rushing air through one filter) is full while you ride and fades and dulls with the camera's distance from the plane.
  **Audit (November 2026, later):** a pass over everything a player sees, from five practice matches played by a script at laptop and phone sizes (drop to knockout, two watched to the end) and film strips of every hold. Fixed, worst first: (1) aiming didn't zoom at all, the menu's 60° view stayed on in a match and each shot's view punch added up and never eased back (66.6° after 30 SMG shots), because a comment in `updateCamera` had swallowed the line that eases the field of view (now: AR aimed 46°, Bolt Sniper 16°, back to 72° after a burst); (2) your long gun hid behind your head and the left hand stopped short of its front (longer arms, `ARM_K`, and the new two-handed hold, see Holding a weapon); (3) Practice bots shivered while they fought, their drawn body turning about 24° a frame because their aim error is re-rolled every frame: what is drawn now turns toward the aim over about a tenth of a second (`u.ey`, `u.ep` in `animModel`, 1.8° a frame; their shots are unchanged); (4) a bot whose guns were all empty stood next to its target for good with the empty one in hand: `botPickWeapon` now puts it away so the bot swings the crowbar; (5) the build ghost never turned red (another comment had swallowed its colour line); (6) chests were brown boxes much like the crates: a chest is now a planked chest on a plinth with gold corner caps, a rounded lid with gold straps and a lock plate, the open one with its lid thrown back over gold light (`chestGeos`), and unopened chests glow gold underneath (in the loot glows' instanced mesh, `drawItems`; no pulsing with reduced motion); (7) rocks, trunks and canopies were flat paint: three more surface layers (see Surfaces); (8) heals sat on stiff straight arms (`HHOLD`); (9) on a phone held sideways the menu's Quick match and Practice, the lobby's Start match and the end screen's Play again were below the fold (the menu is two columns there, the lobby's title one line, the end screen's buttons come before the results); (10) the phone's kill feed cut off weapon names (it names the type there, AR, SMG, Shotgun…, and is wider); (11) the JUMP!
