A test hook (`window.__ph`, with `advance(seconds)` to run the game without drawing) exists only when `navigator.webdriver` is set. **Where players may stand:** the `kit` helpers tag what they build (`kind`: `floor`, `wall`, `fence`, `ceil`; everything else from `box` is furniture, `fix`), and `standRules` (run by `Builder.finish`) turns that into rules every map gets for free: each ceiling is an indoor area whose height limit is the top of its tallest furniture or object base plus 0.12 m (outdoors: 0.3 m under the lowest roof); tops of walls, fences and ceilings, anything above its area's limit, and other players are caps (`b.cap`): `groundAt` never stands you on one and `pushOut` slides you off it, as if it were a wall. You also can't stand where your head would be in the ceiling, ceilings only stop heads (they never push sideways), a long form can't straddle a thin wall (`spineHit`), movement is split into steps shorter than your radius (so a small prop on a slow laptop can't pass through a wall), and a form change (`tfCheck`, used by you and the bots) is refused ("No room to become that here") if it would move you through anything, up onto something higher, or out of bounds. **Out of bounds** (`oobAt`: off the map's floor, under it, on a cap, inside a wall, or more than 0.4 m above the height limit (`high`: a jump from the highest allowed spot is there for under half a second, so your own warning waits 0.6 s and other screens 1 s); the hunters' waiting room only counts for hunters while the props hide): on your own screen a one-line warning counts down from 3 and then puts you back where you last stood; everyone else draws (and can shoot) a player whose drawn position is out of bounds at the nearest walkable floor (`nearestAllowed`); and the host takes out anyone out of bounds for 5 s of a hunt, recording it in the round state's `ob` (kept by `sanitizeRS`) so the feed says "was out of bounds". Older pages ignore `ob` and show such an elimination as "Someone found …"; they don't warn their own player, but are drawn and timed out by newer pages like anyone else. Before these rules players got onto the Willow Lane roofs (up the shed's shelves, or onto a tall disguised player beside the house) and, as a small prop on high furniture next to an outside wall, up past the wall onto the roof (Willow Lane and Brightside Office). For whoever adds the next map, the reachability search used to check this asserts: from every spawn, walking, jumping (running, standing, straight up then over), jumping onto every object, fixture and other player (a tall helper with a low one beside it, on the floor and on furniture), changing form on the spot, beside walls and at the top of a jump, and walking into thin walls at 20 frames a second, no form reaches a wall, fence or ceiling top, a top above the height limit, anywhere off the floor, or another player's head; every raised spot a prop can stand on can be seen from somewhere a hunter can walk to; and 200 practice rounds with bots give no out-of-bounds warning. Landing also counts everything passed on the way down in a frame, so a fast fall at a low frame rate can't skip the floor, and a tab in the background sends its position once a second (so a hunter with a hidden tab isn't left "in the waiting room" when the hunt starts). **Graphics:** a custom tone curve (`CustomToneMapping`, patched into three's shader chunk: colours stay as painted and only the brightest part of each channel is eased off; a filmic curve was tried and greyed the cream walls and the sky) with a key light, fill and exposure per map, a sky dome with sun and clouds, light distance haze, glowing ceiling lights, skirting boards, door trims, soft darkening where walls meet floors and ceilings and under furniture, and a soft contact shadow under every object. The contact shadow is part of the object type (`t.blob`): real objects draw theirs in one instanced mesh per map and disguised players in another (`playerBlobs`, filled just before each render from where each player is drawn), with the same quad, material and matrix maths, so the two still can't be told apart (checked by rendering each type both ways at every quality level and comparing the pixels: like the objects themselves, which are drawn instanced for real ones and as plain meshes for players, they differ only by rounding, at most a few dozen edge pixels per type). Quality (`oddone-gfx`): Low (`fast`, as the old Fast: no shadow maps, sharpness up to 1.1, and none of the tone curve, sky dome, haze, light glow, darkening decals or contact shadows, `applyExtras`), Medium (`med`, everything with a 1024 shadow map, up to 1.35) and High (`high`, a soft 2048 shadow map, up to 1.75, as the old Pretty). Whatever is left out is left out for real objects and disguised players alike. With nothing saved, touch screens start on Low and computers on High, stepping down a level by themselves if the first 4 s of play run slower than about 30 frames a second. **The hunter** (`HG`, `hunterModel`) is a toy figure built in code from rounded boxes (`rbox`), spheres and cylinders: navy jumpsuit, hi-vis vest with two pale bands, pack with an aerial, helmet with an orange band, dark goggles, and a rifle with an orange tip. Its shapes are joined into one geometry per material (`joinGeos`), so a hunter is 16 meshes and about 2,900 triangles; the legs, the arms and the rifle are still separate parts because they swing, aim and kick back. It stands on the floor (the old figure hung 36 cm above it) and is still 1.87 m to the top of the helmet, with the muzzle where it was, so the camera, tracers and aim are unchanged; hunters are never shot at, so there is no hit box to keep in step. On Medium and High it has its own soft contact shadow (a child of the model, not in `playerBlobs`, which is only for disguised players). Nothing about the hunter touches the objects or the players disguised as them. **Shots:** on Medium and High the tracer has a soft glow, the muzzle a short cone of flame and a halo, and every shot leaves a quick pale ring where it lands; the ring is the same colour whatever was hit, so it tells nobody anything new (the sparks were already yellow on a disguised player and still are). Low keeps the plain line and dot. **Menu backdrop:** the camera looks slowly from side to side from a spot inside the chosen map (`menu` in `MAPS`: x, y, z, the way it faces and how far it turns) instead of circling above the roof; a new map needs a `menu` entry, or it falls back to the old circling view. **Turkey Week** (`../events.js`, November 15 to 26, 2026): five object types are drawn as Thanksgiving things, on the maps and as disguises alike (`TK_PROPS` in `tkProps`, used by `defProp`): beach ball as a pumpkin, the gift boxes as a pumpkin pie and a basket of apples, the wooden crate as a hay bale, the garden gnome as a turkey in a pilgrim hat. Each type's box, health and speed are still worked out from its usual parts, so players with the look on and off agree on everything but the picture. Object types are built once at page load, which is also when the game reads the one Turkey Week switch on the Back Room page (see `events.js` below); it has no button of its own. **Wardrobe (November 2026, captains only for now):** hunter outfits, hunter tools, prop taunts and catch poofs, all drawn and played in code by `wardrobe.js` beside the page. Nobody else can see it yet: `ODD_PUBLIC = false` and `wardOn()` asks `../captains.js` (`may(false, 'wardrobe', 'oddone-wardrobe')`), so it opens for the site's captains, or with `?wardrobe=1` on localhost or in an automated browser; to open it to everyone, change `ODD_PUBLIC` to `true`. For everyone else `wardrobe.js` is never fetched (the page asks for it only when `wardOn()` is true or another player arrives wearing something), `WD` stays `null` and the menu, saved data, node fields and draw calls are exactly as before. Captains get a Wardrobe button under their name on the menu and on the pause screen; every item is free and nothing is bought (the shop stays off). There are 14 outfits (Police Officer, Sheriff, Mall Cop, Security Guard, Detective, Store Manager, Janitor, Park Ranger, Exterminator, Construction Worker, Cowboy, Pirate, and the usual hunter with a Top Hat or a Traffic Cone in place of the helmet), 6 tools held in place of the rifle (Flashlight, Price Scanner, Flyswatter, Butterfly Net, Broom, Rubber Chicken), 8 taunts (Bike Horn, Squeaky Duck, Slide Whistle, Boing, Cuckoo, Kazoo, Doorbell, Party Blower) and 5 poofs (Cloud, Feather, Bubble, Star, Price Tag); on 19 Nov 20 more outfits (15 to 34: Soccer Striker, Basketball Star, Gridiron, Baseball Slugger, Hockey Skater, Tennis Ace, Golfer, Boxer, Referee, Racing Driver, Pirate Deckhand with a parrot, First Mate with a hook, Ghost Pirate, The Boss, Pinstripe Suit, Tracksuit, Bodyguard, Butler, Tin Robot, Android) and 9 more tools (7 to 15: Tennis Racket, Hockey Stick, Foam Finger, Golf Club, Spyglass, Walking Cane, Serving Tray, Megaphone, Robot Claw) were added, every one keeping the orange arm bands, brighter overall than the usual hunter, hats inside the 0.28 m and tools inside the rifle's space; their names and numbers are in `../cosmetics.js` under `odd-one-out`, and `wardrobe.js` keeps one list per slot in number order (never reorder: add at the end). What a captain wears is saved as `oddone-wear` (`[outfit, tool, taunt, poof]`) and travels in their own node as one short field, `cz`: `'outfit.tool.taunt.poof.count'`, checked strictly by `parse` (anything else is ignored, and a number this page doesn't know draws the usual hunter, rifle or burst); older pages ignore it. **Fairness:** outfits and tools are drawn only on hunters (`hunterModel(color, wear)`; a prop is still `propModel` and looks exactly like the real object whatever its player wears), they change no hit box, height, speed, camera, aim or reach (shots still come from the camera and tracers from the rifle's muzzle point), hats stay within 0.28 m of the head's centre line and tools within the rifle's own space below the line of sight (checked with rays through the crosshair at every pitch, aimed and not, on a laptop and an upright phone: none touches the hunter), every outfit keeps an orange hunter band on both arms and is no darker overall than the usual hunter, and the flashlight has a lit lens but no real light. An outfit hunter is 7 meshes (one vertex-coloured geometry each for the legs, body and arms, sharing one material, plus the tool, its accent and the contact shadow) instead of 16. **Taunts** didn't exist before, so they are strictly opt-in: nothing plays unless a captain prop with a taunt chosen presses T (only while T is bound to nothing else; the hint line shows it) or taps the Taunt button that appears beside Menu and Scores on a touch screen. It plays from the prop's position for everyone in range, like a ping, so it gives the prop away on purpose; hunters in the waiting room don't hear it (like every prop sound), and in Hide practice the hunter bots go after it as after a ping once the hunt is on. One every 10 s (`TAUNT_CD`), and other pages ignore a player's taunts closer together than 8 s (`TAUNT_GAP`); a taunt is the count in `cz` going up by one. **Poofs** play only when a prop is caught (`onElim`, in place of the usual confetti burst), chosen by the hunter who caught it, using the same small effect meshes as the burst. Pictures in the Wardrobe are rendered once with the game's own renderer into a small render target (no second WebGL context). A test hook: `__ph.WD` (the hooks, the item lists and `figShot`/`toolShot` for contact sheets) and `__ph.wdSync`. It has Spanish: its words, prop names and Wardrobe items are in `odd-one-out/es.js` (lang.js; map names and the signs inside the maps stay in English), with a Language switch in Settings and the pause menu; prop names are translated on each page when shown, never sent.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/tumble-trials/`: Tumble Trials (Back Room), a knockout party show for up to 16 players (Three.js, Firebase Realtime Database rooms under `rooms/ko-…`, same project and rules as Rumble Karts; Quick match uses `rooms/ko-pub-1` to `ko-pub-4`; invite links go through the home page as `#ko-<code>`). Every show, online or solo, starts with at least 16 contestants (`FIELD`): bots take the places players leave, so a room of 3 plays with 13 bots and a solo show is you and 15 bots; a party launch (party.js's room `p` + 6 characters, `partyRoom`) is the exception: the show is only the party's people (`set.bots` 0, no `bm`), the lobby's bot line says "No bots in party games", and a party of one gets a one-round show on their own; the host's Players setting only caps how many people play. A show is two to four rounds and a final: races (the first few across the line qualify; falling in the slime puts you back at the last checkpoint), survivals (no respawns; whoever is still up when the clock runs out or when only the qualifying number are left goes through) and a final for the crown. Two rounds come before the final in a Short show, three in a Normal one, four in a Long one (one fewer below 10 contestants, which can no longer happen at the start). `planShow` makes the plan from the show's seed so that shows differ: the order of races and survivals (never three of a kind running, never all one kind, so a show can open with a survival), how sharply each round cuts the field (each round gets a random weight, so one show goes 16→13→8→5 and another 16→9→6→4), whether 4, 5 or 6 reach the final, and which rounds are used; rounds the show before used come last in the draw, so a show only repeats one when it has run out of fresh ones. A round always sends on the number it promised: if fewer made it (a race that ran out of time, or several falling in the same moment of a survival), the places left go to whoever got furthest in a race or was knocked out last in a survival, and never fewer than two go on. `nextRound` follows who actually came through; if players leaving means no more are left than the final was planned for, the show goes straight to the final rather than play a round that could knock nobody out. A show can only start with one contestant in practice. Levels are built in code by the `build` functions in `LEVELS` with the `B` helpers (boxes, cylinders and spheres that are both the mesh and the collider; the meshes are the hitboxes, so what you see is what hits you): 23 rounds, only ever added at the end so the number the host sends for a round never changes: Whirligig Way, Teeter Tide, Gatecrash Gauntlet, Springboard Skyway, Swingset Straits, Piston Parade, Roly-Poly Rise, Log Jam Lane, Sink or Skip, Belt Bedlam, Blowhard Bridge, Going Up (lifts up a tower, with blocks at the side as the quick way for a good jumper), Barrel Roll Row (bridges that are turning barrels) and Ferry Frenzy (ferries that shuttle between docks, and two that meet mid-river) are races; Whirlwind Dish, Gap Rush, Colour Call, Bowled Over and Roller Rink (a roller chases everyone round a ring, faster all the time; the view turns with you, `L.orbit`) are survivals; Honeycomb Havoc, Last Spin, High Tide Hill and Crown Dash are finals. Crown Dash is a final marked `dash`: it is played like a race (`mechOf`: checkpoints, respawns) and the first bean to touch the crown as it dips wins, or whoever got furthest if the clock runs out; on Whirlwind Dish and Last Spin the low beam (0.08 to 0.58 m up) has to be jumped, and the high beam's underside (`HIGH_BEAM_Y`, 1.95 m) is well above a standing bean (1.6 m), so you can stand, run or dive under it but not jump into it; the Warm-up Yard is the lobby and menu backdrop and the Crown Stage the podium. Every moving obstacle's position is a function of the round clock (seconds since GO, from the server time offset) and the round's seed, so all players see the same hammers and walls without sending them: a level calls `vary(seed)` to shift the clock of every moving piece it builds (`Lvl.to`), or adds its own shift where bots read the timing too (stones, fans, lifts, ferries), and the sweepers take a seeded start angle and direction, so a round is not the same twice. Who stands where on the start grid is shuffled from the same seed; the seesaws react to weight, so the host runs them and sends their angles. Each player writes only their own node `rooms/ko-<code>/players/<uid>`, because the shared rules expect `n, c, x, z, h, ts` there and refuse other paths for new prefixes: position and motion (`y`, `m`), look (`lk`: face, pattern, pattern colour), `rd` ready, `k` the round the position belongs to, `fin`/`out` ("show:round:ms" when you cross the line or drop out), `tt` the honeycomb tiles you touched, `gr`/`fr` grabbing and breaking free. The host (newest host number `he`, else whoever has been in the room longest, chosen only after the room has loaded) publishes the show as JSON in its own `rs` (phase, plan, roster, who qualified and who is out, in order; it goes out whole every time, so changes within a round are coalesced to at most 4 publishes a second, `Net.pubRS`/`flushRS`, while a new phase or round goes straight away; the shape of `rs` is unchanged), its bots in `bt`, seesaw angles in `sw`, and every touched tile in its `tt`; a tile vanishes a fixed delay after the earliest touch anyone reported. Guests treat the host's `rs` as untrusted: `sanitizeRS` rebuilds it field by field (known phases and levels, finite clamped numbers, ids of a safe shape, lists capped at 32, at most 24,000 characters) and drops it if anything doesn't fit, and names and colours from other players are escaped or validated before they reach the page. Guests also run the show on the host's clock (they measure the gap from the `ts` the host writes), and every browser rewrites its `ts` once a second even from a background tab, so a host who switches tabs isn't taken for gone. The host's browser runs the bots and decides qualification from the reports, so it isn't cheat-proof; if the host leaves, the next player carries on from the last show state and takes over the bots from where they were last seen (same traits, speed and checkpoint); a host that goes quiet without leaving is passed over after 4 s (`HOST_QUIET_MS`). `set.bots` is still sent (always 1) because pages from before the bots became permanent read it. A private room starts when everyone in it is ready (one player alone counts) or the host presses Start show; a public lobby starts 20 s after a second player arrives, or after 30 s with one. The physics is the game's own: a fixed 60 Hz step, a capsule of three spheres pushed out of boxes, cylinders and spheres, platforms that carry you, and knockback from the speed of whatever hit you. Bots follow each race's `path` of nodes and wait at `safe` nodes until the obstacles ahead will miss them; the same brain is the test autopilot. Node options a level can use: `w` width, `alt` side-by-side lanes a bot picks between, `xs` bounce pads (the node only counts once the pad has thrown the bot up), `safe` with `len` (how much of the way ahead has to be clear), `wait` (a test of the clock), `on` with `board` (a spot on a lift or ferry, walked to only while it can be boarded), `comp` (lean against a belt or a turning barrel), `near` (must be reached, not just passed). A bot that stops getting nearer its node hops, tries another line, backs off up the course and across, then goes back a node (`unstick`), and one whose jump will land short dives. No two bots are alike: skill comes with the roster, and nerve (how tight a gap it runs, how long it waits), pace and lane choice come from the show id and the bot's id, with a good or bad round from the round number (`botTraits`), so whoever takes the bots over works out the same ones. Keys are rebindable through `keybinds.js` (saved as `keybinds:tumble-trials`). On computers, clicking the game captures the mouse (Pointer Lock) and moving it turns the camera with no button held; a "Click to look" hint shows while it isn't captured, and the click that captures it doesn't also grab. Esc, the pause menu, the main menu, results and podium cards and the Controls panel let go of it (Esc in the online lobby only frees the mouse for the lobby panel; a second Esc opens the menu). A refused capture (Chrome refuses one within about a second of Esc) is only counted: after three in a row the hint goes and drag-to-look carries on, but every click still tries again. A two-finger trackpad scroll (or mouse wheel) also turns and tilts the camera; a pinch is swallowed so the page doesn't zoom. Sensitivity (Settings and the pause menu) scales mouse, scroll and touch look and is saved as `tumble-sens`, clamped to 0.2–3 (anything unreadable loads as 1). The camera comes in at once when a wall is between it and the bean and eases back out; static walls are found with a ray, and moving ones only if marked `camBlock` (Gap Rush's walls). In a race the count pill also says where you are running (`myPlace`), the intro card lists the show's running order, and being knocked out says where you finished. On touch screens the page adds `touch` to `body`: a stick, Jump, Dive and Grab buttons and drag-to-look, hidden while you watch or read a results card (`noctl`, `cardup`). Name, look, settings, best practice times and stats (shows, crowns, rounds qualified) are saved in the browser under `tumble-…`; nothing is stored online between shows. Parties can launch it (its row in `GAMES` in `party.js`). A test hook (`window.__ko`, with `advance(seconds)`) exists only when `navigator.webdriver` is set. **Closet (November 2026, hidden: captains only for now):** extra looks worn on top of the look everyone already has: 10 colours, 6 premium colours (gold, chrome, glow in the dark, see-through jelly, a slow rainbow shift, bubblegum with sprinkles), 9 patterns, 9 pairs of underpants (painted over the lower body as a clean, silly joke; the last pair is worn on the head instead), 19 hats, 9 face extras (an eye patch, dark shades and sports eye black among them), 43 costumes (the November 2026 wave added sports kits, pirates with eye patches, suits and mob-boss costume-party looks, robots, a wizard and a firefighter; a costume may be paint only, so a hat can be worn with it, and may paint over the face), 10 victory dances (danced instead of the cheer when you qualify from a race or win the crown), 6 fall screams made with Web Audio (heard as a bean drops towards the slime, quieter far away) and 5 knockout splashes (as well as the slime splash). `CLOSET_PUBLIC = false` and `closetOn()` near the top of the script (which asks `../captains.js`, with `?closet=1` as the test switch) decide whether a Closet button shows under the look on the menu card; for everyone else the menu, saved data, node fields and draw calls are as before. Everything is drawn by `closet.js` beside the page, fetched only when this player may open the Closet or a player in the room wears a kit; until then `CL` is null and every hook does what it always did. A kit is ten item numbers (the `no` of Tumble Trials' items in `../cosmetics.js`: colour, premium colour, pattern, underpants, hat, face, costume, dance, scream, splash), saved as `tumble-kit` and sent in the player's own node as `ck` (`'3.0.5.0.12.0.0.2.1.0'`, written only while one is worn; `kitOf` checks it strictly, a number `closet.js` doesn't know draws nothing, and older pages ignore the field); bots never wear one. Looks only: colours, patterns, underpants, face extras and a costume's paint go on the bean's existing skin canvas; a hat, the head undies or a costume's shape is one merged mesh per item shared by every bean (one more draw call, and it hides the Turkey Week hat, so none more while that is on; the propeller beanie, the fishbowl and the astronaut's bubble add one more); a premium colour gives the bean its own material, and the rainbow repaints its skin five times a second. The collider, size, speed and name tag are untouched: a costume is drawn around the same body and the face always shows. The Closet hides the menu and stands your bean in the yard beside its panel (below it when the screen is upright); drag to turn it, and choosing a dance, scream or splash plays it. The test hook adds `CL`, `closetOn`, `closetLoad`, `kitOf`, `kitStr` and `myKitNow`. **Turkey Week** (`../events.js`, November 15 to 26, 2026): every bean wears a pilgrim hat (hidden under the winner's crown): one shared hat geometry and material from `events.js` (`tkHat`), one extra draw call each. The game reads the one Turkey Week switch on the Back Room page (see `events.js` below) once, when it loads (off means no hats are made); it has no button of its own. **Spanish:** it loads `../lang.js` and its own words (and the Closet's) in `tumble-trials/es.js`; round and bot names stay English, the show's calls, round descriptions, lobby and results are translated. Text drawn or built in code uses `TRN()` (`TX` is the textures here); the results stamp shows `data-stamp-label` (the stamp's translation) while `data-stamp` keeps the English value the CSS keys on. Nothing translated is sent to the room. EN | ES switch in Settings and the pause card.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/deepword/`: Deepword (Back Room), a daily "rarest answer" word game with a multiplayer mode, Blowfish Brawl (one canvas for the ocean, no libraries; Firebase Realtime Database, same project and rules as Rumble Karts, prefix `dw`; invite links go through the home page as `#dw-<code>`; Quick match uses `rooms/dw-pub-1` to `dw-pub-4`; in the Parties list as `{ k: 'dw', … max: 12 }`, since `party.js` lists its own games). **Prompt bank:** `prompts.js` sets `window.DW_BANK`: `lists` (named answer lists, one string each: answers separated by `;`, spellings of one answer by `|`, and a commonness weight 1–5 after `:`, 5 being what most people say), `prompts` (`[category, question, list name or an inline list, optional filter]`, 866 of them, each with 20 or more hand-written answers; categories `geo`, `animals`, `food`, `words`, `pop`, `science`, `sports`, `life`), `words` (about 39,800 plain English words in five commonness tiers, built from the public-domain 12dicts and ENABLE word lists and ranked by how often each word appears in books and Wikipedia; the credits are in the comment at the top of the file, the list behind `'@words'` letter prompts), `labels` (lists the Brawl may turn into "<label> starting with X" rounds) and `rhymes`. `M('a','b')` merges lists (the game keeps the first copy of a repeated answer). A filter is a small regular expression on the normalised answer, limited by `safeRe` to letters, digits, spaces, `^ $ . ( ) | [ ] -` and `{n}`, and is checked against every spelling of an answer. Answers are normalised by `norm` (lowercase, accents and punctuation stripped, `&` → "and", a leading "the" dropped); category prompts forgive a stray plural, British or American spelling (`canon`) and a near miss (`nearMiss`: the same letters with the spaces left out, or, for six letters or more with the first letter right, one letter wrong, missing, extra or swapped, and only when exactly one answer is that close); both only reach answers people know (a hand-written answer, or rarity under 75, `knownMap`), so a slip next to an obscure answer never turns into a deep cut nobody meant, while the exact spelling of an obscure answer always counts. A wrong answer two letters from one or two known answers gets "Did you mean …?" buttons (`suggest`) instead; word prompts are exact, because a word one letter out is a different word. **The November 2026 expansion** (from 332 prompts and about 18,000 answers to 832 and about 52,000) wrote more answers and spellings into the existing lists and put every new list and prompt in the `EXPANSION` section at the end of the file. **New prompts always go at the end**: the Daily picks prompts by their position, so inserting one earlier reshuffles past days. That section also builds the word-puzzle lists (`w_allvowels`, `w_backwards`, `w_toprow` and so on) from the word list with small functions, so they grow when the word list does. The file is now about 1.4 MB (about 530 KB compressed). Puffer Party and Mainstream load this file too; Mainstream's boards name answers in it, so after editing it run `node check.js` in `mainstream/`. To add a prompt, add a list and a `p(...)` line at the end and give it at least 20 answers; subjective prompts ("something that melts") want 60 or more. **The Abyssal merge (November 2026):** Abyssal's hand-written questions that Deepword didn't have (US presidents, mathematicians, famous paintings, bridges, ships, typefaces, knots, New York City, theme parks, Beatles and Taylor Swift songs and so on, 34 of them) are the `FROM ABYSSAL` section at the end of the file, with a car-model question after them; Abyssal's tiers became weights 5 to 1, its sitcom and cocktail questions were left out, and a few answers failed the school-safe filter. **The big answer files (`bank/`):** on top of `prompts.js`, every list a prompt uses has a file `bank/l-<list>.js` (`p<n>.js` for a prompt with its answers inline, `words.js` for the word list) made by `tools/build.js` from free data: Wikidata (CC0) for names, English aliases and how many Wikipedias have each thing, English Wikipedia pageviews (CC0), GeoNames towns of 5,000+ people (CC BY 4.0, credited in the page footer), Linguabase word familiarity and a few of its word categories (CC0), plus Abyssal's answers; `tools/SOURCES.md` lists the sources, the licences and how to run it (`node fetch.js`, `node pageviews.js`, `node build.js`; the downloads go in `tools/cache/`, which isn't committed). Each file calls `DW_EXT.add(list, answers, fame)`: `answers` is the list again in the same format (the hand-written answers by their first spelling plus any new spellings, then the new answers) and `fame` is two base-36 characters per answer saying how well known it is (`--` for no data). `bank/index.js` (loaded with the page) names each file and its answer count. In November 2026 that took the lists from about 58,000 answers to about 212,000 (about 400,000 spellings) and the word list from 39,742 to 65,792 words; the median prompt went from 83 accepted answers to 130, and "Name an animal" accepts 26,388. `bank/` is about 6.4 MB in 498 files (2.6 MB compressed); the biggest, the two animal lists, are about 850 KB each (310 KB compressed), and a Daily Dive loads eight files. The page loads a prompt's file when it needs it (a Daily Dive asks for all eight at the start and the clock for a prompt starts once its answers are in, or after 6 s without them) and merges it after the hand-written list the way `M()` does, so the hand-written answers keep their weights. The school-safe filter for added answers is `tools/blocklist.js` (hashed words, plus hashed starts of words for look-alikes); `fetch.js safety` also reads every Wikidata item's English description and flags (adult occupation or genre, hate group, neo-Nazi, criminal conviction), and `build.js` drops what it flags, apart from a short hand-checked `KEEP` list; M/AO/PEGI 18 games and R/NC-17 films are left out too. **`node tools/check-safe.js`** (run by hand, like Mainstream's `check.js`) reads `prompts.js` and every `bank/` file the way the game does and fails if any answer or spelling, hand-written ones included, matches a slur, sexual term, drug or look-alike (it prints them masked; `ALLOW` in it lists the few innocent names the rules catch, like the sperm whale); run it after editing `prompts.js` or rebuilding. In November 2026 it took Sex Pistols, Hooters, Omegle, Tinder (the app), a few film and music genres, two musicals, two sweets, a drug in a rhyme list and old spellings like "Squaw Valley" (now Palisades Tahoe) out of the hand-written lists. **Mainstream and Puffer Party read only `prompts.js`**, so they don't see the big files. After changing `prompts.js`, run `node build.js` again (a list that changed without a rebuild still works: its new hand-written answers just have no fame and fall back to their weights). **Daily Dive:** `picksFor(day)` seeds a generator with the local date and takes one prompt from each category with 15+ hand-written answers (`coreCount`, so it doesn't depend on which big files have loaded), so everyone gets the same eight. Adding prompts at the end changes which prompts later days get (not days already played). Each diver's answers go in their own node `rooms/dw-daily-<YYYYMMDD>/players/<uid>` (`n, c, x, z, h, ts` as the shared rules require, `x/z/h` 0, plus `j` and `a`, the answers so far as the answers' first spellings separated by `;`), which stays for the day (no `onDisconnect`) so later divers can count it. After each answer the game reads the day's nodes once and counts how many other divers gave the same answer (`Daily.counts`), only to show "N other divers said this today": **other divers never change the score.** **Scoring** is how good the answer is, from real-world data: inside the prompt the answers are ranked by fame (1 = best known), rarity R = 100 × ln(rank) / ln(K) with K the number of answers that have data, capped at 500 (`RANK_K`), and points = `pointsFor(R)` = R but never under 10 (`rarityOf`, `pointsFor`; change the feel there). An answer with no data uses its weight (5 → 10, 4 → 30, 3 → 50, 2 → 70, 1 → 90). Fame comes from `tools/build.js`: a weighted mean of percentiles within the list of English Wikipedia pageviews (median day of the last 60), the hand-written weight (being on the hand-written list at all counts), Linguabase familiarity, population (places) and Wikidata sitelinks; plain words use Linguabase familiarity. Tiers, only names for R: Shallows (0–24), Reef (25–49), Drop-off (50–69), Deep water (70–89), Abyss (90–99) and Seafloor (100), each with its own share square. Every point is 15 m, capped at the Challenger Deep (10,935 m). After each prompt the card shows the tier, the points, how many answers counted and where yours ranks, a few deeper finds and the most-said answers; the results list has an **All answers** button per prompt that opens every accepted answer, best known first, with yours marked, its tier and points, and a search box (100 at a time). The share text is the date, one square per prompt, the depth, the zone and the deepest tier, plus the link. **Practice** can be limited to one category (the topic menu), and **Past dives** replays any of the last 30 days' Daily prompts as practice (no streak, no database). 30 seconds of air per prompt. Saved in the browser under `deepword:` keys: `days` (`{YYYYMMDD: depth}`, for the streak and the calendar), `best`, `dives`, `today` (the dive in progress, so a reload resumes it), `last` (the finished dive, for reopening today's results; both carry `pts` and `rs`, each prompt's points and rarity, older saves without `rs` show tiers worked out from the points; both carry `picks`, the prompts that dive asked, so a bank update during the day can't change them; older saves without `picks` fall back to `picksFor`), `mute`, `room`; the name is in `deepword-name` (party.js restores it there). Practice serves random prompts and can be paused with Esc. **Blowfish Brawl:** each player writes only their own node `rooms/dw-<code>/players/<uid>` (`n, c, x, z, h, ts`, `j` join time, `a` their answer as `<match>.<round>|<text>|<server time>`, `rm` the match id they want a rematch of) and the host also writes the round state as JSON in `rs` with host number `he`. The host is the live player with the highest `he` carrying a valid round state, else whoever joined first (`Net.elect`); it runs the phases (`lobby`, `count`, `round`, `reveal`, `over`), picks prompts from `brawlCandidates` (plain categories in round 1, then letters, word lengths, letter pairs and rhymes, each with enough answers for the room), fills the room to 4 with bots when the box is ticked (bots only say hand-written answers), except in a party launch (party.js's room `p` + 6 characters, `isPartyRoom`), where the box is unticked and locked with "No bots in party games", the host writes `set: {bots: 0}` and adds no bots whatever `set` says, so a party of one waits in the lobby ("Waiting for your party to join."), and judges (`judge`: answers in timestamp order, so the first sender keeps an answer and later ones are duplicates; blank, invalid or duplicate puffs you, three puffs pop you, and if everyone left would pop at once nobody does). Rounds start at 10 s and lose 0.5 s a round down to 6 s. Guests rebuild every round state with `cleanRS` (known fields only, ids checked, numbers clamped, the prompt's list must exist and its filter must pass `safeRe`), so a new field must be added there too; names and answers are only ever set as text. A background tab keeps its node fresh, and if it's the host keeps the rounds moving, with a one-second timer. Leaderboards hooks: `BackRoomStats.max('depth', …)`, `add('dives', 1)` (finished Daily Dives) and `add('brawlwins', 1)`. **The sea** is one 2D canvas drawn in code, with no image files. `Sea.build(seed)` places the creatures from the day's seed, so everyone sees the same reef; it draws the same numbers from the generator in the same order as before, so don't add or reorder calls there. The scenery (the rock walls on both sides, the coral, kelp, sponges and tube worms growing on them, the glowing specks and the sea floor) comes from a second generator on the same seed, so adding scenery never moves a creature. A creature's kind, depth and position come from the seed; its colour and shape variant are worked out from the values it already has (`col`, `ph`, and its depth for the silvery twilight fish and the glowing deep jellyfish). Vents sit on the nearest rock wall instead of drifting. Each zone has its own water colour (`KEYS`: turquoise, ocean blue, indigo, violet-black, then a warm black in the trench). Anything with shading is drawn once to a small off-screen canvas (`Art.get`, sized in drawing units, turned into an ImageBitmap, and cleared if the screen's pixel density changes) and stamped each frame with `at()` and `put()`; only tails, fins, tentacles and the diver's arms and legs are drawn live. Big soft areas (the water, the sky, the glow under the surface, the light rays) are gradient fills, not stretched bitmaps, because a browser that draws the canvas on the CPU fills a gradient much faster; `Grad.get` keeps the ones that never change, and the marine snow is one path filled once. Keep `shadowBlur` out of `Sea.frame`, and don't add full-screen passes (a vignette was tried and taken out for its cost). `Sea.place()` puts the diver beside the prompt card on a wide screen and under it on a narrow one, so the card never hides it; the diver tips head-down and kicks faster while the depth is catching up with the score, and its lamp comes on below about 420 m. The zone the diver is in sets the CSS variable `--zone` on `#app` (the same five colours as the calendar), which tints the depth pill, the top edge of the prompt card, the zone banner and the results. In the Brawl each player is a pufferfish drawn by `fishSVG` in a bubble seat with three pips for its puffs; the answer checking, scoring, saved data and room state don't depend on any of this. A test hook (`window.__dw`, with `setToday('YYYYMMDD')` to play a made-up day) exists only when `navigator.webdriver` is set. **Spanish:** `es.js` has its words (lang.js): menus, help, tiers and what they say, zones and depth landmarks, results, the every-answer list, share text, Practice by topic and the Blowfish Brawl screens; text built in code goes through `TR()`, dates and numbers follow the language. Prompts, answers and player names are English by design, kept out of translation (`data-noi18n`) and tagged "En inglés"; the Brawl still sends only English prompts and answers, so Spanish and English pages share a room.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/puffer-party/`: Puffer Party (Back Room), a hot-potato category word game for 2 to 12 (one canvas for the sea and the pufferfish, plain elements for the ring and the answer box, no libraries; Firebase Realtime Database, same project and rules as Rumble Karts, prefix `pf`; invite links go through the home page as `#pf-<code>`; Quick match uses `rooms/pf-pub-1` to `pf-pub-4`; in the Parties list as `{ k: 'pf', … max: 12 }`). **Answers** come from Deepword's bank: the page loads `../deepword/prompts.js` (never edits it) and uses the same `norm` (lowercase, accents and punctuation stripped, `&` → "and", a leading "the" dropped), the same list format and the same "keep the first copy" merging as `M()`. `CAT_DEFS` lists the categories as `[key, name, hint, Deepword list(s) joined by +, easy]` (`FOODS` is fourteen food lists merged); `LETTER_DEFS` adds "Words / Animals / Foods starting with X", checking the letter against every spelling of an answer. A category is only used if it has at least `MIN_ANSWERS` (60) answers, so a list that shrinks or disappears simply drops out; 164 make the cut today: 111 plain categories (45 of them added with the bank expansion, from Pokémon and Minecraft to "a wizard's backpack") and 53 letter rounds. Category rounds forgive a stray plural and a near miss (the same `nearMiss` rule as Deepword), word rounds don't, and a repeat is caught by the answer's first spelling, so "USA" after "United States" is "already said". **Rules:** players take turns round the ring; the one on the spot types answers until one fits and hasn't been said in this category (wrong guesses only cost time). The fuse is 12 s, 0.45 s shorter after every answer down to 4.5 s, and each new round starts 0.5 s shorter (not under 8 s); a category changes after 7–15 answers (a bit more than the players left, plus a 1.5 s bonus on that turn) and after every pop. Time out and the puffer pops: lose one of three lives, and the next player starts a new round with a new category; at zero lives you're out. Last one left wins; if only bots are left the healthiest bot wins at once. **Online:** each player writes only their own node `rooms/pf-<code>/players/<uid>` (`n, c, x, z, h, ts` as the shared rules require, `x/z/h` 0, plus `j` join time, `a` their latest attempt `<match>.<turn>.<k>|<text>|<server time>`, `ty` what they're typing `<match>.<turn>|<text>|<''|b|d>` (throttled to one write per 150 ms, shown under the puffer for everyone), `rm` a rematch request), and the host also writes the round state as JSON in `rs` with host number `he`, the same host election as Deepword's Blowfish Brawl (`Net.elect`: the live player with the highest `he` carrying a valid state, else whoever joined first). The host runs the phases (`lobby`, `count`, `turn`, `pop`, `over`), plans the bots' answers and timing into the state (`ba`, `bt`, so a new host can carry on mid-turn), judges attempts (`judgeAttempt`; an answer stamped up to 0.4 s after the fuse still counts, and the host waits 0.7 s before popping) and turns down a bad one with `nk`; players who leave are out (`dropLeavers`). The rules are pure functions (`acceptAnswer`, `popTurn`, `afterPop`, `newMatchRS`). Guests rebuild every round state with `cleanRS` (known fields only, ids checked, numbers clamped, the category key must exist), so a new field must be added there too; names and answers are only ever set as text. A background tab keeps its node fresh, and if it's the host keeps the turns moving, with a one-second timer. Bots fill the room to 4 when the box is ticked; in a party launch (party.js's room `p` + 6 characters, `isPartyRoom`) the box is unticked and locked with "No bots in party games", the host writes `set: {bots: 0}` and adds no bots whatever `set` says, so a party of one waits in the lobby ("Waiting for your party to join.") until a second member arrives; **Solo** plays against 1–7 bots on this device with no database (it pauses while the tab is hidden). Saved in the browser under `pufferparty:` keys: `name` (plain text; party.js restores it), `mute`, `room`, `bots`, `seenHelp`, and `wins`, `answers`, `streak` for the menu. Leaderboards hooks: `BackRoomStats.add('answers', 1)` and `max('streak', …)` per accepted answer, `add('wins', 1)` for an online win with at least two humans. A test hook (`window.__pf`) exists only when `navigator.webdriver` is set. Puffer Party draws no other players' positions, so it doesn't use `netsmooth.js`. **School-safe answers:** at load time it leaves out a short list of Deepword entries a school wouldn't want a bot to say (`BLOCK`: the Sex Pistols, N.W.A, spotted dick, tit and booby, butt, bra, Hooters, dating apps and Omegle, alcohol, bars, casinos and the like; `WORD_BLOCK`, for "Words starting with" rounds only: kill, gun, pistol, knife, weed, blunt, hate), so bots never say them, they don't count toward a category's 60 and typing one just "doesn't fit"; Deepword itself still has them. (That costs "Animals starting with P", which had exactly 60, which the bigger bank has since brought back.) In-game chat: loads `gamechat.js` (T to chat while in a party; T still types in the answer box). It has Spanish for its UI (`puffer-party/es.js`, lang.js, Language switch on the menu); the categories and answers are Deepword's English lists, so they stay English and are tagged "En inglés" in Spanish. **Wardrobe (hidden: captains only for now).** `PF_PUBLIC = false` and `wardOn()` (captains.js's `may(false, 'wardrobe', 'pf-wardrobe')`; `?wardrobe=1` for tests on localhost or in an automated browser) decide whether the menu shows a Wardrobe button; for everyone else `puffer-party/wardrobe.js` is never fetched and the game is exactly as before, unless someone in the room wears something, in which case it is fetched to draw them. It has 32 items in four slots, numbered as in `../cosmetics.js` (`pp-…`): 18 hats the room's one pufferfish wears while it's that player's turn (sports caps and helmets, two robot ones, two pirate ones with an eye patch, a fedora, a top hat, shades, a wizard hat, a crown; drawn on the canvas inside `drawPuffer`'s new last argument; the puffer's size, swelling, colour and spikes, which everyone reads as the timer, are unchanged, and nothing is drawn once it has popped), 6 trails that fly to the next player in place of the bubbles when that player's answer counts (`Fish.deflate(to, skip)`), 5 pops (extra pieces on top of the usual burst when the puffer pops on them) and 3 victory effects (kept to the sides of the screen, for everyone, when they win). The effects are the wardrobe's own few dozen pieces, drawn on the `#fx` canvas only while they play, and skipped under `prefers-reduced-motion`. Saved in this browser as `pufferparty:wardrobe` (JSON `[hat, trail, pop, victory]`); online, a player's own node carries `cz: 'hat.trail.pop.victory'` (written with `Net.upd`) only while something is worn. Older pages ignore `cz`, the round state `rs` is unchanged, and a page draws nothing extra for numbers it doesn't know. The test hook `window.__pf` adds `WD` (with `wear(slot, n)`), `wardOn`, `wdLoad`, `wearOf` and `drawPuffer`. Its words and item names are in `puffer-party/es.js`.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/putterby/`: Putterby (Back Room), online mini golf for 1 to 8 players in a toy-town garden (Three.js r128 with `../autores.js`, `../netsmooth.js` for other players' balls; Firebase Realtime Database, same project and rules as Rumble Karts, prefix `qg`; invite links go through the home page as `#qg-<code>`; Quick match uses `rooms/qg-pub-1` to `qg-pub-4`; rooms hold up to 8; in the Parties list as `{ k: 'qg', … max: 8, nk: 'putterby:name' }`). **Course:** nine holes, par 27, written as data in `COURSE`: floor pieces `P(x0, x1, z0, z1, height, rails, {h1, dir, t})` (axis-aligned boxes, flat or a ramp rising toward `dir`; `t` is `g` felt, `s` sand or `w` water; `ov: 1` marks a sand or water patch laid over another piece), extra walls `W`, bumpers `B`, moving parts `M` (`SLIDE` gates and `SPIN` bars) and loops `L`. Every piece is a solid block down to the ground, so a higher piece is a wall to a ball below it, and rails follow the piece's edges. Holes: Welcome Mat (ramp to a plateau), Bumper Garden, Gatekeepers (two sliding gates round a corner), Sandbar Bend (pond and bunker), Loop-de-Loop, Top Shelf (a drop off a shelf, then a ramp), Island Hop (jump the pond off a ramp, or take the bridge), Spin Cycle (a spinning bar) and Grand Tour (ramp, gate, drop, sand, a second loop and a pond). `prepHole` turns a hole into what the physics uses (rails become wall segments; loops get their frame) and `buildHole` draws it: the felt, sand, water, brick sides and rails are each merged into one mesh, so a hole is a few dozen draw calls. **Physics** (`simStep`, 240 steps a second, so a full-power putt moves less than half a ball per step): on the ground the ball follows the floor, slopes pull it downhill, rolling friction (`MU`: felt, sand much higher) and a little drag slow it; off a ledge or a ramp's lip it flies and lands with a bounce; walls, rails, bumpers (which kick the ball out at least 2.4 m/s) and moving parts push it out and bounce it, moving parts adding their own speed. A loop takes a ball that rolls into its mouth round a circle: too slow and it rolls back out, slow at the top and it falls off, fast enough and it comes out beside where it went in. The cup takes a ball slower than 1.9 m/s; a quicker one rattles over the lip and loses some speed. Water and the ground outside the course cost a stroke and put the ball back where it was putted from. After 7 shots the ball is picked up and the hole scores 8 (`PICKUP`). Moving parts are timed off the shared clock (server time online), so every player sees gates and spinners in the same place. **Controls:** press on or near your ball (90 px, 120 on touch screens), drag back and let go (power is the drag's length against 38% of the screen's shorter side, direction from the drag projected onto the ground); drag anywhere else turns the camera, the wheel or a pinch zooms, V or View shows the whole hole, Tab, C or Card opens the scorecard, arrow keys or A/D/W/S turn and zoom. **Daily hole:** `dailyHole(YYYYMMDD)` builds a straight 2 m lane of a tee section, three modules picked from the local date (ramps, bumpers, a gate, sand, a pond with paths either side, a spinner, a drop) and a cup section, so everyone gets the same hole that day. The first finish of the day is the day's score; `putterby:daily` keeps `{ k: date, first, best, n: streak }`, and finishing on the day after the last one adds to the streak. **Online:** each player writes only their own node `rooms/qg-<code>/players/<uid>`: `n, c, x, z, h, ts` as the shared rules require (`h` is 0; `x, z` and `y` are the ball), plus `j` (join time), `r` (the round id), `ho` (hole), `s` (shots on it), `hd` (the last hole finished this round) and `sc` (this round's card, `"2,3,,…"`). Everyone plays the same hole at once and balls pass through each other. The host (newest host number `he`, else whoever joined first) keeps the round in its node as JSON in `rs` (`{ id, ph: lobby | play | inter | over, ho, t0, ne }`): it starts the round from the lobby (public rooms start by themselves 15 s after a second player arrives), moves to a 5 s break once everyone in the round has finished the hole or after 3 minutes (anyone still putting is picked up), and after the ninth hole shows the final card (public rooms start again after 30 s). Someone who joins during a round plays from the hole in progress (or the next one, if they arrive between holes) and, missing the earlier holes, isn't ranked. Other balls are drawn from a short history of updates (`NetSmooth`), sent at most 10 times a second while the ball moves and every 2 s otherwise. **Saved in this browser:** `putterby:name` (plain text, the key party.js puts back after a party launch), `putterby:color`, `putterby:room`, `putterby:best` (best solo card), `putterby:daily`, `putterby:sound`, `putterby:hinted`. **Stats** (stats.js): `holes` (holes finished in the cup), `aces`, `card9` (fewest strokes for all nine holes in one round, solo or online; in stats.js's `LOWER`), `wins` (online rounds with at least two players who played every hole, lowest total; ties all count) and `dstreak` (best daily streak). The test hook `window.__qg` (automated browsers only) can step the physics, putt, try ghost putts (`trial`) and play a hole by itself (`autoPutt`).
- `e63334287a3deaa3/mainstream/`: Mainstream (Back Room, a daily puzzle on the Daily Puzzles page), Deepword turned upside down: ten prompts a day ("Name a capital city in Europe"), 30 seconds each, and the aim is the answer **most** people would give. The player types (no choices); an answer that isn't accepted can be tried again while the clock runs, the first accepted answer is final, and they can pass. No libraries, no network, no Firebase, no multiplayer; it uses the daily word games' shell (`../wordplay/kit.js` and `kit.css`). **Answers** come from Deepword's bank: the page loads `../deepword/prompts.js` (never edits or copies it) and has the same `norm`, `parseList`, `safeRe`, `oneOff` and `nearMiss`, so the same spellings, stray plurals and small typos count. **Boards:** `boards.js` sets `window.MAINSTREAM_BOARDS`, 342 rows of `[question, [ten answers], optional {extra spelling: board answer}]`: the question must be, word for word, one of the bank's questions, and the ten answers are that prompt's most common answers in order, written the way they should be shown (any accepted spelling). **The boards are prepared estimates, not survey results and not what players answered**, and the page says so (How to play, the start card, under every board): they started from the bank's commonness weights (5 = what most people say first) and the order inside a weight is a judgement call, tilted towards what a US school pupil would say. Chosen prompts are plain categories only (no letter filters, no word puzzles), each with 25 or more accepted answers; prompts where an obvious answer is missing from the bank were left out (for example "something you could find in space", which has no "planet"). The extra-spelling map is for the few lists where the bank holds one thing twice ("bike" and "bicycle" under "something with wheels"); a stray plural of a board answer also scores as that answer even where the bank lists the plural separately ("brownie" and "brownies"). **`node check.js`** (in the folder, no libraries) reads both files the way the page does and fails if a question isn't in the bank, a prompt has under 25 accepted answers, a board doesn't have ten answers, a board answer isn't accepted, two board answers are the same answer, or there aren't 250 to 350 boards; run it after editing `boards.js` **or Deepword's bank**. If a board stops matching the bank anyway, the page leaves that prompt out rather than score it wrongly. **Scoring (version 1, `SV`):** board places 1 to 10 score 100, 84, 70, 58, 48, 40, 32, 26, 20, 16; an accepted answer off the board scores 10; a pass or a time-out 0. Prompts 1 to 3 count ×1 ("Spring"), 4 to 7 ×2 ("Stream") and 8 to 10 ×3 ("River"), so a perfect run is 2,000 and the last three prompts are 45% of it. Bands: Up the creek (under 500), Backwater (500+), In the flow (1,000+), Main current (1,400+), Full flow (1,700+), Pure mainstream (1,900+). A daily run of 1,000 or more is the "win" that keeps the streak (below that the Daily Puzzles tile reads Missed, like the other scored dailies). The table, the bands and the weights are this game's own. **Which ten:** `dailyPicks(day)` deals the whole list out ten a day: each cycle of 34 days shuffles every category with a seed (`mainstream-cycle-<n>-<category>`), lays the categories end to end and gives day d every 34th prompt from position d, so no prompt comes back inside a cycle, every day mixes all seven categories (three from one at most), and everyone gets the same ten in the same order; 340 of the 342 are used each cycle. **Adding, removing or reordering rows changes which prompts future days get** (a day already started keeps its ten, saved by their questions). Unlimited deals ten random prompts, two a category at most, and never writes anything. **The clock** counts only while the page is on screen and no window (How to play, Statistics) is open, and the prompt is blurred while one is; an answer sent up to 0.7 s after zero still counts, and an accepted answer still in the box when time runs out counts too. A reload in the middle of a prompt comes back to a "Carry on" card with the time that was left. **The strip under the mode switch** is the run drawn as a river in ten stretches that widen with the multiplier; a finished stretch is filled as far as that answer placed (the top answer fills the channel, an off-board answer is a thread, a pass or time-out a dry dashed bed). After each answer the board shows all ten with bars as long as their points, the player's row marked; the results list opens each prompt's board again. On a phone the Enter and Pass buttons don't take the cursor out of the box (the keyboard stays up between tries), the keyboard drops for the board, a phone on its side gets the board in two columns, and under 300 px of height (sideways with the keyboard up) only the prompt, the clock and the box show. Reduced motion turns off the row, fill and shake animations. Storage: the shared daily record `daily:mainstream` (`score` = points, `tries` = top answers, `dist` = band 0 to 5, 0 the best), `mainstream:day` (`{ v, sv, day, picks: [questions], res: [{ a: answer shown, r: place 1-10, 0 off the board, -1 passed, -2 out of time }], left: ms left on the prompt being asked, done }`; points are worked out again from `r`), `mainstream:stats` (`{ v, runs, sum, best, tops, hits, asked, days: { YYYYMMDD: { sv, s, r } } }`, 60 days kept, each with the scoring version it was scored under) and `mainstream:seen`. It doesn't load `stats.js` (which talks to Firebase) and has no Leaderboards entry yet; adding both later is one line each. A test hook (`window.__ms`) exists only when `navigator.webdriver` is set, and `window.__WP_TODAY` sets the day as in the other kit games. **Spanish:** `es.js` has its words (lang.js), the shared shell's included; text built in code goes through `TR()` and the page redraws on a switch. The prompts and board answers come from Deepword's English bank and stay English (`data-noi18n`, tagged "En inglés").
- `e63334287a3deaa3/lunge-line/`: Lokop (Back Room; first released as Lunge Line, and the folder, the room prefix `gd`, the `lungeline:` storage keys, `keybinds:lunge-line` and the stats id `lunge-line` all keep that name on purpose, so saved settings, stats, leaderboard rows and invite links carry over), an 8-bit side-on sword tug-of-war for two (one small canvas, Web Audio, no libraries; Firebase Realtime Database, same project and rules as Rumble Karts, prefix `gd`; invite links go through the home page as `#gd-<code>`; Quick match uses `rooms/gd-pub-1` to `gd-pub-6`, preferring a room where someone is waiting alone, and always plays the standard rules on a random arena; in the Parties list as `{ k: 'gd', … max: 8 }`). **Rules:** one touch of a blade kills; a blade is deadly on contact (thrust, lunge, or walking or jumping into the point), unless the other blade is held at the same height in the way. Three heights (low, middle, high); moving your sword across theirs disarms them; switching into a thrust within 8 ticks of it landing is a parry (white flash, the attacker staggers). Up above high raises the sword to throw; a high guard deflects throws, crouching and rolling dodge them. **Facing:** a fencer always faces the way they last pressed and turns at once (a press the other way while standing turns on the spot; a tap the way you face is a short step; holding runs); standing still never turns you, there is no auto-turn or backpedal, so walking away turns your back; the finisher and stomp only work facing the fencer on the floor (or standing over them). **Going down:** a tap of down lowers the sword one height; holding down for 8 ticks (`crouchHold`), or pressing it with the sword already low or with no sword, crouches (`p.cr`, a deep squat drawn about 40% of standing height, body 22 px high instead of 54: under a middle or high blade, a low one still hits; the sword is tucked in, so it neither blocks nor cuts; shuffle at `crawl`; let go to stand; `p.hd` counts how long down is held). Down while running rolls (under middle and high blades, not low ones). **The kick** (down held + attack on the ground, armed or not, with no need to wait for the crouch; state `SWEEP`, how `kick`): a 7-tick wind-up on one hand, a slide of about 60 px along the floor until tick 21 that hits with the whole leg (body to foot, 30 px ahead, so it lands from blade range out to about 100 px), then getting up until tick 36; a sword held low and facing the kick stops it (the kicker is touched); it knocks the other fencer flat and their sword flies off a short way (they can mash any button to get up sooner); it goes under a middle or high guard, but a sword held low touches the kicker first, and a low thrust started on reaction beats it. Pushing into a ledge from a jump climbs it (never out of a pit). **Hanging:** a fencer falling past an edge (`world.edges`: any corner of ground or end of a platform with a drop of 50 px or more beside it and room to stand above) whose hands (`REACH`, 50 px above the feet) pass its level within 8 px of it grabs it and hangs (state `HANG`: feet `HANG_D` = 44 px below the edge, facing it, sword put away so it neither blocks nor cuts; `p.ex`, `p.ey`, `p.es` are the edge). Holding down while falling drops past on purpose; the runner dropping into their own winning pit never grabs (anyone else does grab a pit's lip). Up or jump pulls you up (the `CLIMB` state, 14 ticks, onto the ledge 12 px in); down or a new press away from the wall lets go, and you can't grab again for `regrab` (30) ticks. You can hang as long as you like, but the hands and head come up to the ledge, so a low blade from up there touches you (a middle or high one goes over), and a kick or a stamp (attack standing over them unarmed, or with the sword up) knocks you off (`kd` with how `off`). Of several edges or ledges in reach, the nearest (then the highest) is taken, so the choice never depends on list order. Bots pull themselves up (Easy after about 0.75 s), or let go when the opponent is right below on firm ground. Unarmed: punch, the kick and dive kick (attack in the air) knock down; a high guard skewers dive kicks; attack near someone lying down finishes them. Crouching over a dropped sword picks it up (the nearest, the one in front on a tie; two reaching for one, the closer gets it). **Long stages and the camera:** each stage is a long stretch you can roam both ways (2,400 to 2,880 px; the camera shows 640 of it). The camera's focus is part of the match state (`S.cam`, with its speed `S.cv` and the runner's look-ahead `S.la`, all in `hashState`), so every mode and both online players agree on it and the rules use it. With nobody on the run it follows the midpoint of the fencers, and the edges of its 640-px window (`VIEW`) are soft walls: they stop you going further out but never push, so the two can't get more than a screen apart, but together they can walk anywhere along the stage. With a runner it looks ahead of them (`look` 150 px, so they sit about a third of the way in from the trailing edge), swings round (`lookV` 5 px a tick) only when the runner actually moves the other way (turning on the spot does nothing), and keeps a defender who is ahead of the runner in the picture; the defender can't run off its leading edge (nor more than 580 px ahead of the runner). A defender off the trailing edge for more than `lost` (45) ticks is left behind (`lost` event): they vanish with a puff (no paint, no touch, no death counted) and come back like after a touch. The camera eases (`camK`), speeds up gently (`camA`), slows in proportion to the distance left so it never overshoots, has a top speed (`camV`, 10 px a tick) and a 1.5 px dead zone; it never shows past a stage's ends; a stage change cuts straight to the new stage. The only solid walls are the two ends of the stage: the winner of the last duel (Tide right, Bloom left) passes through the end on their side into the next stage; nobody else can leave it. The loser respawns after about a third of a second (`respawn`) 250 to 320 px ahead of the runner (or, with nobody on the run, on their own side of the opponent at a similar distance), found by searching the ground (`findSpot`/`spotOK`): inside the stage and the window, on level ground with the same height a little either side, not by a ledge, not under a roof lower than 60 px, not on top of anyone; if the runner is about to leave the stage there is no room, so they wait for the next stage. Gaps kill. **The ending:** the last stage at each end finishes in a pit (the last `PIT` = 92 px of the line, `world.goal`); the fencer who has the run wins by dropping into their own end pit (`S.win`, a `win` event, and the simulation then stands still), while anyone else who falls in just dies and comes back like any other fall. The page then plays the drop: a moment of falling, a cut to a long shaft with walls and streaks rushing past and the winner tumbling (about 2.5 s, with a falling-whistle sound), then a big pixel banner "<name> WINS" and a bar with Next game and Menu; any key (Esc goes to the menu) or a tap on the picture starts the next game with the same settings (online: back to the lobby and ready at once, winner stays on; tournament: the bracket). **Code:** everything that decides the match is in `<script id="core">` (`window.LLCore`): a fixed 60 Hz step `step(S, world, input0, input1)` on plain objects, with no `Math.random`, clocks or trig, and positions and speeds kept on a 1/256 grid with rounding that's the same either side of zero, so the same inputs give the same match in every browser and a mirrored match plays out as an exact mirror (fair to both sides; ties such as facing when two fencers stand on the same spot, or which sword is picked up or cleared away, are settled by position, never by player order; checked by swapping players and left/right over 400 bot matches, comparing fencers, facing, loose swords and the camera; walls are met in the order you reach them, loose swords are capped once at the end of each tick, and two fencers coming back on the same tick place themselves independently, all so player order never matters). Timings and distances are in `BASE_T`, tuned to be calm and readable (run 3.7 px a tick, about 220 px/s or a screen in 3 s, top speed in 6 ticks, stops in 4; lunge 17 ticks; roll 5.5 px a tick for 16 ticks; a slightly floaty jump that hangs at the top while held; a dive kick of 4.6/4.8 px a tick; respawn 36 ticks; inputs buffered for 8 ticks, 6 ticks of coyote time); variants change them in `tuning()` (`lowg`; `turbo` puts back the old quick numbers: 324 px/s in 3 ticks, lunge 14, roll 7.4, respawn 22); `thr` turns throwing off, `bounce` lets thrown swords bounce twice, `N` is 3 (the default, since stages became long: a novice's match against Easy then has a median of about two minutes), 5 or 7 stages; `prac` is practice (below). The match settings travel online as 7 numbers whose 4th (it used to be hazards) is always 0. **Arenas** (`ARENAS`): each has a middle stage and three stage layouts (`st`, built into `LAYOUTS` by `layout()`) for the runner heading right, mirrored for the left half, so both sides are fair by construction; layout C is always the last stage and ends in the pit (7 stages use the middle, A, B and C each side; 5 use the middle, A and C; 3 the middle and C). Stages are long: the middle 2,880 px (4.5 screens), A and B 2,560, the pit stage 2,400, so the whole line is 7,680 px (12 screens) with 3 stages, 12,800 (20) with 5 and 17,920 (28) with 7, where it used to be one 640-px screen per stage (1,920, 3,200 and 4,480). A layout is a short list of features (`G` gap, `T` low tunnel, `P` lone platform, `W` upper walkway with open ground under it, `U`/`D` a raised section with three 20-px steps up to a plateau 54 to 60 px high and a cliff or walkway at the other end, `S` a bump of steps) spread evenly along flat ground, so there is at least 200 px of flat ground at both ends and between features; the middle stage is built from half a list mirrored about a centre feature, so it is the same both ways. They are plain fencing ground with no moving or changing parts: the Lantern Market (night rooftops, awnings and a covered lane), the Old Aqueduct (sunset over the sea, stone arches and low tunnels), the Dune Observatory (dusk dunes, a raised dais in the middle, steps, domes) and the Signal Yard (a rail yard at night, freight-car walkways and gantries). Every layout has flat ground at y 300 at both ends so stages join up. **Bot:** `botInput` sets the same buttons a player presses (it turns round by pressing a direction, like a player); it sees the opponent's sword height `react` ticks late, and the four levels differ only in the numbers in `BOT`: **Easy** (the default for new players) is a clumsy beginner: 34-tick reactions, long pauses (`idle`), a visible stop before each strike (`tele`), often the wrong height (`match`, `wrongH`), sometimes walks onto a held blade (`blunder`), slow to chase a runner (`chase`), slow to turn (`face`), and never parries, disarms, throws or dodges a throw; **Normal** is about the old Easy, with a short `tele` and the odd pause; **Hard** and **Expert** are the old Normal and Hard. The level is saved as `lungeline:bot`; an older `lungeline:diff` maps easy to Normal, normal to Normal and hard to Expert, and a saved tournament list without `lungeline:tourv` is mapped the same way. Tuned against a scripted novice human (walks at the bot, sees it 15 ticks late, picks its height at random half the time, attacks every 40 to 90 ticks in reach, never rolls, kicks or dive-kicks; the bot's pathing, without those moves, gets it round the arena): over 200 matches on 3 long stages the novice beat Easy 198 times (99 %, median match 145 s; with one-screen stages it was 179 times and 40 s), Normal 43 (21.5 %, median 177 s), Hard 1 and Expert 1; on 5 stages the median against Easy is 266 s, which is why 3 is the default. It mirrors heights, strikes when they differ, parries (Hard), disarms, throws, picks swords up, reads the guard for sweeps and dive kicks when unarmed, plays out its jump arc (with the real hang-time gravity, `gravAt`) before jumping a gap, looks further ahead before stepping off a platform, and as the runner treats its own end pit as the way out rather than a hazard. Headless, Expert beats Easy 60–0 (median 64 s) and Hard beats Normal 60–0 (median 84 s). Equal bots are a long tug-of-war on long stages (160 matches per level over 3, 5 and 7 stages and every arena, capped at 20 minutes: median 509 s for Easy, 267 for Normal, 193 for Hard and 240 for Expert, with 42, 13, 5 and 11 matches still going at the cap, but never 40 s without a touch, a knock-down, a stage change or someone left behind); a median duel lasts 1.4–2.0 s. Bots break a long standoff with matched blades by switching height every couple of seconds once nothing has happened for 6 s, keep running as the runner when the other is right behind them, attack a turned back, and settle a tie over a dropped sword by punching or shuffling closer. Before a change to the bots, play old against new headlessly over a couple of hundred matches; single results swing a lot. **Drawing:** the canvas is 180 game pixels high and up to 320 wide (the camera's whole 640-px window; narrower on upright phones, which show the part of the window nearest the focus that keeps your own fencer at least 100 px from the edges, gliding rather than jumping when that has to move a long way, with a small arrow in the other fencer's colour at the edge when they're out of the picture); the camera is rounded to a whole game pixel once and everything is drawn from that number, rounded relative to the fencer it's keeping pace with while it does, so nothing shimmers; on a 60 Hz screen each frame counts as exactly one tick, so frame-time jitter never shows as a stutter while scrolling, and one game pixel is 2 world px; `layout()` scales it up by a whole number when that loses less than a fifth of the room (4× at 1280×800, 5× at 1920×1080, 2× on phones), else by the exact fit, always with `image-rendering: pixelated`. Everything is drawn in game pixels: a dithered sky, two repeating parallax strips per arena (`buildLayer`), each stage drawn once per match into its own long canvas (`buildRoom`, its set pieces laid out a screen at a time and mirrored on the left half); the sky stays put and the two strips scroll at a tenth and a third of the ground's speed. Arena colours are mixed toward near-black (`DIM`) so the fencers and paint are the brightest things on screen. **Stages:** each stage has its own look: the sky and strips take a tint by stage template (`STAGE_TINT`: the middle as it is, the first pair cooler, the second warmer, the pit stages deep red; the new one fades in over 0.4 s when the view moves on) and `stageProps` draws its own set pieces behind the floor (a landmark in the middle stage, stalls, columns, telescopes or freight cars, chimneys, a broken arch, tents or a water tower further out, and chains and warning posts in the colour of the fencer who wins there on the pit stages); crossing into the next stage shows a banner ("STAGE 4 OF 7", counted from the runner's own end, or "LAST STAGE") for under a second with a small sound, without stopping play; the bar at the top shows the stages as blocks (the lit one with a dark tick that slides along it as the camera moves through the stage), the pit stages at the ends in the colour of who wins there (Tide right, Bloom left) with a pit notch and an arrow outside, cleared stages in the runner's colour and the current one lit white; the fencers as plain flat pixel figures, one solid colour each (Tide teal, Bloom rose) with a blocky head, a thick body, thin limbs and a 1-pixel pale sword, no face, outline or shading, about 27 game pixels tall, posed frame by frame from the simulation state (`pose`/`sprite`: idle at three heights, step, run, crouch, shuffle, jump, thrust, lunge, parry flick, roll, dive kick, punch, the kick's wind-up, slide and getting up, raised sword and throw, disarmed, stagger, knocked down, getting up, finisher, stomp, ledge climb, hanging from a ledge (arms up, sword on the back) and pulling up, unarmed, and a buckling pose while a touched fencer flashes), and text in a 5×7 pixel font defined in the page (`FONT`, `text()`). The sword is always drawn at its height (low, middle, high), even in a roll. **Paint:** a touched fencer flashes white for the hit-stop (`hitstop`, 8 ticks, in the simulation) with a small screen shake, then bursts into paint of their colour (drawing only, never read by the simulation, so rollback, checksums and spectators are unaffected; checked by replaying a real-time match's recorded inputs headless and comparing `hashState`). Drops fly with gravity and stick where they land on floors (2 to 3 pixels thick), walls and ceilings (with short drips) and platform tops (with drips hanging under the ends); a pool 20 to 35 pixels wide and up to 6 thick spreads where the fencer fell, stops at the floor's ends and runs down the front of the floor there and under itself. It is all drawn straight into one transparent canvas per stage (`R.decal`) that lasts the whole match, on every stage, through respawns, banners and stage changes, and nothing is ever trimmed (a canvas holds any number of stamps; only drops still in the air are capped, at 900). Before this, the splats were 1-pixel stamps on the floor's bright edge line in mixed shades with a 15-pixel pool, so they read as speckle and seemed to vanish, and each stage stopped taking new stamps after 1,400 (about ten touches). The sword drops from the simulation as before. **Online (rollback):** every node carries `v`, the simulation version (`NETV`, 3 since the long stages, camera and ledge hanging; 2 was the calmer movement, facing and kick; pages from before have none and count as 1). Only players on the same version are seated, watched or played; anyone else in the room is named in the lobby as having an older copy that needs a refresh, while they're there you can't press Ready, and Quick match avoids rooms with them. (An old page can't be told anything, but it never gets a match from a new one.) The first two people in the room by join time `j` fence (seat 0 Tide is the host); others watch; after a match the loser re-queues behind anyone waiting (winner stays on). Each player writes only their own node `rooms/gd-<code>/players/<uid>`: `n, c, x, z, h, ts` (x, z, h are their position and sword height, for the shared rules only), plus `j` join time, `rd` ready, `m` (host only: `id|seed|cfg|D|T0|uid0|uid1`, the match), `ma` accepted match, `i` their input log `id.<first tick>.<run-length codes>` covering the last 240 ticks, `tk` tick, `ad` how far ahead they run, `hc` a state checksum every 60 settled ticks, `ss` (host only) a snapshot of the settled state every 120 ticks for watchers and repairs, `fx` the finished match and winner, and `pq`/`pe` a ping counter and its echo. Both run the same simulation: your own inputs go in `D` ticks ahead (2–5, picked by the host from the measured ping), the opponent's are guessed (their last input) and, when the real ones arrive and differ, the game rewinds to that tick from a ring of saved states and plays forward again (at most `MAXR` 30 ticks ahead of what it knows, else it waits); the one running ahead slows by a tick now and then. Effects play once per event key after a rewind, and a touch (its sound, paint burst, "runs" banner and the stats) and the win (the drop into the pit) are only shown once both players' inputs for that tick are in, with the victim drawn where they stood meanwhile, which the 8-tick hit-freeze mostly covers. Kills, wins and stats come only from settled ticks, so both players always agree; if the checksums ever differ the guest reloads the host's snapshot and replays. Watchers play both input logs a few ticks behind from the host's latest snapshot. The other player is drawn from the simulation, not from position updates, so `netsmooth.js` isn't used; small corrections after a rewind glide out over a few frames. A background tab keeps stepping on a 250 ms timer. **Modes:** Vs bot (Easy/Normal/Hard/Expert), **Practice** (alone in the chosen arena straight away, no score or win and no stats or room: `prac` in the match settings lets you walk through every stage either way with the camera following you (looking ahead the way you're going) and a fall puts you back in the middle stage; a move list beside the picture on a computer, above it on an upright phone and across the top between the button gutters on a phone on its side, shows each move with your own bound keys or the touch buttons and ticks it when you do it: move, jump, sword up and down, stab, lunge, crouch, kick, roll, dive kick, throw, hang and climb; a Dummy switch (`lungeline:dummy`) adds player 1 as a target that stands still changing height every few seconds, or fights back as the Easy bot, and comes back 1.5 s after a touch in front of you (or behind, if there's no room), and is brought back the same way if it ends up off the screen; Leave goes back to the menu), Local 2 players on one keyboard (and gamepads 1 and 2), a knockout Tournament for 3–8 people or bots on one computer (byes for odd numbers; bot-vs-bot matches can be decided instantly with the same simulation, up to 6 minutes of play, then by who has the run or which side of the line the fight is on), Quick match and rooms.
