One-line tips (`TIPS`) show once each the first time you do the thing (remembered in `set.tips`), and the How noise works card (menu and pause) lists every cause from loudest to quietest with how far it carries on that difficulty. Each noise shows as rings spreading on the water (the water shader's `uRings`, 14 at a time) and on the HUD's noise meter. **The monster** (`monStep`): there is one, and it follows the party from hall to hall. It drifts (0), goes to look at the loudest thing it remembers (1), freezes and turns towards a fresh noise close by (7, for `TUNING.react` s), then rushes at it at about 14 m/s (2), holds someone (3) or is stunned by a flare (4). When most of the party is in another hall it sinks under the drains (5, `partyRoom`) and rises somewhere in that hall far from everyone (6, `riseSpot`); it hears nothing while it's under. Memory fades over 8 s (`memWeight`), and it paths around tiles and pillars on a 0.5 m grid (`gridPath`). A rush takes anyone within 2 m at the end of it, so the way to survive one is to be far away or to get moving the moment you hear its shriek; otherwise it only takes someone who has just made a noise right next to it (walking into someone makes a bump that counts), so standing still and quiet beside a wandering monster is safe. Being grabbed shows a short face-to-face scare (a hard cut to its face lunging at you, a red strobe and a distorted screech, then black with TAKEN; no blood), with a gentler version in Settings. **Halls** (`ROOMS`, built from a seed by `buildWorld(df, seed)`): each is 56 m wide (`RHW`) and 56 to 70 m long. Its obstacle course is placed somewhere across the hall (`newRoom`), and `furnish` adds stepping-stone trails from the door you came in by to the course and from the course to three doors in the far wall, only one of them open (`W.doors`, `real`; the open one leads into the next hall), plus dead-end trails, lone stones and pillars, all from the seed. The courses: Pillar Hall, Broken Walkway (two routes), Sinking Tiles (light-edged tiles sink a moment after you land), Turning Bridge (planks that line up every 4 s), Moving Tiles, Currents (the water pushes you sideways), Memory Bridge (six rows of three tiles: the safe one in each row lights up in turn, the others sink under you), Crushing Walls (one lane in three is safe, shown by a green light) and the Maintenance Lock: two red valves (hold E, loud) raise the water and a stair, four number pillars give the keypad code, and the door out opens. Everything that moves is worked out from the run's clock and seed (`updateDyn`, `plankAngle`, `moverPos`, `crushOut`, `memLit`), so every browser agrees without sending it. **Items** (1–5): pebbles (6 to start, 3 more from each grey stone pile every 20 s; they skip on the water), bottles (loud, with an echo), wind-up toys (rattle for 8 s), flares (stun the monster for 5 s if it's within 6 m, and free anyone it's holding) and rescue ropes (pull a downed teammate out from 8 m). A thrown item's flight is the same function in the thrower's browser (shown straight away) and on the host (which decides what it does: `stepProj`, `throwVel`). Being held by the monster for its full grab puts you **down**: a teammate holds E next to you (2.6 s) or throws a rope, and you come back with a 3 s shield; while down you watch a teammate. Everyone down sends the party back to the last checkpoint (the start of the furthest room reached). **Online:** each player writes only their own node `rooms/hp-<code>/players/<uid>`: `n`, `c`, `x`, `z`, `h`, `ts` (the six the shared rules need), `y`, `j` (when they joined), `my`/`li`/`lv` (their lowest feet since the last update, their landing count and last landing speed: the host decides what those mean), `tp` (how many host moves they've applied), `hd` (what they're holding E on), `a` (pose flags), `up` (upgrade levels, 6 digits), `pk` (items packed for the run), `rd` (ready), `ac` (their last few actions as JSON, each with a rising `i` and the run number `g`), `ch` (proximity chat) and `pg` (a ping). The host is whoever has the highest `he` with a published state, else whoever joined first; it runs the run (`hostSense` turns each player's reported position into noises, falls, downs, checkpoints and coins, and checks speed and floating; `hostAct` checks every throw, pebble pickup, keypad try and rope; `hostStep` runs the monsters, items, valves, revives and the exit) and publishes it as JSON in its own node's `rs` about 10 times a second (`encodeRS`; clients check every value in `decodeRS`). Players move themselves (so jumps feel instant) and the host moves them only by teleport (`tp`), after a fall, a too-fast move, a revive or a checkpoint retry. If the host leaves, the next player rebuilds the run from the last snapshot (`stateFromRS`) and carries on. Someone who drops out keeps their place for 60 s (`TUNING.rejoin`) and the menu offers Rejoin (`hushpools:last`). `?dev=1` adds a fake-lag slider to Settings for testing. **Saved in this browser** (localStorage, all `hushpools:`): `name`, `set` (settings: sensitivity, field of view, volumes, gentler scare, invert, graphics, dev lag, and `tips`, the tips already shown), `save` (coins, upgrade levels, items owned (at most 3 of each), Normal and Hard escapes, best time), `last` (the room of a run in progress, for Rejoin). Items you take into a run leave the shelf and whatever you didn't use comes back after it. **Coins** (no real money anywhere): 10 a room cleared and 50 for the escape, times the difficulty's factor; the lobby's Equipment stall sells bottles 15, toys 25, flares 30 and ropes 25, and the Upgrades stall has six upgrades at 40/90/160 coins a level (throw range, pebble skips and rescue speed from the start; toy battery, flare radius and rope reach once you've owned that item). **stats.js:** `add('escapes')`, `min('fastest', seconds)` (Normal or Hard only), `add('revives')`, `add('rooms')` and `add('hard')`; the Leaderboards show Elo, Fastest escape, Escapes and Rescues (see elo.js for how a co-op escape is rated). **Touch:** shown only on touch screens (`body.touch`): a stick on the left, drag anywhere else to look, and Jump, E, Throw (hold to aim), Run, Creep and Ping buttons; the HUD rearranges itself for an upright or sideways phone. **Tests** aren't in this repo (it has no build step or packages): the core is checked from outside with Node (`node:test`: noise and hearing, wading and climbing out, the monster's states, rush, forgetting and following the party, item checks, seeds, the halls' doors and stepping stones, the memory tiles and keypad, coins and upgrades, checkpoints, wipes, revives and valves), headless bots that play whole runs with the real physics (1 to 4 bots escape on Easy, Normal and Hard), and two headless browsers in one online room (joining, ready, falling in and wading, being grabbed and going down, a rescue and the host leaving). Automated browsers get a test hook, `window.__hp` (`hold(true)` freezes your body, for screenshots of the landing marker). The Blackwade rework was checked with its own Node tests of the CORE (one noise per mistake, the landing marker against the real physics, the body, saves, seeds, halls and loudness unchanged) and a jump matrix comparing the host's verdicts with the real landing spots, also kept outside the repo.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/flipline/`: Flipline (Back Room), a one-button side-view stunt bike game in a cut-paper look (Canvas 2D and Web Audio, no Firebase except stats.js). Hold to ride; in the air the same hold tips the bike over backwards, so holding long enough is a flip; let go and the rider steadies the bike. Endless always opens with ~60 m of gentle ground. It is deliberately not like the neon line-art bike games: every layer is a filled paper shape with a soft drop shadow, the rider has a scarf on a little rope simulation, and hazards always look the same (berry red with cream stripes and teeth, outlined in ink) so they read by shape as well as colour in every place; coins are always gold rounds with a star. One self-contained `index.html` plus `es.js` (Spanish). Everything that decides what happens is the `FL` module between `// ==CORE==` and `// ==END CORE==`, with no DOM in it, so a Node script can run it on its own (the checks used for the first version did exactly that). **Physics** (`P`, every number with its unit): a chassis with two sprung wheel contacts and a belly skid, stepped 240 times a second whatever the screen does; held on the ground the back wheel pushes (10.5 m/s² from rest, fading to nothing at 17 m/s, never more than grip × load); released it coasts with a little rolling resistance; held in the air it spins backwards (up to 8 rad/s); released in the air the spin fades and, within 57° of level, the rider eases the bike level. A crash is the head or body touching the ground, a hazard, falling into a pit, landing more than 75° off the slope, or not moving forward for 5 s. Flips are counted from the rotation in the air against the change in ground slope (a flip from 308°), and only banked 0.22 s after a landing that didn't crash. Inputs carry their timestamps and take effect on the physics step they fall in, so the same timing gives the same run at 30, 60 or 144 Hz. **Scoring**: 1 point a metre, flips 100 / 300 / 600 / 1000…, a clean landing (within 15° of the slope after a real jump) +50 per flip and adds to the combo, which multiplies the next tricks (×1.5, ×2 … ×5) until a sloppy landing; near misses past a hazard +25, long air a bonus, coins +10. **Track** (`MOD`): 34 modules, each a function that writes ground from the current cursor and ends on level ground: flat, recover, roll, whoop, hill, valley, hump, kick, dkick, bigkick, tabletop, stepup, stepdown, stairsup, stairsdown, gap, gap2, gapup, gapdown, longjump, pillars, bridge, lpipe (a steep launch you have to flip off), dropin, seesaw, shuttle and lift (moving platforms), pend (swinging spiked ball), rotor, beams (low beams to ride under), crusher, saw, spikes and spikeroll. Ground is polygons for drawing and one-sided edges in 4 m buckets for collision. Moving hazards start their clock when the bike comes within 31 m and are timed so a bike at a good pace (`VD`, 14 m/s) meets them at their safest; keep the speed up and they swing out of the way. **Endless** (`endless(seed)`, `extend`) picks modules by weight with difficulty rising over 2600 m (`PICK`, `ENDLESS_RAMP`); every new module is checked by `vRun` (a point that always holds the button, at full and at 85% power, following the ground, flying off lips, landing, timing the moving hazards; it fails a module that hits a wall, falls in a pit, stalls or touches a hazard) and taken back out and replaced if it fails. The Daily run uses one seed per UTC day (`dailySeed`). **Levels** (`LEVELS`): 24 in four tiers of six, each `{id, n: name, th: place, f: flips target, t: time target in seconds, c: course}`, where the course is module tokens separated by spaces, numbers after colons, for example `flat:20 kick:1.1:26:16 pend:1:3.2:0 gap:6:0:0.7` (the arguments are each module's own, in metres, degrees and counts; see `MOD`). To add a level, add a line to `LEVELS` and check it with the bot (`botRun`), which plays from physics prediction by trying a few hold patterns ahead; all 24 were finished by the bot, which also set the time targets (its time + 12% + 1 s) and the flips targets (what a flip-seeking version of the bot managed). The track editor writes the same tokens, and its share code is `FL1.` + the text in base64; tracks stay on the device. **Saving**: localStorage `flipline:save` (versioned, `v: 1`): coins, owned and chosen vehicle, rider and place, Endless and Daily bests, per level medals (bits 1 finish, 2 flips, 4 time), best time and the ghost (the step numbers where the button changed, replayed through the same physics), challenge state (three active from 40 in `CH`, with progress), totals, secret-vehicle feats and settings (music, sound, mute, fewer effects, ghost). Vehicles (12 bought with coins, 3 secret: a quadruple flip, 1000 m without a flip, all 24 levels finished), riders (8) and places (6) are looks only: they all use the same physics. Coins only come from playing (score / 100 per run, coins picked up, medals and challenges). **Board**: `BackRoomStats.max('distance')`, `max('score')` and `add('runs')` after an Endless run, `max('medals')` after a level; Flipline's entry in leaderboard.js shows them. Controls through keybinds.js: Ride / flip (Space, up arrow, W; or the mouse, or touch anywhere), Restart (R), Pause (Esc, P). Restart is instant; after a crash any press goes again. A slow-motion replay of the last crash is on the results card (“Watch the crash”), never automatic. Test hooks (`window.__fl`, with manual stepping) exist only when `navigator.webdriver` is set. Nobody has played it by hand yet or listened to the sound; the feel numbers in `P` are the first thing to adjust.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/tidewick/`: Tidewick (Back Room), a classic 8-bit style side-scrolling platformer for one player (Canvas 2D and Web Audio, no Firebase except stats.js). Pip, a child in a yellow raincoat, crosses a rainy harbour at dusk in two stages: **HARBOR 1** (the quay, about 210 tiles) and **HARBOR 2: THE BREAKWATER** (a short stone fortress over dark water, about 100 tiles) with a boss at the end. Unlike the other Back Room games it isn't one self-contained `index.html`: it's ES modules (the owner asked for them) in `js/`, loaded by `<script type="module" src="js/main.js">` with relative imports only, so it works from `python3 -m http.server` and GitHub Pages with no build step: `constants.js` (**every tunable number**: speeds, jump bands, gravities, air control, hitboxes, timers, scores, the boss), `level.js` (the stages and the cellar as rows of characters, with a legend at the top of the file: edit a row by hand, keeping every row of a map the same length), `player.js` (Pip's movement and collision), `entities.js` (enemies, power-ups, sparks, the Tollkeeper and its bolts), `game.js` (the states, scoring and how things touch; no DOM, so a Node script can import it and run the game headless), `collision.js`, `camera.js`, `input.js`, `sprites.js` (a 50-colour palette and every sprite as a pixel grid using at most three colours plus transparency, drawn to offscreen canvases at startup), `render.js`, `hud.js` (a 5 × 7 pixel font drawn in code, the HUD and the full-screen cards), `audio.js` (two pulse voices, a triangle and noise, synthesised; every tune is Tidewick's own) and `main.js` (the page, the loop, touch buttons). **Loop**: a fixed 60 updates a second in `requestAnimationFrame` (the number of updates is worked out from the time passed, so 60, 120 and 144 Hz screens run identically), a frame gap is capped at 100 ms, and the game pauses itself when the tab is hidden or loses focus. The picture is 256 × 240, drawn into an off-screen buffer and shown as large as the window allows in its 16:15 shape (`fit()` in `main.js`; F or the Full screen button gives it the whole screen and hides the key strip). The canvas is sized in device pixels and laid out at one CSS pixel per canvas pixel, then shrunk by the device pixel ratio with a transform (a fractional CSS width gets rounded by layout and stretches the picture by a pixel or two). When the size comes out within 4% of a whole number of device pixels per game pixel it snaps to it and the buffer is copied straight across; otherwise `present()` first blows the buffer up to the next whole number with hard edges and then eases that down to the canvas, so every game pixel is the same width and nothing shimmers as the view scrolls. Making the picture bigger never changes the game: the rules only ever see the 256 × 240 world. **Physics** follow the owner's spec to the number (pixels per frame): from rest Pip starts at 0.0742, walks up to 1.5625 (0.0371 a frame) and runs up to 2.5625 (0.0557), slows by 0.0508 with nothing held and skids at 0.1016 when pushing the other way, turning round below 0.6875; the jump's start speed and two gravities are picked at take-off from the speed (`JUMP_BANDS`: under 1, up to a full walk, and anything faster than a walk, which is the higher, heavier running jump; the lighter gravity only while jump is held and Pip is still rising), falling speed drops back to 4 whenever it reaches 4.5 (`MAX_FALL`, `FALL_TRIP`), air control has its own accelerations and a cap set by the take-off speed. A standing jump rises 66 px (4.1 tiles), a full-speed running jump 82.5 px (5.2). Running into a wall stops Pip dead (so a jump from against a wall is a standing jump), and big Pip ducks by himself if he ever stands where there is only room to crouch. The thresholds for the running jump and the skid turn, and the falling-speed rule, were checked against a public disassembly of the 1985 game these numbers come from. Head bumps hit only the block nearest Pip's centre and slide past a corner clipped by 4 px or less. The view only scrolls right and its left edge is a wall. **Things**: driftwood planks (small Pip bumps them, big Pip breaks them), lantern crates (a brass button, or a loaf of bread when small and an ember pepper when big), planks hiding up to 10 buttons for 4 s after the first hit, planks hiding a firefly jar (12 s of glowing invincibility with its own music), a hidden crate with a clover (an extra life), stone crates, clay chimney pots (the smoking one leads down to a cellar of buttons, whose flue brings Pip back up a second chimney), harbour water (costs a life), the signal mast (100 to 5000 points by grab height) and the harbour-master's hut, where the time left counts down at 50 points a second. **Enemies**: Bilge, a walking puddle (0.5 px a frame, turns at walls and at other enemies, walks off ledges, stomped flat for half a second); Whelk, a snail whose shell can be kicked (3 px a frame; a sliding shell knocks out other enemies on the shell chain, hurts Pip from the side, and a stomp stops it); and a wary Whelk with a coral shell that turns round at ledges. Stomps score 100, 200, 400 … 8000 then a life; shell knock-outs 500 … 8000 then a life. **The Tollkeeper** waits on a plank drawbridge at the end of the Breakwater: it paces 40 px either way, hops on a fixed random-looking rhythm (`BOSS_HOP_GAPS`), spits a slow brine bolt every 2 to 3 s (`BOSS_SPIT_GAPS`) at one of three heights (jump the low one; big Pip ducks the middle one), and its bolts start drifting in from off screen before it shows. Stomping it hurts. Get past it to the winch at the far end (the bridge drops it into the water) or hit it with 5 sparks; either way 5000 points, then the hut, the count, and an ending card. Dying in the Breakwater restarts the Breakwater. **Saved in the browser only**: `tidewick:hi` (best score) and `tidewick:mute`, both in try/catch; keys under `keybinds:tidewick`. Stats hook: `max('score', score)` at game over and at the ending (play time is counted by stats.js as usual). Controls: arrows or A/D move, down or S crouches and goes down a chimney, X or Space jumps, Z or Shift runs and throws sparks, Enter (or Esc) pauses, M mutes, all rebindable under Controls; backtick shows the debug view (hitboxes, position, speed, state, fps) and, while paused in it, P steps one update. On touch screens (`body.touch`): a pad bottom left (slide a thumb between left and right), RUN and JUMP bottom right, sound and pause top right; the picture sits above them upright and between them sideways. A test hook (`window.__tw`) exists only when `navigator.webdriver` is set. It has Spanish: its words are in `tidewick/js/es.js` (lang.js; a plain script loaded before the modules), and the pixel font in `hud.js` has Á É Í Ó Ú Ñ ¡ ¿ for it; it has no menu, so the language is switched on the Back Room page (the canvas follows the switch on the next frame). **The Wardrobe** (captains only for now, like the other games' hidden cosmetics): 30 looks for Pip in `tidewick/wardrobe.js`, listed in `cosmetics.js` under `GAMES.tidewick` (16 outfits, 7 hats, 3 face pieces and 4 trails: sports kits, a tin robot, pirates, suits and more). `js/main.js` fetches `wardrobe.js` and `../cosmetics.js` only when `wardrobeOn()` is true (`TIDEWICK_PUBLIC`, the one line that opens it to everyone; a site captain through `captains.js`; or `?wardrobe=1` on localhost or in an automated browser), and then adds a Wardrobe button to the key strip and a hanger button to the round touch buttons; for everyone else `js/look.js` stays empty and Pip is drawn exactly as before. Looks only: an outfit repaints the coat pixels of Pip's ordinary form (the ember form and the firefly flashes keep their own colours, so power-ups still read), every outfit's coat is at least 4.5 times as bright as the dusk sky (`MIN_CR`; the raincoat is 8.2), hats go in rows added above the sprite (the feet and the rules' hit box don't move), face pieces paint over the face, and trails are a few pixels left behind Pip that fade within half a second. Opening it pauses a game in progress. What is worn is saved in this browser as `tidewick:look` (`{o, h, f, t}`, item numbers, 0 for none); nothing is sent anywhere. The item names and the screen are in `js/es.js`.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/beatvault/`: Beatvault (Back Room), a one-button rhythm runner for one player (Canvas 2D and Web Audio, no Firebase except stats.js). Original game and art: a **cut-paper stage** (flat card shapes with a lighter top edge and a hard offset shadow, never a blur, and a paper grain laid over the whole picture); the player is a little cream paper lantern with an amber flame, hazards are vermilion **burrs** (round seed heads with spikes, the only red in the game, drawn half-buried when they sit on a surface), the pickups are three turquoise **vault keys** per level (the only turquoise), and the finish is a round vault door whose three lights show the keys you carry. Each level has its own stage colours (`THEMES`: dawn hills, kite-weather clouds, violet windmills, a sea at dusk, a night pond, a clock-face theatre). It is one self-contained `index.html` plus `es.js`; the first script is the **rules module `BV`** between `// ==CORE==` and `// ==/LEVELS==` (no DOM, so Node can run it: load that slice into a `vm` context), the second is the page (sound, drawing, menus, practice, the editor). **Rules**: a fixed 240 steps a second (`BV.HZ`); the page runs as many steps as the clock says, and every press is time-stamped and applied at the exact step it falls in, so a run is identical at 30, 60 or 144 frames a second; nothing in `step()` uses `Math.sin`/`Math.cos` (rotors use the polynomial `dsin`), so it's the same on every engine. You move forward on your own at 4 units a beat (speed gates: 3, 4, 5 or 6), and all the forms' numbers are in units and beats, scaled by the song's tempo (`consts`), so a level means the same at any BPM: the **Lantern** hops 2.15 units high and lands exactly one beat later (hold to keep hopping; a press up to 0.1 s before landing still jumps, and up to 0.07 s after running off an edge), the **Kite** rises while held and sinks when let go, the **Pinwheel** flips between floor and ceiling on each press, the **Swift** climbs at 45° while held and dives when let go, and the **Frog** jumps higher the longer you hold (1.3 to 3.6 units). Gates across the whole height change the form, gravity, speed or size (small forms are about two thirds the size); bounce pads fire on touch, rings take a press while you're inside them. Hitting the side of a block or touching a burr ends the run at once (the hurt box is 72% of the drawing; a block corner clipped by under about 0.2 units is stepped onto), and the next attempt starts 0.32 s later with the music back at the top. **Levels** (`BV.LEVELS`, built by `BV.Builder` from small text grids, one character a unit; the legend is above `Builder`): 1 First Fold (112 BPM, 1:08, jumping), 2 Kite Weather (124 BPM, 1:13, the Kite and pads), 3 Turnstile (132 BPM, 1:20, the Pinwheel, gravity gates, rings), 4 Swallowtail (140 BPM, 1:29, the Swift, speed and size gates), 5 Leapfrog (150 BPM, 1:16, the Frog and burrs that hop on the beat), 6 Clockwork Curtain (164 BPM, 1:27, rotors and every form). `b.sec('drop')` throws if a level has drifted off its song's sections, so each part of a level starts where that part of the music does, and the drops are the hardest parts. **Songs** (`BV.SONGS`) are data: tempo, key, mode, a four-chord loop, a seed for the two-bar melody, and the sections (intro, build, drop, break, finale, end); `BV.song(id)` turns that into note events (kick, snare, hats, crash, toms, bass, pad, arpeggio, lead or bell, a riser before each drop) and the page plays them with a look-ahead scheduler on the audio clock, pads, bass and arpeggios ducking under each kick in the drops. They are synthesised, written for this game, and **nobody has listened to them yet**: they were only checked as rendered waveforms (levels, no clipping, the sections audible as changes in loudness). **To add a level**: add a song to `SONGS` (or reuse one), write a builder function like the six (one `b.g()` grid per bar, `b.sec()` at each section), push it onto `BV.LEVELS`, then run the bot: in Node, `BV.solve(BV.compile(level))` must return `ok: true` (it's a breadth-first search over hold and let go, every 6 steps); levels made in the editor are the same format (`BV.compile` takes either). **Practice**: checkpoints save themselves every two bars while you're steady, Z (or Save point on a phone) adds one and X removes the last; dying goes back to the last one with the music picked up from that point; a practice best and a practice finish are kept apart from real ones. **Editor** (Made here → New level): pieces on a grid with the song's beats, bars and sections drawn on it, any of the six songs, play from the start or from the view, undo; a level can only be shared once its author has cleared it from the start (Verify), and sharing is a text code (`BV.encode`/`BV.decode`: `BV1-` + base64 of the level + a checksum) copied by hand: there's no server, nothing is uploaded, and names in pasted codes go through the name filter. Online sharing would need somewhere to store levels (for example a new Realtime Database path with its own security rules for size, rate and who may delete), a moderation step for names, and a browse page; none of that exists yet. **Settings**: music and effects volume, an audio delay (−200 to +200 ms) with a tap-along delay finder, Reduced effects (no screen shake or beat pulses, fewer scraps; nothing ever flashes the whole screen, and it starts on Reduced when the system asks for reduced motion), and hit boxes in practice. **Saved in the browser only**: `beatvault:save` (`{v: 1, lv: {<level id>: {b: best %, pb: practice best %, c: clears, k: key bits, a: attempts, t: best clear seconds}}}`), `beatvault:set` (`{v: 1, mv, sv, lat, fx, hb}`), `beatvault:edit` (`{v: 1, lv: [{id, name, track, start, o, vf, by, at}]}`, the levels made or pasted here; `vf` is a hash of the level as it was verified, so any edit unverifies it) and keys under `keybinds:beatvault`. Stats hooks (built-in levels only, real clears only): `max('levels', levels cleared)`, `max('keys', vault keys held)` and `add('clears', 1)`, shown on its Leaderboards entry. Controls: Space, Up or W (or a click, or a tap anywhere) to act, P or Esc to pause, R to restart, Z and X for checkpoints, M for sound, all rebindable under Controls. **Touch** (`body.touch`): tap anywhere, a big pause button top right, and in practice two checkpoint buttons bottom left; the stage is sized to show at least 13 units across (16 in landscape), so a phone held upright sees the same distance ahead with more sky above and below. **Spanish**: `beatvault/es.js` (lang.js); level and song names stay English, everything else is translated, including the form names. A test hook (`window.__bv`: state, presses, a fake-clock runner, frame freezing for screenshots) exists only when `navigator.webdriver` is set, and never reports stats.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/gridpop/`: Gridpop (Back Room), a single-player block-placement puzzle on an 8 × 8 board (one canvas, no libraries, no Firebase). The rules live between `// ==CORE==` and `// ==/CORE==` with no drawing or input: `DEFS` lists the 41 pieces, every orientation a separate piece because pieces never turn, each with a weight (pieces of 5 or more squares are about one draw in six); `deal` makes three independent weighted draws and only looks at the board to make sure an empty board gets something that fits, so sets are never rigged either way; `applyMove` places a piece, clears every full row and column at once (crossing squares once), scores 1 a square plus `lineScore(n)` (10, 30, 60, 100, 150, 210 for 1–6 lines, `5n(n+1)`) times the combo, and deals a new set only when all three tray slots are empty. The combo goes up by one for each clear that comes within 2 non-clearing placements of the last one (`combo`, `miss`); a third miss resets it. Game over is checked after the clears, against every position of every piece left in the tray. Saved in the browser only: `gridpop:save` (the game in progress as `{v:1, b: 64 digits 0–8, t: tray as [piece id, colour] or 0, s, cb, ms, mv, best}`, written after every move and removed when the game ends; `deserialize` rejects anything out of range, unknown piece ids or a board with a full line, and a bad save just starts a new game), `gridpop:best`, `gridpop:mute`, `gridpop:seen` (How to play shown once), and keys in `keybinds:gridpop` (keybinds.js). Dragging (`updateSnap`, `liftFor`, `slotAt`): a press anywhere in a piece's third of the strip beside the board picks it up (a press in an empty slot's third takes the nearest piece left, if close), and the piece grows to board size at once. With a mouse it is centred on the cursor; on touch it rides 1.75 squares above the finger, the same everywhere, except that when the board reaches almost to the bottom of the screen (phones sideways) the lift shrinks over the last 2.5 squares so the bottom row can be reached with the finger still on the screen. It snaps by its centre to the nearest square; if that spot is blocked, the nearest free spot within about half a square more is used, a piece hanging a little off the edge counts as at the edge, and a chosen spot is kept until the piece is clearly nearer another (so it doesn't flicker or move as the finger lifts). The shadow (the piece's colour plus a white outline drawn over the dragged piece) is always exactly where it will drop, and there is no shadow when it can't drop; letting go drops it on the shadow or sends it back to the tray. Leaderboards hooks: `BackRoomStats.max('score', …)` (best score) and `add('lines', n)` (lines cleared). A test hook (`window.__gp`) exists only when `navigator.webdriver` is set. **Looks** (all outside the rules, in the `ART` table and `buildSprites` above `layout`): everything is lit from the top-left. Blocks are pressed tiles (`makeBlock`): their thickness shows along the bottom and right, and the centre is a plain shallow dish. Nothing is drawn on a block: for a day each colour had a stamped mark, and those were taken out to keep the blocks simple. So that the colours can still be told apart, the 8 shades in `COLORS` (in the rules) differ in lightness as well as hue (deep blue against light purple, deeper orange against yellow, lighter green against sky); keep that spread if you change one. Colour has no part in the rules, and saved games hold colour numbers 1–8, not the shades, so old saves load as before. Shading is worked out from them by `tint` (lighter tones turn warm, darker ones cool). Every picture is drawn once per layout into its own small canvas and only copied each frame: the blocks at board and tray size, the board (frame, shadow and 64 sunken wells, whose four quarters alternate between two close tones so rows and columns are easier to count; it has a margin `BM` for the shadow), the tray's three wells (`dockSpr`, sizes in `wells`, which the keyboard's pick ring follows), the round sparks (`pip`) and the streak of light along a cleared row or column (`beamH`, `beamV`, timed by `beamT0`). The dotted backdrop and the strip of block colours along the top of every menu are CSS, so they cost nothing per frame. With the device's reduce-motion setting on (`calm()`), there is no shake, no bounce when blocks land or pieces arrive, one slow spark a square instead of six, and the score and New High Score badge don't animate. `tone` is the sound helper, so the colour helper is `tint`. **Spanish:** `es.js` has its words (lang.js); the words popped up on the board go through `T()`. **Styles** (captains only for now, like the other games' hidden cosmetics): 8 looks in `gridpop/styles.js`, listed in `cosmetics.js` under `GAMES.gridpop`: 4 block sets (Sports Balls, Circuit Rivets, Treasure Corners, Pinstripe) and 4 boards (Stadium Night, Pirate Deck, Robot Steel, Gold Pinstripe). The page fetches `styles.js` and `../cosmetics.js` only when `stylesOn()` is true (`GRIDPOP_PUBLIC`, the one line that opens it to everyone; a site captain through `captains.js`; or `?styles=1` on localhost or in an automated browser), and then a Styles button appears on the How to play and pause panels; for everyone else `WR` stays null and `makeBlock` and `buildSprites` draw exactly as before. Looks only: a block set keeps every block's `COLORS` colour, shape, lip and dish and adds art only in white or black at low strength (plus the same brass corners on every colour), so the eight colours are as far apart as before for every kind of colour vision (Sports Balls gives each colour its own ball, an extra clue); the grey, red and white blocks are the page's own; a board recolours the frame, bed and wells, with the wells as dark as the game's own (`MAX_WELL_L`) and still in two tones by quarter. What is picked is saved in this browser as `gridpop:look` (`{b, w}`, item numbers, 0 for the game's own); scores, `gridpop:save` and the best score are untouched. The words are in `es.js`.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/2048/`: 2048 (Back Room), the single-player number-sliding puzzle, drawn on one canvas (no libraries). The rules are pure functions at the top of the page script (`slideLine`, `moveBoard`, `spawnTile`, `isOver`); a board is an array of n × n numbers, row by row, with 0 for empty (every rule takes the size from the board's length), and `linesFor(n)` lists each direction's rows or columns starting from the wall being moved toward, which is where joins start (`LINES` is the 4 × 4 set). A move that changes nothing does nothing (no new tile, animation or score). A move made during an animation finishes that animation at once and then runs, so fast keys never lose a move or add an extra tile. **Modes** (picker under the buttons and in the menu; the last one played reopens): **Classic** is the 4 × 4 game; **Mega** is the same game on a 6 × 6 board (same spawn odds, win at 2048 with keep-going); **College** and **Dessert** are rule-identical to Classic (same engine, only the drawing differs) but each tile shows a step on a 12-step ladder from 2 to 4096 with its own colours, the number small in the corner, and a win at the 2048 step: colleges from ASU up to UChicago (2048) and Princeton (4096), a deliberately silly joke ranking with no basis beyond rough selectivity (the ladder is `COLLEGE` in the script), and desserts from a plain cracker up to a wedding cake (2048) and a croquembouche (4096), each with a small icon drawn with canvas shapes (`DESSERT` and `ICONS`); past the top of a ladder tiles show plain numbers. Strict mode (on by default; the setting was called Classic before the modes arrived, and its key is still `classic`) hides Undo; with it off there is a one-step Undo, and a game where Undo was used no longer reports to the leaderboard. Everything is saved only in this browser, one game and one best per mode: `g48:game` and `g48:best` for Classic (unchanged, so older saves carry over), `g48:game:<mode>` and `g48:best:<mode>` for `mega`, `college` and `dessert` (the game in progress is `{v:1, b: board, s: score, w: reached 2048, wp: the 2048 screen is still showing, u: the undo step {b, s, w} or null, a: Undo was used, m: moves}`, with 36 entries in `b` for Mega), `g48:opts` (`classic`, `mute`, `mode`) and `keybinds:2048` (keybinds.js). Keep these keys and the `v:1` format so saved games carry over; every read is checked (a save of the wrong size for its mode is ignored), and a broken save just starts a new game. Leaderboard hooks, sent only from games without Undo: `BackRoomStats.max('best', score)` and `BackRoomStats.max('tile', biggest tile)` for Classic, and `best-<mode>` / `tile-<mode>` for the others (`best-mega`, `tile-mega`, `best-college`, `tile-college`, `best-dessert`, `tile-dessert`; stat names can't hold underscores). The 2048 leaderboard has a chip for each mode's best score plus Classic's biggest tile (`tile-<mode>` is sent but has no board yet), and account-nudge.js saves `g48:best` and every `g48:best:<mode>` to the signed-in account. **Drawing:** the number tiles have the game's own colour ramp (`PAL`: paper, leaf, mint, teal, blue, indigo, violet, berry, getting darker at every step; 2048 is the one gold tile and glows; the tiles past it are deep colours with a gold rim) and the board is a walnut tray with a sunken well per square. Each tile value is painted once (`paintTile`: shadow, lower lip, a face lit from above, rim, number or ladder name and icon) into its own small picture (`spriteOf`) and then stamped with one `drawImage`, and the empty tray is one picture too (`boardOf`); `clearStyles()` throws these away when the size, mode or font changes, so call it rather than `styles.clear()`. The gold glow is one soft picture drawn under all the tiles (`drawGlow`), so it never covers a neighbour. A move slides the tiles, then a join pops with a white flash and a ring (`drawJoinFx`) and the new tile fades in as it grows, all inside the same 110 ms slide and 190 ms settle as before; the score number bumps when points arrive. With the system's reduce-motion setting none of this moves (the board just redraws). The win, game-over and new-game screens dim the board behind a cream card sized from the board's width (`--s`); the win screen adds a gold sunburst and a star badge, both plain CSS. A webdriver-only hook `window.__g48` exposes the rules, the mode switch and game controls for automated tests. **Spanish:** `es.js` has its words (lang.js), including the dessert tile names drawn on the board (the tile looks are rebuilt on a switch); college names stay as they are. **Styles** (captains only for now, like the other games' hidden cosmetics): 8 looks in `2048/styles.js`, listed in `cosmetics.js` under `GAMES['2048']`: 4 tile sets (Baseball Stitches, Circuit Rivets, Treasure Corners, Pinstripe) and 4 boards (Stadium Turf, Pirate Deck, Robot Steel, Velvet and Gold). The page fetches `styles.js` and `../cosmetics.js` only when `stylesOn()` is true (`G2048_PUBLIC`, the one line that opens it to everyone; a site captain through `captains.js`; or `?styles=1` on localhost or in an automated browser), and then a Styles row appears in the How to play panel; for everyone else `WR` stays null and `paintTile` and `boardOf` draw exactly as before. Looks only, and no number gets harder to read: tile sets only add art at a tile's edges and corners (Pinstripe's lines are at 7% strength) on the number tiles of Classic and Mega, keeping every tile's colour, number colour, font and size (the College and Dessert ladders are left alone); boards keep the walnut tray's brightness within 10% step for step, so tiles stand out from the wells as before. What is picked is saved in this browser as `g48:look` (`{t, b}`, item numbers, 0 for the game's own); scores, saves, Undo and the leaderboard are untouched. The words are in `es.js`.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/stackfall/`: Stackfall (Back Room), a single-player falling-block puzzle with two modes on one page (Canvas 2D, Web Audio, no Firebase). The rules are two pure engines with no DOM in them, `Classic(opts)` and `Sand(opts)`, driven by `update(ms, softDrop)` and the move, rotate, hardDrop and holdPiece calls; the view only reads their state, and they report sounds and effects through `onEvent`. **Classic**: a 10 × 20 well plus 2 hidden rows, 7-bag randomizer (`makeBag`), SRS rotation with the published kick tables (`KICKS_UP`, one table for J/L/S/T/Z and one for I; `KICKS` is the same data converted to y-down), DAS 170 ms and ARR 50 ms, 500 ms lock delay that up to 15 moves or turns can reset (`LOCK_MS`, `MAX_RESETS`), gravity from the standard `(0.8 − (level − 1) × 0.007)^(level − 1)` s per row curve, a level every 10 lines, hold once per piece and a five-piece preview. Scoring is 100/300/500/800 × level for 1–4 lines (4 is called a "QUAD!"), T-spins by the 3-corner rule (0–3 lines 400/800/1200/1600, minis 100/200/400, all × level; the 5th kick test upgrades a mini), back-to-back × 1.5 for quads and T-spin clears in a row, combo +50 × combo × level, and 1 per soft-dropped row, 2 per hard-dropped row. It tops out when a new piece overlaps the stack or a piece locks entirely in the hidden rows. **Sand**: the well is a grain grid of 10·B × 20·B in typed arrays (B = 10, so 100 × 200, on computers; B = 8, so 80 × 160, on touch screens and small windows; every rule is in block units, so both play the same and grain counts and scores are normalised to 64 grains per block). Each piece is rigid and steerable (moves in half-block steps) until it touches the floor or the sand, then it crumbles into grains of its one colour (per-grain shade). `step()` is the cellular automaton: bottom row up, each grain falls straight down if it can, else slides diagonally down to a random free side, else rests, so each grain moves at most once a step; it runs at 22.5·B steps a second, up to 10 a frame. `findClear()` flood-fills (4-neighbour, one colour, `Int32Array` queue) from every grain on the left wall, and a component that also reaches the right wall clears whole: it flashes for 200 ms, a dissolve sweeps across it for 260 ms, and then all its grains vanish together so the sand above drops and can make another link. Points are the grains removed × the chain count in that cascade × (1 + 0.25 × (level − 1)), plus 300 × (colours − 1) × level when a new colour joins the chain; the chain resets once the sand comes to rest with nothing left to clear. The level goes up every 3,000 grains cleared (`GRAINS_PER_LEVEL`) and the fall speed is `sandSpeed(level)` blocks a second. Game over: the danger line is 2 blocks below the top (`DANGER`); a new piece only appears when no sand is above it, and if sand is still above it 1 s after the piece is due (`SPAWN_WAIT_MS`), the game ends. Sand mode has 4 colours by default, or 5 or 6. Saved in this browser only: best scores in localStorage `stackfall:best:classic`, `stackfall:best:sand` (4 colours), `stackfall:best:sand5` and `stackfall:best:sand6`, plus `stackfall:sandColours`, `stackfall:mute` and the key bindings (`keybinds:stackfall`, via `keybinds.js`). At game over it reports `BackRoomStats.max('classic' | 'sand', score)` and `max('lines', lines)`; `stats.js` has no `READ` entry for it yet. In an automated browser, `window.__sf` exposes both engines, the shape and kick tables, `state()`, `layout()` and `start(mode)` for the rule tests. **Spanish:** `es.js` has its words (lang.js); the canvas HUD and callouts go through `T()`, and the help paragraphs with bold words are swapped whole (`data-i18n-html`). **Styles** (captains only for now, like the other games' hidden cosmetics): 15 looks in `stackfall/styles.js`, listed in `cosmetics.js` under `GAMES.stackfall`: 7 block sets (Sports Balls, Circuit Board, Treasure Chest, Pinstripe and Gold, 8-Bit, Gem Cut, Jelly Candy), 5 boards (Stadium Night, Pirate Deck, Robot Lab, Gold Pinstripe, Outer Space) and 3 clear effects (Confetti, Gold Coins, Star Sparkles). The page fetches `styles.js` and `../cosmetics.js` only when `stylesOn()` is true (`STACKFALL_PUBLIC`, the one line that opens it to everyone; a site captain through `captains.js`; or `?styles=1` on localhost or in an automated browser), and then a Styles button appears on the mode menu and the pause card; for everyone else `WR` stays null and `tileFn`, `buildWell`, `buildBackdrop` and `drawParticles` draw exactly as before. Looks only, and no piece gets harder to read: every block set keeps each piece's own `PIECE_RGB` colour, square and lip and only changes the art on the face (in white or black at low strength, plus small metal corners the same on every piece), so the seven colours are exactly as far apart as before for every kind of colour vision (Sports Balls even gives each piece its own ball); Sand's grains are never restyled; every board keeps the inside of the well as dark as the game's own (`MAX_INNER_L`); clear effects redraw the same particles in other shapes. What is picked is saved in this browser as `stackfall:look` (`{b, w, c}`, item numbers, 0 for the game's own); scores, saves and stats are untouched. The words are in `es.js`.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/melon-merge/`: Melon Merge (Back Room), a single-player fruit-merging puzzle with its own 2D circle physics (no libraries, no Firebase). Fruit are dropped from above a glass jar; two of the same stage that touch merge into the next of 11 stages (cherry → watermelon) at their combined centre with their averaged velocity, growing to full size over 0.12 s so neighbours get pushed aside; two watermelons that touch both burst for `MELON_BONUS` (200). Drops are uniformly random from stages 1–5 (`makeQueue`). Merging into stage k scores `SCORES[k]` (1, 3, 6, … 66), and every merge in a chain counts. Game over when any fruit that isn't still falling in (it counts once it first lands, or after `ENTER_GRACE` 1 s) stays above the red line (`DANGER_Y`) for `OVER_LIMIT` 1.5 s in a row. The physics (`makeSim`) has no DOM in it: world units with the jar 520 × 720, a fixed 1/120 s step with 2 sub-steps, warm-started sequential impulses with friction and rolling, bounce only on a contact's first touch (so piles can't pump themselves up), then 4 position-correction passes (heavier fruit move less) and wall clamps; change `RADII` and the jar size together, since the danger line and drop height are measured against them. Only the best score is saved, in this browser under `melon-merge:best` (also `melon-merge:mute` and `melon-merge:faces`); a game in progress isn't saved. Keys are rebindable through `keybinds.js` (`keybinds:melon-merge`). Calls `BackRoomStats.max('score', …)` at game over and `BackRoomStats.add('melons', 1)` for each watermelon; `leaderboard.js` needs entries for those if they should show on the leaderboards. **Turkey Week** (`../events.js`, November 15 to 26, 2026): the eleven stages are drawn as a Thanksgiving dinner (cranberry, pecan, sprout, dinner roll, yam, apple, corn, ham, pumpkin, pumpkin pie, turkey): `FR_DINNER` holds the names and colours and `paintDinner` the art, swapped in by `setDinner`; the help text and the first-turkey banner follow. Sizes, scores, physics and the saved best are unchanged. The game reads the one Turkey Week switch on the Back Room page (see `events.js` below) once, when it loads (off means fruit); it has no button of its own. **Spanish:** `es.js` has its words (lang.js), fruit and dinner names included; the canvas HUD labels go through `T()`, and the brand stays English. **Styles** (captains only for now, like the other games' hidden cosmetics): 12 looks in `melon-merge/styles.js`, listed in `cosmetics.js` under `GAMES['melon-merge']`: 5 fruit sets (Sports Balls, Robot Bolts, Treasure Gems, Dapper, Planets), 4 walls (Stadium, Pirate Cabin, Robot Workshop, Gold Pinstripe) and 3 merge pops (Confetti Pop, Star Shower, Bubble Rings). The page fetches `styles.js` and `../cosmetics.js` only when `stylesOn()` is true (`MELON_PUBLIC`, the one line that opens it to everyone; a site captain through `captains.js`; or `?styles=1` on localhost or in an automated browser), and then a Styles button appears on the start card and the pause card; for everyone else `WR` stays null and `paintFruit`, `buildBg` and `burst` draw exactly as before. Looks only: a fruit set paints each stage in that stage's own three colours (the fruit's, or the dinner's during Turkey Week) at its own size, with the page's light, shadow and faces, and adds art only in white or black at low strength, so the stages stay as far apart in colour as before; it does drop the stems, leaves and crowns that stick out of some fruit, so size and colour are the clues left (Sports Balls adds a different ball pattern per stage). Every wall is as dark as the plum one (`MAX_WALL_L`), because it shows through the glass; merge pops use the page's own particle kinds. What is picked is saved in this browser as `melon-merge:look` (`{f, w, p}`, item numbers, 0 for the game's own); physics, sizes, scores and the saved best are untouched. The words are in `es.js`.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/patchwork-tower/`: Patchwork Tower (Back Room), a one-screen fix-it arcade game in pixel art for one player (one file, one canvas, Web Audio, no libraries, no Firebase except stats.js). It took the place of Hivefix; `e63334287a3deaa3/hivefix/index.html` is now only a redirect to it, so old links and bookmarks still work (`m/thumbs/hivefix.jpg` stays for the old What's new entry). Rivet, a repair bot with a wrench, hops between the windows of a brick tower at night (5 across, 4 down) and fixes each broken one until it glows; cracked windows take one fix, dark ones two, and fixing roots the bot to the spot. A gargoyle walks along the roof, stops over a column (more often than not the bot's), winds up (eyes red, a red arrow under the roof ledge on every column about to be hit) and kicks a brick straight down it. Clearing every window ends the floor (500 plus 10 for each second left of 60) and the next one starts with a new layout. **Rules** live between `// ==RULES==` and `// ==END RULES==`: no page, clock, sound or `Math.random` in there, so the same seed and presses always give the same game (`create(seed)`, `step(g, 1/120)`, `press(g, k)` for a tap and `hold(g, k, on)` while a key or button is down, with k one of up, down, left, right, fix; what should be heard goes into `g.ev` as words the page plays). `g.win[r][c]` is 0 fixed / 1 cracked / 2 dark; `g.blockV[r][c]` is a planter under the sill of (r, c), which blocks climbing between rows r and r+1; `g.blockH[r][c]` is an open shutter between (r, c) and (r, c+1), which blocks stepping sideways. **Tuning** is the constants at the top of the rules: a hop takes 0.12 s (`HOP`), a fix 0.26 s (`FIX`; 0.08 s with cocoa), a press made during a hop or fix is remembered for 0.3 s (`BUF`), a held direction or FIX repeats after 0.22 s (`REPEAT`), the wind-up is 0.4 s (`TELE`) plus 0.3 s on floor 1 shrinking to nothing by floor 4 (`TELE_EARLY`), 3 lives, a hit freezes the world for 1 s and restarts the bot at the door with 2 s of blinking. Per floor: 7 + 2 × floor windows broken (all 20 from floor 7), the chance one is dark is 0.15 + 0.1 × floor up to 0.75, one more planter or shutter each floor up to four (each on its own edge, redrawn until every window can be reached), the gargoyle walks at 55 + 9 × floor pixels a second, bricks fall at 65 + 11 × floor, and its rest between kicks is 1.5 − 0.13 × floor seconds, never under 0.35; the speeds stop rising at floor 12 (`TOP_LEVEL`). From floor 2, bats cross a row (an arrow blinks at that edge of the row for 0.8 s first, `BAT_WARN`); from floor 3 a kick sometimes (35%) sends a second brick down a neighbouring column 0.18 s later, chosen at the start of the wind-up so its arrow shows too. Cocoa appears on a sill now and then for 7 s: 6 s safe from everything, the first 4 with one-tap repairs, +300. The hit boxes are narrower than the sprites (9 pixels either side for bricks and bats). **Drawing**: the game is a 240 × 320 picture. `layout()` shows it at a whole number of device pixels per game pixel (the canvas is sized in device pixels and scaled back by 1/devicePixelRatio, its corner snapped to a device pixel), so pixels stay square and sharp on any screen; never size the canvas with CSS percentages or `vh`. The sky, skyline and brickwork are drawn once (`back`), the windows, planters and shutters are redrawn only when one changes (`g.winDirty`), and words use a 5 × 5 pixel alphabet drawn in code (`FONT`, capitals, digits and `+ - : ! .` only), not a web font. The loop runs the rules in fixed steps of 1/120 s and draws between steps, so it plays at the same speed on 60, 120 and 144 Hz screens. The game pauses when the tab is hidden or the window loses focus. **Saved in the browser only**: `patchwork-best`, `patchwork-mute` (in try/catch), keys under `keybinds:patchwork-tower`. Stats hook: `max('score', score)` and `max('floor', floor reached)` when a floor is cleared and at game over (the Leaderboards show best score). Controls: arrows or WASD hop (hold to keep hopping), Space, Z or X fix (hold to keep fixing), P, Enter or Esc pause, M sound, all rebindable under KEYS. On touch screens (`body.touch`): a d-pad (slide a thumb between arrows) and a round FIX button under the screen, or either side of it when the phone is sideways. How to play sits beside the screen when there is room, otherwise behind the ? button. A test hook (`window.__pt`) exists only when `navigator.webdriver` is set. It has Spanish: its words are in `patchwork-tower/es.js` (lang.js), with a Language switch in How to play; the canvas words are chosen to need no accents, since the 5×5 pixel font has none. **The Wardrobe** (captains only for now, like the other games' hidden cosmetics): 29 looks for Rivet in `patchwork-tower/wardrobe.js`, listed in `cosmetics.js` under `GAMES['patchwork-tower']` (15 paint jobs, 7 hats, 3 face pieces and 4 hop trails: sports kits, tin and chrome robots, pirates, suits and more). The page fetches `wardrobe.js` and `../cosmetics.js` only when `wardrobeOn()` is true (`PATCHWORK_PUBLIC`, the one line that opens it to everyone; a site captain through `captains.js`; or `?wardrobe=1` on localhost or in an automated browser), and then a hanger button appears in the top bar; for everyone else `WR` stays null and the bot is drawn exactly as before. Looks only: a paint job recolours the bot's own rectangles (cocoa still turns it gold, the blinking after a hit is unchanged), every paint stands out from the bricks at least 3.5 times (`MIN_CR`; factory teal is 4.1) and none is brick-orange or bat-lilac, hats sit on the head (some in place of the antenna), face pieces go on the visor, and hop trails are drawn under the bricks, bats and bot and fade within half a second. Opening it pauses a game in progress. What is worn is saved in this browser as `patchwork-look` (`{p, h, f, t}`, item numbers, 0 for none); nothing is sent anywhere. The words are in `es.js`.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/flickerwood/`: Flickerwood (Back Room), an 8-bit maze chase for one player (one file, one canvas, Web Audio, no libraries, no Firebase except stats.js). Glim, a firefly, gathers every speck of pollen in a moonlit hedge maze (a glade) while four night critters come out of the hollow stump in the middle and chase its light. It is our own take on the maze-chase genre: the name, theme, characters, mazes, art, sounds, words and numbers are all ours, and only the genre's ideas are used. **The four critters** never find a path: at the middle of every tile each one picks the open way (never straight back) whose next tile is nearest, in a straight line, to the tile it wants (`target()`), and all of its character is in that tile. Swoop the bat wants Glim's tile; Gulp the frog wants the tile 3 ahead of the way Glim faces (`AHEAD`); Tangle the spider wants the point on the far side of Glim from Swoop (twice Glim's tile minus Swoop's); Dither the moth wants Glim while more than 5 tiles away (`NEAR`) and its own corner once closer. They take turns at two moods on a clock: **roost** (each heads for its own corner: Swoop top right, Gulp top left, Tangle bottom right, Dither bottom left) and **hunt**; every time the mood changes they all turn straight round, which is the tell. Glim is faster than they are in an empty corridor and a little slower while eating, turns can be pressed early and are remembered until the next gap, a turn started up to 4 pixels before the middle of a tile cuts the corner and gains those pixels (`CUT`; up to 3 pixels late it is still taken, `LATE`), and a reverse is taken anywhere. A **moonbloom** (four a glade) dazzles the critters: they turn round, go pale, slow to 60% and mostly run away, and touching one sends it home as a puff of dust for 100, 250, 500 then 1000 (`CATCH`); they flicker for the last 2 seconds, and a caught one rests 1.2 s in the stump and comes back out as itself. **Tunnels** at the edges (`~` tiles) wrap round and slow critters to 55%. A **find** (dewdrop, acorn, clover, toadstool, bluebell, feather, snail shell, moonstone, one kind a glade) shows under the stump for 9 s when 35% and again when 70% of the glade is eaten, worth 200 plus 100 a glade up to 1000. Our own twists: **Dash** (0.32 s at 2.1 times speed, then 6 s to recharge, shown as a meter) and the **chain** (each speck eaten within 1 s of the last carries it on; every 12 raises the multiplier on specks, up to 5 times). Specks are 5, moonblooms 30, clearing a glade 100 times its number up to 1000 plus 300 if no life was lost in it; 3 lives, one more at 6,000 and every 15,000 after, 5 at most. **Rules** live between `// ==RULES==` and `// ==END RULES==`: no page, clock, sound or `Math.random` in there, so the same seed and presses always give the same game (`create(seed)`, `startGame(g)`, `step(g, 1/120)`, `turn(g, d)` with d 0 right, 1 down, 2 left, 3 up, and `dash(g)`; what should be heard or shown goes into `g.ev` as words, and eaten tiles into `g.gone`). Positions in the rules are maze pixels; the page adds the score bar above. Glim and the critters are a tile, a direction and how far along it they are (`c, r, dir, off`), so they can never leave a path; they touch when both gaps are under 7 pixels (`HIT`, smaller than the sprites). **Glades** are the five `MAZES` at the top of the rules, drawn by hand as 23 rows of 21 letters (12-pixel tiles): `#` hedge, `.` pollen, `o` moonbloom, `-` path, `~` tunnel, `HHH` the stump with `G` its gate above the middle, `P` Glim's start, `B` the find, space outside the glade. Hedge Court is a full rectangle with a tunnel across the middle, Lily Pond has its corners cut off and tunnels top to bottom, Bramble Wheel is not mirrored (its bottom half is its top half turned half round) and has two tunnels, Mossy Ruin has tunnels both ways through the middle of each edge, and Moth Hollow is an hourglass with none; glade 6 is Hedge Court again, quicker. A new or changed glade must stay: every path one tile wide (no open 2 × 2), no dead ends, all joined up, a border tile that is a path matched on the opposite border, the start at least 8 steps from the gate with a way left and right (Glim sets off left), one moonbloom in each quarter, nothing but hedge next to the outside. A script checked all of that for the five glades when they were made (it is not in the repo), so check again by hand if you change one. **Tuning** is `tune(level)` and the constants at the top of the rules: Glim 66 pixels a second plus 1.3 a glade; critters 70% of that plus 3 points a glade (97% at glade 10); the dazzle 8 s less 0.67 s a glade, never under 2; roosts of 7, 7, 5 and 4 s (less 0.55 s a glade, never under 1.5) between hunts of 13, 15 and 18 s (plus 1.2 s a glade), then hunting for good; Gulp leaves the stump after 3 s, Tangle after 12 s or 30 specks, Dither after 28 s or 90 specks, all sooner glade by glade; from glade 3 Swoop speeds up 6% when 20% of the pollen is left and 12% under 8%, and keeps hunting through roosts; nothing tightens after glade 10 (`TOP_LEVEL`). Glade 1 is meant to be cleared by a first-time player: a test bot that only heads for the nearest pollen and runs when something is three tiles away clears it nearly every time. **Drawing**: the game is a 252 × 300 picture (a 14-pixel score bar, the 252 × 276 maze, a 10-pixel strip for spare lives, the dash meter and finds). `layout()` shows it at a whole number of device pixels per game pixel, so pixels stay square and sharp, and tries three places for it, biggest picture first: under the top bar with the key hint, under the bar without the hint, and between the bar's title and buttons (so a 1366 × 768 laptop with a short browser window still gets 2 ×); only when a whole number would be 1 is it drawn at 2 and shrunk smoothly to fit. Never size the canvas with CSS percentages or `vh`. Sprites are rows of letters turned into small canvases once (`bake`; Glim is drawn facing right and turned for the other three ways; two frames each; the dazzled look is the same shapes in two pale tones); the hedges, stump and tunnel marks are drawn once per glade (`mazeL`, and a lit copy `flashL` for the blink when a glade is cleared), the pollen sits on its own layer and each speck is wiped from it as it is eaten, the score bar is redrawn only when something on it changes, and words use a 5 × 5 pixel alphabet drawn in code (capitals, digits and `+ - : ! .` only). The loop runs the rules in fixed steps of 1/120 s and draws between steps. It pauses when the tab is hidden or the window loses focus. **Sound** is made in the page: square, triangle and noise blips in `SFX`, and a 16-bar tune of our own in A minor in `TUNE` (notes as numbers), booked a tenth of a second ahead on the audio clock; while a moonbloom is on, a quicker bright figure plays instead. Nobody has listened to it yet, so the loudness and the tune want a check by ear. **Saved in the browser only**: `flickerwood-best` (score), `flickerwood-glade` (furthest glade), `flickerwood-mute` (in try/catch), keys under `keybinds:flickerwood`. Stats hook: `max('score', score)` and `max('glade', glade reached)` when a glade is cleared and at game over (the Leaderboards show best score). Controls: arrows or WASD turn, Space or Shift dashes, P, Enter or Esc pause, M sound, all rebindable under KEYS. On touch screens (`body.touch`): swipe anywhere to turn (keep sliding to turn again without lifting), tap the screen to dash, or use the d-pad with a DASH button either side of it under the screen (pad on the left and DASH on the right when the phone is sideways). How to play, with a picture of each character, sits beside the screen when there is room, otherwise behind the ? button. A test hook (`window.__fw`) exists only when `navigator.webdriver` is set. It has Spanish: its words are in `flickerwood/es.js` (lang.js), with a Language switch in How to play; the canvas words are chosen to need no accents (the 5×5 pixel font has none), and glade and critter names stay English. **The Wardrobe** (captains only for now, like the other games' hidden cosmetics): 28 looks for Glim in `flickerwood/wardrobe.js`, listed in `cosmetics.js` under `GAMES.flickerwood` (14 kits, 7 hats, 2 face pieces and 5 trails: sports kits, a tin firefly, pirates, suits and more, all seen from above). The page fetches `wardrobe.js` and `../cosmetics.js` only when `wardrobeOn()` is true (`FLICKERWOOD_PUBLIC`, the one line that opens it to everyone; a site captain through `captains.js`; or `?wardrobe=1` on localhost or in an automated browser), and then a hanger button appears in the top bar; for everyone else `WR` stays null and Glim is drawn exactly as before. Looks only: a look swaps `SP.glim` for pictures of the same 16 × 16 size (so the spare Glims on the score bar wear it too), no kit recolours the glowing yellow tail or the halo (what tells Glim from the critters), and trails are drawn under the critters and Glim, fade in 0.4 s and are never small yellow dots that could pass for pollen. Opening it pauses a game in progress. What is worn is saved in this browser as `flickerwood-look` (`{k, h, f, t}`, item numbers, 0 for none); nothing is sent anywhere. The words are in `es.js`.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/lanternhold/`: Lanternhold (Back Room), a co-op tower defense game for 1 to 4 players (one file, three.js r128 with `../autores.js`, `../netsmooth.js` for teammates' pointers, Web Audio, Firebase Realtime Database rooms under `rooms/lh-<code>`, same project and rules as Rumble Karts; invite links go through the home page as `#lh-<code>`; no Quick match, rooms hold up to 4). The name, the Gloom, the towers, the maps, the art and the sounds are all our own. The Gloom walks a road across a floating island (a 20 × 12 grid of 2 m tiles, `GW`/`GH`/`T`) to the lantern; each one that gets there costs lives (`lv` in `EN`: 1, 2 for Shellbacks and Splitblobs, 15 for the boss). Towers go on grass tiles only (not road, scenery or the lantern). **Rules** are plain data and pure functions near the top of the script, the same in every browser, so a client can work out wave previews and enemy health without asking: `MAPS` (Meadow Bend, one long road; Twin Fords, two roads that meet; Whorl Garden, a spiral, whose flyers come from their own pink gate, `fly`, so they don't skip almost the whole map), `DIFFS` (Easy, Normal, Hard: health factor, lives 30/20/12, starting gold 720/620/540, bounty factor and seconds between waves), `EN` (Gloomling; Skitter, fast; Shellback, armour 7 taken off every hit, at least a quarter always goes through; Duskmoth, flies straight at the lantern; Splitblob, splits into three Bloblets; Mender, heals the Gloom near it 5% a second; Gloomwarden, the boss on every 10th wave, armoured, slow, and calls two Skitters every 5 s), `TW` (Bolt Spire, quick single shots, two at once at level 3; Thumper, lobbed blasts that hurt groups, ground only; Frost Bell, rings out to slow and chip everything near it, bosses half as much; Spark Coil, lightning that jumps between enemies; Sky Lance, flyers only, splash on nearby flyers; Sun Lens, a long beam that ignores armour), each with three levels (`lv`: range, damage, seconds between shots and the upgrade's cost) and a target mode (First, Last, Strong, Close). Selling gives back 70% of what went into the tower (`SELL`). `waveDef(w, map)` builds wave w from a seeded random pool (new kinds join at waves 2, 4, 5, 6 and 8, and the wave a kind first appears always has a group of it), `hpScale` makes them tougher each wave and faster still after wave 30. Survive 30 waves (`CAMPAIGN`) to win; then Keep going is endless mode. Gold: every kill's bounty and every wave's bonus (25 + 5 a wave) is split evenly between the players (`income`), each player spends their own share, and the team list has +50 to give gold to a teammate. Sending a wave before its countdown ends pays 2 gold per second left. Speed 1×, 2× or 3× (`sp`, anyone can change it). Only whoever built a tower can sell it (anyone can once they've left); anyone can upgrade or retarget it. `simStep(S, dt)` runs the game at a fixed 30 steps a second in solo, behind the menu (a demo game, `attract`) and on the online host; `act(S, uid, a)` applies one action (`b` build, `u` upgrade, `s` sell, `m` target mode, `w` send the wave, `f` speed, `g` give gold, `c` continue into endless) and returns '' or what went wrong. **Online:** each player writes only their own node `rooms/lh-<code>/players/<uid>`: `n` name, `c` colour, `x`/`z` where their pointer is on the board (drawn on the others' screens as a ring and a name tag from a short history, NetSmooth), `h` (always 0), `ts` (server time), `j` (when they joined), and `ac`, their last 10 actions as JSON, each with a rising number `i` and the game number `g`. The host is whoever has the highest `he` with a published state, else whoever joined first (`Net.elect`); it runs the game, takes each player's new actions (`takeActs`, at most once each, by `i`, and only for the current game), and publishes the whole state as JSON in its own node's `rs` about 5 times a second (`encodeRS`: phase, wave, lives, gold per player, towers, enemies, Thumper shells in the air and the last shots, so clients can draw them). Clients rebuild the game from it (`decodeRS`, which checks every value) and move the Gloom on between snapshots from its speed, easing towards the host's numbers. If the host leaves, the next player picks up the last snapshot and carries on (`he` goes up by one), and a tab in the background keeps the host's game running once a second. The host picks the map and difficulty in the room's lobby and presses Start; anyone joining later gets a share of gold (the average, at most 250). Nodes are removed on leave and onDisconnect. **Saved in this browser** (localStorage, all `lanternhold:`): `name`, `color`, `map`, `diff`, `gfx` (Pretty, with shadows, or Fast; Fast is the default on touch screens), `sound`, `room` (the last room code) and `best:<map id>:<difficulty>`, the best wave held. **stats.js:** `max('wave', waves held)` and `add('bosses', n)`; the Leaderboards show Most waves held and Gloomwardens beaten. **Touch:** shown only on touch screens (`body.touch`): tap a tower button, tap a tile to see the tower there, tap it again to build; drag to move the board, pinch to zoom. The camera fits the board into the space the HUD leaves (`fitCam`, `measureHud`), and on an upright phone the wave box becomes a row under the top bar and the dock gets compact. **Drawing:** the island, scenery and lantern are merged meshes with vertex colours; enemies, health bars, shots and rings are instanced meshes (their colour buffers are made at full size in `whiteColors`, because three r128's `setColorAt` sizes the buffer from the mesh's current count); particles are one Points draw. Automated browsers get a test hook, `window.__lh`. It has Spanish: its words, tower names and descriptions are in `lanternhold/es.js` (lang.js), with a Language switch on the menu and the pause screen; map names and the Gloom's names stay English, and nothing translated is sent to the room. **Wardrobe (hidden: captains only for now).** `LH_PUBLIC = false` and `wardOn()` (captains.js's `may(false, 'wardrobe', 'lh-wardrobe')`; `?wardrobe=1` for tests on localhost or in an automated browser) decide whether the menu shows a Wardrobe button under the colour swatches; for everyone else `lanternhold/wardrobe.js` is never fetched and the game is exactly as before, unless a teammate wears something, in which case it is fetched to draw them. It has 30 items in four slots, numbered as in `../cosmetics.js` (`lh-…`): 12 tower banners (a small flag on a pole at the back-left of every tower that player builds, seen by the whole team: sports pennants, a gear, a pirate flag with an eye-patched skull, a ship's wheel, pinstripes, a crown; baked into the tower's base mesh through `towerParts`' new `ban` argument, so a tower is still the same draw calls and its shape, owner band and level gems are untouched), 9 lanterns (the lantern's stand and cage on the captain's own screen only: `lanternGeo()` is the usual one, and `world.core.body`'s geometry is swapped; the flame, halo and light stay where they are and the same size), 5 flame colours (the flame's and halo's colour on the captain's own screen only; the point light is not recoloured, the low-lives flicker and the red flash are kept) and 4 build bursts (in place of the usual puff in `fxBuild` when that player builds or upgrades, seen by everyone, from the same particle pool). Saved in this browser as `lanternhold:wardrobe` (`'banner.lantern.flame.burst'`); online, a player's own node carries `cz: 'banner.burst'` (written with `Net.upd`) only while one of those is worn. Older pages ignore `cz`, `rs` and the actions are unchanged, and a page draws the usual look for numbers it doesn't know. The test hook `window.__lh` adds `WD` (with `wear(slot, n)`), `wardOn`, `wdLoad`, `wearOf`, `towerViews`, `towerMesh`, `wx`, `wz` and `world`. Its words and item names are in `lanternhold/es.js`.
- `e63334287a3deaa3/daily/`: Daily Puzzles (Back Room), the dashboard for every daily puzzle. No libraries, no network except stats.js. `DAILY_GAMES` at the top of the script lists every daily game (`slug` = folder = storage key `daily:<slug>`, name, icon, `cat`: word, geo, logic, est (Estimation) or soccer, a one-line blurb, `ready`), plus `EXTRA` link cards under Play together (Puffer Party, `chip` in place of a daily status). Tiles only show for `ready:true` games, and "Coming soon" ones are listed under each category; flip `ready` when a game's folder lands. For each game it reads the shared record `daily:<slug>` (written by the game when a daily ends: `{ v:1, days:{YYYYMMDD:{done,win,score,tries,time}}, played, wins, streak, maxStreak, last, dist }`) and shows today's status (Not played, Solved in N / in m:ss, Done, or Missed) and the game's 🔥 streak (only while its last win was today or yesterday). Deepword is read from its own `deepword:` keys. The summary counts today's finished puzzles, the overall streak (days in a row, ending today or yesterday, with at least one daily done, worked out from every game's `days`), and counts down to local midnight; when every ready game is done it shows an "All done today" banner. stats.js (`data-game="daily"`): `max('streak', overall)` and `add('done', 1)` once per game per day (remembered in `daily-hub:counted`). It re-reads storage when the tab comes back into view or another tab writes, so finishing a puzzle in another tab updates it. Test hook: `window.__dh` under webdriver (`setToday`, `render`, `streakOverall`). A game whose win shouldn't read "Solved in <tries>" gives a `show` template (`{tries}`, `{score}`, `{time}`; `{score|hint}` adds the word, plural unless the number is 1, and `zero` is the text used when the number is 0): Crosslet shows its time, Ninefold puzzles solved of 3, Crossbar squares, Stat Duel calls right, Top Bins entries found, Quartets mistakes (Perfect for none), Sleight hints, Poopdle moves, Mainstream its points, Yardstick its total out of 500 and Thereabouts its average miss (`{score|x100}`: a score saved as a hundred times a decimal, so 137 reads "1.37×"). A missed puzzle's tile has the finished look but no ✓. Poopdle and Thereabouts are under Logic. `RETIRED`, under `DAILY_GAMES`, holds the daily puzzles taken off the list on 18 Nov 2026 (the least played; see the Back Room page above): they have no tile and aren't in today's total, but their records still count toward the overall streak, so taking a puzzle off never shortens anyone's streak, and a category with nothing listed (Soccer, for now) isn't shown. To bring one back, move its line back into `DAILY_GAMES`. The daily games don't have Back Room or phone-page cards of their own: the Daily Puzzles card and tile are the way in (the phone thumbnails in `m/thumbs/` are kept for if they ever do).
- `e63334287a3deaa3/hunch/`: Hunch (Back Room, a daily puzzle on the Daily Puzzles page), a five-letter word guess in six tries, like Wordle. No libraries, no network: `words.js` holds `window.WORDS5 = { ANSWERS, GUESSES }` as space-separated strings, 2,019 answers (common, school-safe, no plurals) and 6,334 more allowed guesses (8,353 in all), written for the site and checked by a script (five letters a–z, no repeats, every answer also a guess, a blocklist for rude words). `ANSWERS` is already in daily order (a fixed seeded shuffle): day n after 1 November 2026 (puzzle #n+1, from the player's local date) uses `ANSWERS[n % length]`, so new answers must be **appended** to keep past days unchanged. Scoring marks greens first, then yellows only for as many copies as the answer has left. Hard mode (Settings, only before a game's first guess) makes every green stay put and every found letter be reused; High contrast swaps green/yellow for orange/blue. Unlimited mode picks random answers and never touches daily stats. Storage: the shared daily record `daily:hunch` (`{ v:1, days:{YYYYMMDD:{done,win,score,tries,time,hard?}}, played, wins, streak, maxStreak, last, dist }`, 60 days kept; the Daily Puzzles page reads it), the day in progress `hunch:day` (`{ d, g:[guesses], hard, t0 }`), Unlimited `hunch:free`, settings `hunch:settings`, `hunch:seen` (how-to shown) and `hunch:mode`. stats.js (`data-game="hunch"`): `add('wins')`, `max('streak')`, `min('fewest')`. The page is generated from the same engine as Fourcast (`CFG` at the top of the script: boards, rows, offset); keep the two in step when fixing the engine. Tile size is worked out in `fit()` (bigger of the layouts that fit); on a sideways phone the keyboard sits right of the board. Test hooks: `window.__wg` under webdriver (`setToday('YYYYMMDD')`, `type(word)`, `score`, …). Thanksgiving Day 2026 (`TG_DAY`) has its own answer, FEAST, set in `answersFor` for Hunch only; every other day and Fourcast are unchanged. **Spanish:** `es.js` has its words (lang.js), with a language switch in Settings; the words are English by design and tagged "En inglés".
- `e63334287a3deaa3/fourcast/`: Fourcast (Back Room, a daily puzzle), four five-letter words at once in nine guesses, like Quordle. The same engine as Hunch with `boards: 4, rows: 9, offset: 777`, and the same word lists (it loads `/e63334287a3deaa3/hunch/words.js`, so don't move that file). Each guess goes on every unsolved board; a solved board stops. Day n uses `ANSWERS[(777 + 4n + k) % length]` for boards k = 0–3, so appending answers keeps past days. Each keyboard key shows four corner colours, one per board in reading order (top left = board 1). Tiles may be up to 1.45× wider than tall, and `fit()` picks a 1 × 4 or 2 × 2 layout, whichever gives bigger tiles; on a sideways phone the keyboard stays underneath in short rows. Win = all four within nine; the share card shows the guess each board fell on (🟥 for missed). Storage: `daily:fourcast` (same contract; each day also has `boards`, how many were solved), `fourcast:day`, `fourcast:free`, `fourcast:settings`, `fourcast:seen`, `fourcast:mode`. stats.js: `add('wins')`, `max('streak')`, `min('fewest')`. **Spanish:** `es.js` has its words (lang.js), with a language switch in Settings; the words are English by design and tagged "En inglés".
- `e63334287a3deaa3/wordplay/`: shared files for the three daily word games (Quartets, Crosslet, Sleight); Mainstream uses them too. **`kit.js`** sets `window.Kit`: localStorage wrapped in try/catch (`Kit.store`), the local date and puzzle number (`Kit.today()`, `Kit.dayNum()`, 2026-11-01 is #1), `Kit.pick(name, length, day, inOrder)` (the daily puzzle: a seeded shuffle of the list, a new shuffle each time round and never the same puzzle twice across the seam; with `inOrder` the first time round follows the list as written), the `daily:<slug>` record the Daily hub reads (`Kit.Daily.load/finish`, which also calls `BackRoomStats.add('wins')` and `max('streak')`), and the page shell (top bar with Back to the Daily hub, How to play on the first visit, Stats with distribution and countdown, toast, share). **`kit.css`** is the shared look (Deepword's and the geography games' colours, fonts, pills, modals, on-screen keyboard). Each game saves the day's progress in `<slug>:day` and whether How to play was seen in `<slug>:seen`. A test hook reads `window.__WP_TODAY` (YYYYMMDD) only when `navigator.webdriver` is set. Adding, removing or reordering puzzles in a game's data file changes which puzzle future days get (a game already started today keeps its puzzle).
